![Predictive Correlations at the Instance-Level Across Language Models](https://arxiv.org/html/2309.08638v2/emnlp2023-latex/figures/matrices/QQP_corrmat.png)

*Predictive Correlations at the Instance-Level Across Language Models*

<sub>figure: `2309.08638v2/emnlp2023-latex/figures/matrices/QQP_corrmat.png`</sub>

## 机制
### 1. Micro-Benchmarking Approach

We measure how well an example ( x 1 , y 1 ) (x_{1},y_{1}) represents another example using the Pearson correlation of correct class confidence in the two examples measured across the predictions of the source models in 𝒮 = { ϕ n } n = 1 N \mathcal{S}=\{\phi_{n}\}_{n=1}^{N} . If our data is not annotated, we can instead compute 𝒴 \mathcal{Y} Pearson correlations (one across each class) per example pair and average them. However, model confidences are bounded by [0, 1] and thus can only follow a linear trend for a bounded range. Akin to Miller et al. (2021) , we take the logit transform of the confidences to scale the axes from [0,1] to [ − inf , + inf ] [-\inf,+\inf] prior to computing correlations. We denote this composite function as C ​ O ​ R ​ R 𝒮 CORR_{\mathcal{S}} . The task of selecting a X a ​ c ​ q X_{acq} that is maximally representative of D e ​ v ​ a ​ l D_{eval} for all models in 𝒮 \mathcal{S} reduces to solving a K-Medoids problem that maximizes the correlation between the selected and the remaining points: where B B is the number of anchor points and Q i Q_{i} is the set containing all x e ​ v ​ a ​ l x_{eval} that are more strongly correlated with x a ​ c ​ q i x_{acq}^{i} than any other x a ​ c ​ q j x_{acq}^{j} . This objective makes the assumption that the estimate for ϕ m ​ ( x e ​ v ​ a ​ l ) \phi_{m}(x_{eval}) leverages only the most strongly correlated ϕ m ​ ( x a ​ c ​ q i ) \phi_{m}(x_{acq}^{i}) . We efficiently solve this objective using the Partitioning Around Medoids (PAM) algorithm Kaufman and Rousseeuw (2009) .

<sub>§4 Micro-Benchmarking Approach</sub>

### 2. Anchor Point Techniques / Technique 1: Anchor Point Predictor

We present two techniques for leveraging anchor points to benchmark model performance with minimal evaluation examples. We propose the Anchor Points Predictor, an ensemble of univariate linear regression models that use model predictions on each anchor point to estimate predictions on all other points in X e ​ v ​ a ​ l X_{eval} . Specifically, anchor point ϕ ⁡ ( x a ​ c ​ q i ) \phi(x_{acq}^{i}) is used to estimate all ϕ ⁡ ( x e ​ v ​ a ​ l ) ∈ Q i \phi(x_{eval})\in Q_{i} . These instance-level prediction estimates can then be used to detect regions of model weaknesses (see Section 4.1 ) and compute an estimate of any performance metric for the target model. This technique requires that the trend lines fitted to source model predictions explains the variance in target model predictions well. We explore when this is the case in Appendix A . Algorithms 1 and 2 show pseudocode for fitting and making predictions with the Anchor Point Predictor Model.

<sub>§4.1 Anchor Point Techniques · §Technique 1: Anchor Point Predictor</sub>

### 3. Technique 2: Anchor Point Weighted Score

Rather than attempting to estimate exact model performance at the instance-level, we can aim to produce a score for each target model that highly correlates with the model’s performance on 𝒟 e ​ v ​ a ​ l \mathcal{D}_{eval} . Using the predictions of the source model set 𝒮 \mathcal{S} , we select N ∈ 1 ​ … ​ B N\in{1...B} anchor points according to Equation 1 . Each of these points x a ​ c ​ q , i x_{acq,i} strongly correlates with a subset of the untested points in 𝒟 e ​ v ​ a ​ l \mathcal{D}_{eval} , namely all x e ​ v ​ a ​ l j ∈ Q i x_{eval}^{j}\in Q_{i} . We propose the Anchor Point Weighted (APW) score, a weighted average of the model’s correct class probability predictions on the anchor points with weights proportional to cluster size.

<sub>§Technique 2: Anchor Point Weighted Score</sub>

### 4. Anchor Point Maps

To highlight the insights provided by anchor points, namely model strengths and weaknesses in distinct regions of a dataset, we propose to visualize the cross-model correlative space from which anchor points are drawn. We compute pairwise correlations between all points using C ​ O ​ R ​ R 𝒮 CORR_{\mathcal{S}} , creating a | 𝒟 e ​ v ​ a ​ l | × | 𝒟 e ​ v ​ a ​ l | |\mathcal{D}_{eval}|\times|\mathcal{D}_{eval}| correlation matrix C C . We then represent the distance matrix Z Z as a positive, monotonically decreasing function of correlation: Z = 1 − C Z=1-C . Finally, we cast the points to a high-dimensional continuous space using Multi-Dimensional Scaling on Z Z and project the space to two components with U-MAP McInnes et al. (2018) . This technique visualizes the dataset in a space where a model’s performance on each point generalizes to the local neighborhood of that point.

<sub>§Anchor Point Maps</sub>

### 5. Predictive Correlations

To motivate our approach, we first show that language models make predictions with consistent structure. For many pairs of points from a given dataset, the predicted probability of the correct class strongly correlates across models.

<sub>§5 Predictive Correlations</sub>

### 6. Experimental Set-Up

We obtain a diverse set of language models from HuggingFace and the OpenAI API: 27 BERT-family models, 11 non-instruction-tuned GPT-family models, 5 instruction-tuned GPT-family models, and 5 GPT-3/3.5 variants (all listed in Tables 9 - 10 ). We denote these model sets as the BERT-family, GPT-family, InstructGPT-family, and OpenAI-family respectively. For six datasets from the GLUE benchmark Wang et al. (2019) , we finetune each BERT-family model on the training split 𝒟 t ​ r ​ a ​ i ​ n \mathcal{D}_{train} and obtain its predicted probability of the correct class on each instance in the validation split 𝒟 e ​ v ​ a ​ l \mathcal{D}_{eval} . For the other model families we zero-shot prompt for the same datasets using three multiple-choice prompts per dataset (Appendix D ). We obtain the predicted probability of each class by performing softmax over the log probabilities of candidate answer sequences. Thus, we obtain a prediction matrix P P for each model family and dataset with size N s ​ u ​ b ​ j ​ e ​ c ​ t ​ s × | 𝒟 e ​ v ​ a ​ l | N_{subjects}\times|\mathcal{D}_{eval}| matrix where N s ​ u ​ b ​ j ​ e ​ c ​ t ​ s = 27 N_{subjects}=27 for P B ​ E ​ R ​ T P_{BERT} , 30 30 for P G ​ P ​ T P_{GPT} , 15 15 for P I ​ G ​ P ​ T P_{IGPT} , 15 15 for P O ​ A ​ I P_{OAI} , and 87 for their union P A ​ L ​ L P_{ALL} .

<sub>§Experimental Set-Up</sub>

### 7. Model predictions on pairs of examples are linearly correlated across models.

The prediction matrices are approximately low-rank for all tasks (Table 1 ). This can be attributed to a simple phenomenon: let ϕ n ​ ( x 1 ) ​ [ y ] \phi_{n}(x_{1})[y] denote the probability mass (equivalently, model confidence) outputted by ϕ n \phi_{n} on x 1 ∼ 𝒟 e ​ v ​ a ​ l x_{1}\sim\mathcal{D}_{eval} for a given class y y . We see that ϕ n ​ ( x 1 ) ​ [ y ] \phi_{n}(x_{1})[y] often linearly correlates with the model’s prediction on a different instance ϕ n ​ ( x 2 ) ​ [ y ] \phi_{n}(x_{2})[y] across models, i.e. ϕ n ​ ( x 2 ) ​ [ y ] ≈ w ​ ϕ n ​ ( x 1 ) ​ [ y ] + b \phi_{n}(x_{2})[y]\approx w\phi_{n}(x_{1})[y]+b , where w w and b b can be found by fitting a trend line through the correct class confidences of all source models. Figure 3 visualizes this phenomenon. We first observe that the correlation matrices of model predictions on the same evaluation set show rich structure. Figure 3(b) shows a QQP example pair that is strongly positively correlated across models from all families. This suggests that evaluating a model on both examples is redundant. This phenomenon is wide-spread: it holds for many pairs of examples across the six GLUE tasks and 87 widely-used language models and prompts.

<sub>§Model predictions on pairs of examples are linearly correlated across models.</sub>

### 8. Predictive Correlations Tend to Generalize Across Diverse Models / Sample-Efficient Model Evaluation

Figure 6 in Appendix A shows how well the strong trends from each family transfer to other families. Errors are lowest in the bottom row, suggesting that using source models that span all model families result in the most generalizable trend lines. We also observe that the predictive correlations of the BERT-family do not generalize well to other model families, which we explore further in Figure 8(b) . However, most errors in the table are generally low, suggesting that predictive correlations tend to generalize across diverse models. We now evaluate the anchor point techniques and compare against baselines for 1) selecting representative evaluation subsets to rank models 1 1 1 We also evaluate anchor point techniques for ranking MMLU performance in Appendix B and 2) estimating model instance-level performance on held-out points in 𝒟 e ​ v ​ a ​ l \mathcal{D}_{eval} .

<sub>§Predictive Correlations Tend to Generalize Across Diverse Models · §6 Sample-Efficient Model Evaluation</sub>

### 9. Subset Selection Baselines and Metrics

For baselines, we use uniform random sampling as well as K-Medoids sampling over the embedding spaces of a generic sentence encoder– SentenceBERT Reimers and Gurevych. (2019) – and an encoder fine-tuned on the dataset– the CLS token of bert-base-uncased Devlin et al. (2018) . For these techniques, we use model performance on the selected points as the estimate of aggregate model performance on the entire dataset. We further consider variants of these baselines that consider model confidence. We propose mean model confidence in the correct class on randomly selected points as one baseline. We also propose to produce a score estimate for each model by computing a weighted average of the correct class probabilities assigned to each selected point. The weights are proportional to the size of each selected point’s cluster and sum to 1, akin to Anchor Points Weighted. To assess the ranking performance of each technique, we compute the Kendall rank correlation coefficient (Kendall’s τ \tau ) for a range of anchor point set sizes and report the resulting Area Under the Correlation Curve (AUCC) from 1 to a maximum budget B B . We then normalize AUCC by dividing by the best possible area. In the experiments we use B = 30 B=30 , the small data regime for which it is reasonable for practitioners to manually inspect predictions.

<sub>§Subset Selection Baselines and Metrics</sub>

### 10. Prediction Estimation Baselines and Metrics

We consider two naive baselines: 1) using the instance-level predictions of a randomly selected source model on 𝒟 e ​ v ​ a ​ l \mathcal{D}_{eval} as the estimate of each target model’s prediction and 2) using the mean prediction of all source models on each point in 𝒟 e ​ v ​ a ​ l \mathcal{D}_{eval} as the estimate. We also propose Nearest Source Neighbor, a strategy where each target model’s predictions on k k randomly selected points are compared to the predictions of all source models on the same points. We select the source model with the most similar predictions on these k k points, as measured with L ​ 1 L1 -distance. The predictions of this source model on all other points in 𝒟 e ​ v ​ a ​ l \mathcal{D}_{eval} are then used as the estimate of the target model’s predictions. To measure the performance of each technique, we use agreement – the percentage of points for which the estimation technique assigns the highest probability to the same class as the target model.

<sub>§Prediction Estimation Baselines and Metrics</sub>

### 11. Anchor Point Weighted Correlations Are Reliable, Unlike Strong Contenders

We observe that Anchor Point Weighted (APW) performance tends to be more reliable across evaluation set sizes and test settings than APP as well as the baselines. We suspect APW is more reliable than APP because it makes a weaker assumption that source model predictions simply follow similar correlations as target model predictions rather than closely matching the exact regression line fitted to the source model predictions. This is corroborated by results in Appendix B with the MMLU dataset, where APW still demonstrates superior performance over baselines despite a small number of source models, but APP performance worsens. We observe inconsistent and sometimes erratic behavior in the embedding-based baselines. Despite Fine-tuned and Fine-tuned Weighted baselines collectively achieving the best performance in 10 settings, these baselines prove to be inconsistent: Fine-tuned and Fine-tuned Weighted perform worse than random in 12 and 10 settings, respectively. We suspect this is because language model embedding spaces are not always smooth and are likely to be inconsistent across models.

<sub>§Anchor Point Weighted Correlations Are Reliable, Unlike Strong Contenders</sub>

### 12. Anchor Points Efficiently Estimate Model Predictions

The Anchor Point Predictor can estimate model performance at the instance-level over the entire dataset. Table 3 shows the average agreement between Anchor Point estimated instance-level model predictions and true model predictions using various numbers of anchor points and randomly selected source models. The estimates of a small number of anchor points achieve high average agreement with true model predictions. However, the source model correlations do not generalize well to all target models, resulting in lower agreement on these outlier models and thus large standard deviations in Table 3 . As a general rule of thumb, selecting a source model set that is diverse (e.g. comes from multiple families, see Figure 6 ) results in the best generalization. Strategies for intelligently selecting source models are a promising direction to resolve poor anchor point generalization, which we discuss further in Appendix A .

<sub>§Anchor Points Efficiently Estimate Model Predictions</sub>

### 13. Nearest Source Neighbor is a Strong Baseline

Nearest Source Neighbor achieves competitive performance at estimating model instance-level predictions. The agreement achieved by this technique is upper bounded by the agreement of each target model and its true nearest source model neighbor (indicated by the B B = | 𝒟 e ​ v ​ a ​ l | \mathcal{D}_{eval}| column in Table 3 ). Surprisingly, comparing just B = 10 B=10 random points closely approaches this bound, suggesting that the similarity of the predictions of different models can be approximated cheaply. Note that using B = 100 B=100 anchor points surpasses the upper bound of this baseline on 3 of the 6 datasets.

<sub>§Nearest Source Neighbor is a Strong Baseline</sub>

## 总结与展望
这篇给的是一条和我们完全不同的省钱路线：不去删任务，而是找出少数「锚点」任务，用模型在锚点上的表现回归出它在全量上的表现（Anchor Points Predictor 是一组一元线性回归的集成），或者干脆只给一个与全量高度相关的加权分。它对本方向的意义在于把「少花钱还能排名」变成一个可测的量——锚点与全集之间的相关系数——而我们现在的便宜档从不报告它与贵档的相关性，只报告省了多少钱。

**It connects to**

- `2308.11696` — 同一年的两篇都在问「benchmark 里哪一部分是冗余的」，这篇答「用锚点回归」，那篇答「用 bootstrap 量每个设计选择的可信度」；两者合起来才是「选哪里省」而不是「省多少」。
- `2402.14992` — tinybenchmarks 引用了它，走的是同一条「用相关性换样本量」的路；区别是 tinybenchmarks 用 IRT 拟合题目难度，这篇用锚点做回归。

## 不足之处
**What a reader should still not conclude**

- 相关性是在「多个源模型之间」算的，跨模型可比不等于跨运行稳定——它对我们真正的问题（同一模型跑两次差 2–6 个点）一句话没有。
- 机制段抽到的是相关性的定义与两个技术的名字，回归的具体形式与特征在正文后面，200 字摘录读不到。

# Efficient Benchmarking (of Language Models)

arXiv:2308.11696 · 2024 · [source](https://arxiv.org/html/2308.11696)

> Written by an agent that read the body (DeepSeek Harness, 2026-11-24) after reading the paper; the mechanism quotes are the paper’s own words.

## 出发点
Given the ongoing advances in the versatility and performance of Language Models (LMs), they are now expected to perform a diverse range of tasks. This expectation raises a profound challenge – how do we evaluate and rank the quality of different LMs over a variety of capabilities?

<sub>§1 Introduction</sub>

## Pipeline
```mermaid
flowchart LR
  N0["§2 The Objective, Validity, Reliability"]
  N1["§The Objective."]
  N2["§Validity."]
  N3["§Reliability."]
  N4["§2.1 Quantifying Reliability: DIoR"]
  N0 --> N1
  N1 --> N2
  N2 --> N3
  N3 --> N4
```

![HELM model ranks for different numbers of inference calls. Each colored curve represents a different model. Model ranks are extremely stable even when compute drops dramatically: a × 10 \times 10 decrease in the number of examples per scenario produces nearly the same results as the full benchmark, while a × 400 \times 400 reduction still clusters models in the same small groups seen in the full compute regime.](https://arxiv.org/html/2308.11696v5/locs.svg)

*HELM model ranks for different numbers of inference calls. Each colored curve represents a different model. Model ranks are extremely stable even when compute drops dramatically: a × 10 \times 10 decrease in the number of examples per scenario produces nearly the same results as the full benchmark, while a × 400 \times 400 reduction still clusters models in the same small groups seen in the full compute regime.*

<sub>figure: `2308.11696v5/locs.svg`</sub>

## 机制
### 1. The Objective, Validity, Reliability

In this section, we first define 3 critical aspects for evaluation: the objective , validity , and reliability . Then, we discuss benchmark reliability and how to measure it, in more detail, being the focus of this study.

<sub>§2 The Objective, Validity, Reliability</sub>

### 2. The Objective.

The question the benchmark aims to answer. For example, “How good is a given model at sentiment analysis?” or “Which is the best language understanding model?”. The objective guides the initial, high-level decisions such as the choice of metric, tasks, domains, and datasets.

<sub>§The Objective.</sub>

### 3. Validity.

Ensuring that the benchmark actually satisfies the objective, i.e., that it answers the right question, is not trivial. Following common psychometrics literature ( Cronbach, 1946 ) , we refer to this quality as validity . Validity challenges are often discussed in the literature, in general, ( v. Kistowski et al., 2015 ) and in validating metrics ( Choshen and Abend, 2018a ; Freitag et al., 2022 ) or data ( Poliak et al., 2018 ; Gururangan et al., 2018 ; Northcutt et al., 2021b ; Northcutt et al., 2021a ) . For example, if the objective is to measure broad language understanding capabilities but the benchmark measures only a narrow aspect of language understanding, the benchmark has a validity problem.

<sub>§Validity.</sub>

### 4. Reliability.

Due to the noisy nature of broad evaluation, two valid protocols may yield different results ( Maynez et al., 2023 ) . Reliability assesses the degree to which the evaluation answer remains consistent under different random decisions, many of which are selections from the distribution of elements composing the benchmark ( Kuder and Richardson, 1937 ) . Building a benchmark involves numerous decisions (e.g., the number of datasets, or of examples per dataset). Importantly, such design decisions determine the reliability of the benchmark, and the conclusions that can (or cannot) be drawn from it. Therefore, we argue that such decisions must be made in an informed manner, including considering their impact on reliability. From a practical point-of-view, well-informed decisions can lead to improved benchmarks, yielding more reliable results with lower computational costs.

<sub>§Reliability.</sub>

### 5. Quantifying Reliability: DIoR

Just like significance, which relies on p-value, reliability requires a metric. However, such a metric is not available. Thus, we propose a new metric – the Decision Impact on Reliability test (hereafter DIoR ) – as a way to assess the effect of a benchmark design decision (e.g., choosing 16 16 specific language understanding datasets) on the reliability of the benchmark. Given a collection of instantiations of the decision (e.g., dataset samples of size 16 16 ), a benchmark scoring function (e.g., rank) and a similarity meta-metric to measure the consistency of the scoring function under a pair of different instantiations (e.g., correlation between rankings), DIoR assesses the stability of the meta-metric across different instantiations. Specifically, we define DIoR as the lower bound of the confidence interval for the value of the meta-metric; we report the lower bound as this corresponds to the minimal value we are certain of. Formally, given a set of models M M , random instantiations of the decision c ∼ D c\sim D , and the original instantiation c o c_{o} , a benchmark scoring function s c : M → r s_{c}\colon M\to r and a similarity meta-metric f : r , r → [ 0 , 1 ] f\colon r,r\to[0,1] , DIoR is defined as: A reliable decision should receive a high DIoR , implying that different instantiations do not substantially affect the results.

<sub>§2.1 Quantifying Reliability: DIoR</sub>

## 总结与展望
它把「benchmark 该怎么设计」变成一组可以用 bootstrap 量出来的可靠性：给定 HELM 的 16 个场景、40 个子场景与大量样例，逐个删掉一类再看排名是否还稳。结论的方向很反直觉——子场景与场景的可靠性低，样例的可靠性高，所以省算力应该删样例而不是删子场景。这恰好是我们 screening 做法的理论版本：我们删的是候选（相当于样例），但从来没有量过删掉之后排名还在不在。

**It connects to**

- `2309.08638` — 它给出的「哪些设计选择不可靠」清单，是锚点法选任务时的先验：锚点应当从可靠性高的那类里挑，而不是从最便宜的那类里挑。
- `2009.03300` — 它拿 HELM 当对象，而 HELM 的 16 场景来自 MMLU 这类多学科基准；57 个学科的粒度正是「删一部分还剩多少信息」这个问题能被问出来的前提。

## 不足之处
**The authors say:** Future work will analyze other benchmarking decisions and other benchmarks. Thus, while the paper’s results are sound, they might ignore common unreliable decisions in other benchmarks which were not apparent in this scenario or were left out (such as the choice of prompt templates, choices of non-textual benchmarks, etc.). A decision of special interest is that of efficient inference methods. With many efforts to tackle the tradeoff between performance and computation ( Chen et al., 2023 ; Choukroun et al., 2019 ) , future work would wonder if there is a validity and reliability tradeoff as well or if such methods can be used to evaluate models (that do not use them) as well.

**What a reader should still not conclude**

- 「可靠性」定义为 bootstrap 下排名的稳定性，不是我们关心的「判决错了没有」；一个排名稳定的 benchmark 仍然可以稳定地判错。
- HELM 规模下的结论（40 个子场景）不能直接搬到三个任务的 evalset 上，那里 bootstrap 的样本数根本不够。

<sub>§Limitations</sub>

---

# Time-uniform central limit theory and asymptotic confidence sequences

arXiv:2103.06476 · 2024 · [source](https://arxiv.org/html/2103.06476)

> Written by an agent that read the body (DeepSeek Harness, 2026-11-24) after reading the paper; the mechanism quotes are the paper’s own words.

## 出发点
The central limit theorem (CLT) is arguably the most widely used result in applied statistical inference, due to its ability to provide large-sample confidence intervals (CI) and p p -values in a broad range of problems under weak assumptions. Examples include (a) nonparametric estimation of means, such as population proportions, (b) maximum likelihood and other M-estimation problems [ 60 ] , and (c) modern semiparametric causal inference methodology involving (augmented) inverse propensity score weighting [ 46 , 58 , 24 , 7 ] . Crucially, in some of these problems such as doubly robust estimation in observational studies, nonasymptotic inference is typically not possible, and hence the CLT yields asymptotic CIs for an otherwise unsolvable inference problem.

### 1. Asymptotic confidence sequences

We first define what it means for a sequence of intervals to form an asymptotic confidence sequence (AsympCS). Then, we derive a ‘‘universal’’ AsympCS in the sense that the AsympCS does not depend on any features of the distribution beyond its mean and variance. 2 2 2 We use “universal” in the same way that the CLT and law of large numbers are considered universal [ 54 ] , as they describe macroscopic behaviors that are independent of most microscopic details of the system. Much like classical asymptotic confidence intervals based on the CLT, this universal AsympCS fundamentally relies on Gaussian approximation. However, in this setting, the particular type of central limit theory being invoked is that of strong invariance principles, where an implicit Gaussian process is coupled with a partial sum with probability one (more details are provided in Section 2.2.2 ). Finally, similar to CIs based on martingale CLTs, we derive a Lindeberg-type martingale AsympCS that can track a moving average of conditional means. <sub>§2 Asymptotic confidence sequences</sub>

### 2. Defining asymptotic confidence sequences

Here, we define and present “asymptotic confidence sequences” as time-uniform analogues of CLT-based asymptotic CIs, making similarly weak moment assumptions and providing a universal closed-form boundary. The term “asymptotic confidence sequence” may at first seem paradoxical. Indeed, ever since their introduction by Robbins and collaborators [ 10 , 28 , 29 ] , CSs have been defined nonasymptotically, satisfying the time-uniform guarantee in equation ( 3 ). So how could a bound be both time-uniform and asymptotically valid? We clarify this critical point soon, with an analogy to classical asymptotic CIs. Similar to asymptotic CIs, AsympCSs trade nonasymptotic guarantees for (a) simplicity and universality, and (b) the ability to tackle a much wider variety of problems, especially those for which there is no known nonasymptotic CS. Said differently, AsympCSs trade finite sample validity for versatility (exemplified in Section 3 with a particular emphasis on modern causal inference). Indeed, there is a clear desire for (asymptotically) time-uniform methods with CLT-like simplicity and versatility, especially in the context of causal inference. For example, Johari et al. [21, Section 4.3] use a Gaussian mixture sequential probability ratio test (SPRT) to conduct A/B tests (i.e. randomized experiments) for data coming from (non-Gaussian) exponential families and mentions that CLT approximations hold at large sample sizes. Similarly, Yu et al. [68] develop a mixture SPRT for causal effects in generalized linear models, where they say that their likelihood ratio forms an “approximate martingale”, meaning its conditional expectation is constant up to a factor of exp ⁡ { o ℙ ​ ( 1 ) } \exp\left\{o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)\right\} . Moreover, Pace and Salvan [36] suggest using Robbins’ Gaussian mixture CS as a closed-form “approximate CS” and they demonstrate through simulations that the time-uniform coverage guarantee tends to hold in the asymptotic regime. However, all of these approaches justify time-uniform inference with o ℙ ​ ( ⋅ ) o_{\mathbb{P}}(\cdot) approximations that only hold at a fixed, pre-specified sample size , and yet inferences are being carried out at data-dependent sample sizes . This section remedies the tension between fixed- n n approximations and time-uniform inference by defining AsympCSs such that Gaussian approximations must hold almost surely for all sample sizes simultaneously . The AsympCSs we define will also be valid in a wide range of nonparametric scenarios (beyond exponential families, parametric models, and so on). To motivate the definition of an AsympCS that follows, let us briefly review the CLT in the batch (non-sequential) setting. Suppose Y 1 , … , Y n ∼ ℙ Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n}\sim\mathbb{P} with mean 𝔼 ⁡ ( Y 1 ) = μ \mathbb{E}(Y_{1})=\mu and variance var ⁡ ( Y 1 ) = σ 2 \Var(Y_{1})=\sigma^{2} . Then the standard CLT-based CI for μ \mu (with known variance σ \sigma ) takes the form where μ ^ n \widehat{\mu}_{n} is the sample mean and Φ − 1 ​ ( 1 − α / 2 ) \Phi^{-1}(1-\alpha/2) is the ( 1 − α / 2 ) (1-\alpha/2) -quantile of a standard Gaussian N ⁡ ( 0 , 1 ) N(0,1) (e.g. for α = 0.05 \alpha=0.05 , we have Φ − 1 ​ ( 0.975 ) ≈ 1.96 \Phi^{-1}(0.975)\approx 1.96 ). The classical notion of “asymptotic validity” is While the above is the standard definition of an asymptotic CI, one could have arrived at an alternative definition by noting the following rather strong statement that can be made under the same conditions: there exist 3 3 3 Technically, writing ( 7 ) may require enriching the probability space so that both Y Y and Z Z can be defined (but without changing their laws). See Einmahl [15, Equation (1.2)] for a precise statement. i.i.d. standard Gaussians Z 1 , … , Z n ∼ N ⁡ ( 0 , 1 ) Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n}\sim N(0,1) such that From this vantage point, we note that the CI in ( 5 ) has the additional guarantee that there in fact exists an (unknown) nonasymptotic ( 1 − α ) (1-\alpha) -CI [ μ ^ n ± 𝔅 ˙ n ⋆ ] [\widehat{\mu}_{n}\pm\dot{\mathfrak{B}}_{n}^{\star}] such that We deliberately highlight the above property of asymptotic CIs because it ends up serving as a natural starting point for defining asymptotic confidence sequences . In particular, we will define AsympCSs so that an analogous approximation to ( 8 ) holds uniformly over time, almost surely. Statements like ( 7 ) are known as “couplings” and appear in the literature on strong approximations and invariance principles where similar guarantees can indeed be shown to hold almost surely and at faster rates under additional moment assumptions [ 15 , 26 , 27 ] . In words, Definition 2.1 says that an AsympCS ( C t ) t ∈ 𝒯 (C_{t})_{t\in\mathcal{T}} centered at ( θ ^ t ) t ∈ 𝒯 (\widehat{\theta}_{t})_{t\in\mathcal{T}} is an arbitrarily precise approximation of some nonasymptotic CS ( C t ⋆ ) t ∈ 𝒯 (C_{t}^{\star})_{t\in\mathcal{T}} centered at ( θ ^ t ) t ∈ 𝒯 (\widehat{\theta}_{t})_{t\in\mathcal{T}} as t → ∞ t\to\infty . Throughout the paper, we will mostly focus on the case where 𝒯 = ℕ \mathcal{T}=\mathbb{N} with t 0 = 0 t_{0}=0 . It is important to note that alternate definitions fail to be coherent in different ways. As one example, we could have hypothetically defined a sequence of intervals ( C t ​ ( α ) ) t ∈ 𝒯 (C_{t}(\alpha))_{t\in\mathcal{T}} to be a ( 1 − α ) (1-\alpha) -AsympCS if lim sup m → ∞ ℙ ( ∃ t ≥ m : μ ∉ C t ( α ) ) ≤ α \limsup_{m\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(\exists t\geq m:\mu\notin C_{t}(\alpha))\leq\alpha , analogously to asymptotic CIs which satisfy lim sup n → ∞ ℙ ⁡ ( μ ∉ C ˙ n ​ ( α ) ) ≤ α \limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(\mu\notin\dot{C}_{n}(\alpha))\leq\alpha . In words, we could have posited that if we just start peeking late enough, then the probability of eventual miscoverage would indeed be below α \alpha . Unfortunately, even for nonasymptotic CSs constructed at any level α ′ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \alpha^{\prime}\in(0,1) , the former limit is zero , so this inequality would be vacuously true, regardless of α ′ \alpha^{\prime} , even if α ′ ≫ α \alpha^{\prime}\gg\alpha . However, we do show that sequences of our AsympCSs have a guarantee of this type if peeking starts late enough (see Section 2.5 ), but we delay these definitions until later as they are slightly more involved. By virtue of being defined in terms of their limiting behavior, one can obviously construct AsympCSs (as well as asymptotic confidence intervals) with nonsensical finite-sample behavior. It is thus imperative that if a practitioner decides to employ an asymptotic method, they do so with the understanding that its effectiveness relies on it exploiting some well-approximated nonasymptotic phenomenon, and that its limiting behavior should be viewed as a rigorous manifestation of a guiding principle rather than a panacea. Going forward, we may omit “a.s.” from o a . s . ​ ( ⋅ ) {o_{\mathrm{a.s.}}(\cdot)} and O a . s . ​ ( ⋅ ) {O_{\mathrm{a.s.}}(\cdot)} and instead simply write o ⁡ ( ⋅ ) o(\cdot) and O ⁡ ( ⋅ ) O(\cdot) , respectively to simplify notation. Now that we have defined AsympCSs as time-uniform analogues of asymptotic CIs, we will explicitly derive AsympCSs for the mean of i.i.d. random variables with finite variances (i.e. under the same assumptions as the CLT).

<sub>§2.1 Defining asymptotic confidence sequences</sub>

### 3. Warmup: AsympCSs for the mean of i.i.d. random variables / Robbins’ Gaussian mixture boundary / Strassen’s strong approximation

We now construct an explicit AsympCS for the mean of i.i.d. random variables by combining a variant of Robbins’ (nonasymptotic) Gaussian mixture boundary [ 43 ] with Strassen’s strong approximation theorem [ 52 ] . Before presenting the result, let us review Robbins’ boundary and Strassen’s result, and discuss how they can be used in conjunction to arrive at the AsympCS in Theorem 2.2 . The study of CSs began with Herbert Robbins and colleagues [ 10 , 43 , 44 , 28 , 29 ] , leading to several fundamental results and techniques including the famous Gaussian mixture boundary for partial sums of i.i.d. Gaussian random variables [ 43 ] (see also Howard et al. [19, §3.2] ) which we recall here. Suppose ( Z t ) t = 1 ∞ (Z_{t})_{t=1}^{\infty} are i.i.d. standard Gaussian random variables. Then for any ρ > 0 \rho>0 , Notice that the above boundary scales as O ⁡ ( log ⁡ t / t ) O(\sqrt{\log t/t}) for any ρ > 0 \rho>0 . In fact, Robbins [43, Eq. 11] noted that ( 11 ) holds not only for Gaussian random variables, but for those that are 1 1 -sub-Gaussian, and hence pre-multiplying the boundary by σ \sigma yields a σ \sigma -sub-Gaussian time-uniform concentration inequality, serving as a time-uniform analogue of Chernoff or Hoeffding inequalities. The connections between these fixed-time and time-uniform concentration inequalities are made explicit in Howard et al. [19] . Nevertheless, Eq. 11 requires a priori knowledge of σ > 0 \sigma>0 unlike CLT-based CIs which we aim to emulate in the (asymptotically) time-uniform regime. The following strong Gaussian approximation due to Strassen [52] will serve as a technical tool allowing the nonasymptotic sub-Gaussian bound in ( 11 ) to be applied to partial sums of arbitrary random variables with finite variances. Strassen [52] initiated the study of “strong approximation” (also called strong invariance principles or strong embeddings) which blossomed into an active and impactful corner of probability theory research over the subsequent years, culminating in now-classical results such as the Komlós-Major-Tusnády embeddings [ 26 , 27 , 31 ] and other related works [ 53 , 14 , 34 , 6 ] . In his 1964 paper, Strassen [52, §2] used the Skorokhod embedding [ 51 ] (see also [ 4 , p. 513] ) to obtain a strong invariance principle which connects asymptotic Gaussian behavior with the law of the iterated logarithm. Concretely, let ( Y t ) t = 1 ∞ (Y_{t})_{t=1}^{\infty} be an infinite sequence of i.i.d. random variables from a distribution ℙ \mathbb{P} with mean μ \mu and variance σ 2 \sigma^{2} . Then, on a potentially richer probability space, 4 4 4 A richer probability space may be needed to describe Gaussian random variables, if for example, ( Y t ) t = 1 ∞ (Y_{t})_{t=1}^{\infty} are { 0 , 1 } \{0,1\} -valued on a probability space whose probability measure is dominated by the counting measure. This construction of a richer probability space imposes no additional assumptions on ( Y t ) t = 1 ∞ (Y_{t})_{t=1}^{\infty} , and is only a technical device used to rigorously couple two sequences of random variables, and appears in essentially all papers on strong invariance principles, not just Strassen [52] . there exist standard Gaussian random variables ( Z t ) t = 1 ∞ (Z_{t})_{t=1}^{\infty} whose partial sums are almost-surely coupled with those of ( Y t ) t = 1 ∞ (Y_{t})_{t=1}^{\infty} up to iterated logarithm rates, i.e. Notice that the law of the iterated logarithm states that | ∑ i = 1 t ( Y i − μ ) / σ | = O ⁡ ( t ​ log ⁡ log ​ t ) |\sum_{i=1}^{t}(Y_{i}-\mu)/\sigma|=O(\sqrt{t\log\log t}) while ( 12 ) states that the same partial sum is almost-surely coupled with an implicit Gaussian process — i.e. replacing O ⁡ ( ⋅ ) O(\cdot) with o ⁡ ( ⋅ ) o(\cdot) . It may be convenient to divide by t t and interpret ( 12 ) on the level of sample averages rather than partial sums, in which case the right-hand side becomes o ⁡ ( log ⁡ log ⁡ t / t ) o(\sqrt{\log\log t/t}) . Let us now describe how Strassen’s strong approximation can be combined with Robbins’ Gaussian mixture boundary to derive an AsympCS under finite moment assumptions akin to the CLT.

<sub>§2.2 Warmup: AsympCSs for the mean of i.i.d. random variables · §2.2.1 Robbins’ Gaussian mixture boundary · §2.2.2 Strassen’s strong approximation</sub>

### 4. The Gaussian mixture asymptotic confidence sequence

Given the juxtaposition of ( 11 ) and ( 12 ), the high-level approach to the derivation of AsympCSs becomes clearer. Indeed, the essential idea behind Theorem 2.2 is as follows. By Strassen’s strong approximation, we couple the partial sums S t := ∑ i = 1 t ( Y i − μ ) / σ S_{t}:=\sum_{i=1}^{t}(Y_{i}-\mu)/\sigma with implicit partial sums G t := ∑ i = 1 t Z i G_{t}:=\sum_{i=1}^{t}Z_{i} of Gaussians ( Z t ) t = 1 ∞ (Z_{t})_{t=1}^{\infty} , and then use Robbins’ mixture boundary to obtain a time-uniform high-probability bound on the deviations of | G t | |G_{t}| , noting that the coupling rate o ⁡ ( t ​ log ⁡ log ​ t ) o(\sqrt{t\log\log t}) is asymptotically dominated by the concentration rate O ⁡ ( t ​ log ⁡ t ) O(\sqrt{t\log t}) , leading to asymptotic validity in the formal sense of Definition 2.1 . The proof of Theorem 2.2 is in Appendix A.1 . We can think of ρ > 0 \rho>0 as a user-chosen tuning parameter which dictates the time at which ( 13 ) is tightest, and we discuss how to easily tune this value in Section B.2 . A one-sided analogue of ( 13 ) can be found in Section B.1 . While ( 13 ) may look visually similar to Robbins’ (sub)-Gaussian mixture CS [ 43 ] — written explicitly in Howard et al. [19, Eq. (14)] — it is worth pausing to reflect on how they are markedly different. Firstly, Robbins’ CS is a nonasymptotic bound that is only valid for σ \sigma -sub-Gaussian random variables, meaning 𝔼 ​ exp ⁡ { λ ⁡ ( Y 1 − 𝔼 ​ Y 1 ) } ≤ exp ⁡ { σ 2 ​ λ 2 / 2 } \mathbb{E}\exp\{\lambda(Y_{1}-\mathbb{E}Y_{1})\}\leq\exp\{\sigma^{2}\lambda^{2}/2\} for some a priori known σ > 0 \sigma>0 , while Theorem 2.2 does not require the existence of a finite MGF at all (much less a known upper bound on it). Secondly, Robbins’ CS uses this known (possibly conservative) σ \sigma in place of σ ^ t \widehat{\sigma}_{t} in ( 13 ), and thus it cannot adapt to an unknown variance, while ( 13 ) always scales with var ⁡ ( Y 1 ) \sqrt{\Var(Y_{1})} . In simpler terms, Theorem 2.2 is an asymptotically time-uniform analogue of the CLT in the same way that Robbins’ CS is a time-uniform analogue of a sub-Gaussian concentration inequality (e.g. Hoeffding’s or Chernoff’s inequality [ 16 , 18 ] ). It is important not to confuse Theorem 2.2 with a martingale CLT as the latter still gives fixed-time CIs in the spirit of the usual CLT but under different assumptions on the martingale difference sequence (however, we do present an analogue of Theorem 2.2 under martingale dependence in Proposition 2.5 ).

<sub>§2.2.3 The Gaussian mixture asymptotic confidence sequence</sub>

### 5. An asymptotic confidence sequence with iterated logarithm rates / A general recipe for deriving asymptotic confidence sequences

As a consequence of the law of the iterated logarithm, a confidence sequence for μ \mu centered at μ ^ t \widehat{\mu}_{t} cannot have an asymptotic width smaller than O ⁡ ( log ⁡ log ⁡ t / t ) O(\sqrt{\log\log t/t}) . This is easy to see since This raises the question as to whether C ¯ t 𝒢 \widebar{C}_{t}^{\mathcal{G}} can be improved so that the optimal asymptotic width of O ⁡ ( log ⁡ log ⁡ t / t ) O(\sqrt{\log\log t/t}) is achieved. Indeed, we can replace Robbins’ Gaussian mixture boundary with Howard et al. [19, Eq. (2)] (or virtually any other Gaussian boundary for that matter) in the proof of Theorem 2.2 to derive such an AsympCS, but as the authors discuss, mixture boundaries such as the one in Theorem 2.2 may be preferable in practice, because any bound that is tighter “later on” (asymptotically) must be looser “early on” (at practical sample sizes) due to the fact that all such bounds have a cumulative miscoverage probability ≤ α \leq\alpha . This is formally a concern for nonasymptotic CSs, but only applies to AsympCSs insofar as they are asymptotic approximations of nonasymptotic bounds. Nevertheless, we present an AsympCS with an iterated logarithm rate here for completeness. We omit the proof of Proposition 2.3 as it proceeds in a similar fashion to that of Theorem 2.2 . In fact, both of these AsympCSs are simply instantiations of a more general recipe for deriving AsympCSs by combining strong approximations with time-uniform boundaries for the approximating process, an approach that we discuss in the following section. The proofs of both Theorem 2.2 and Proposition 2.3 follow the same general structure, combining strong approximations with time-uniform boundaries along with some other almost-sure asymptotic behavior. Abstracting away the details specific to these particular results, we provide the following four general conditions under which many AsympCSs can be derived, including those from the previous section but also Lyapunov- and Lindeberg-type AsympCS that we will state in Section 2.4 ). In what follows, let 𝒯 \mathcal{T} be a totally ordered infinite set that includes a minimum value t 0 ∈ 𝒯 t_{0}\in\mathcal{T} (for example, one may think about 𝒯 \mathcal{T} as ℝ ≥ 0 \mathbb{R}^{\geq 0} or ℕ \mathbb{N} with t 0 = 0 t_{0}=0 ) and let ( θ ^ t ) t ∈ 𝒯 (\widehat{\theta}_{t})_{t\in\mathcal{T}} be a sequence of estimators for the real-valued parameters ( θ t ) t ∈ 𝒯 (\theta_{t})_{t\in\mathcal{T}} . Then, consider the following four conditions where we use the “Condition G - X ” enumeration as a mnemonic for the X th {}^{\text{th}} condition in the section on G eneral recipes for AsympCSs). Deriving new AsympCSs then reduces to the conceptually simpler but nevertheless nontrivial task of satisfying the requisite conditions above. For example, in the previous section, we satisfied Condition G-1 via Strassen [52] , Condition G-2 via Robbins [43] , Condition G-3 via the combination of Strassen [52] and Robbins [43] , and Condition G-4 via the strong law of large numbers (SLLN). The only difference between Theorem 2.2 and Proposition 2.3 was in what boundaries were being used for [ L t , U t ] t ∈ 𝒯 [L_{t},U_{t}]_{t\in\mathcal{T}} and [ L ^ t , U ^ t ] t ∈ 𝒯 [\widehat{L}_{t},\widehat{U}_{t}]_{t\in\mathcal{T}} . More generally under Condition s G-1 – G-4 , we have the following abstract Theorem for AsympCSs. We provide a short proof of Theorem 2.4 in Section A.2 . Note that the lower boundaries given by L t ⋆ L_{t}^{\star} and L ^ t \widehat{L}_{t} are not the same, but rather L t ⋆ L_{t}^{\star} is constructed from L ^ t \widehat{L}_{t} (and similarly for U t ⋆ U_{t}^{\star} and U ^ t \widehat{U}_{t} . In the following section, we will use the general recipe of Theorem 2.4 to obtain AsympCSs for time-varying means from non- i.i.d. random variables under martingale dependence akin to the Lindeberg CLT [ 30 , 4 ] .

<sub>§2.2.4 An asymptotic confidence sequence with iterated logarithm rates · §2.3 A general recipe for deriving asymptotic confidence sequences</sub>

### 6. Lindeberg- and Lyapunov-type AsympCSs for time-varying means

The results in Theorem 2.2 and Proposition 2.3 focused on the situation where the observed random variables are independent and identically distributed, as this is one of the most commonly studied regimes in statistical inference. One may also be interested in the case where means and variances do not remain constant over time, or where observations are dependent. We will now show that an analogue of Theorem 2.2 holds for random variables with time-varying means and variances under martingale dependence . In this case, rather than the AsympCS covering some fixed μ \mu , it covers the average conditional mean thus far: μ ~ t := 1 t ​ ∑ i = 1 t μ i \widetilde{\mu}_{t}:=\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=1}^{t}\mu_{i} --- to be made precise shortly. 5 5 5 Throughout this section and the remainder of the paper, we use the overhead tilde (e.g. μ ~ t \widetilde{\mu}_{t} , σ ~ t \widetilde{\sigma}_{t} , and C ~ t \widetilde{C}_{t} ) to emphasize that these quantities can change over time. For example, Fig. 2 explicitly displays means and CSs with sinusoidal behaviors resembling a tilde. Given the additional complexity introduced by considering time-varying conditional distributions, we will first explicitly spell out the conditions required to achieve a time-varying analogue of Theorem 2.2 . Suppose ( Y t ) t = 1 ∞ (Y_{t})_{t=1}^{\infty} is a sequence of random variables with conditional means and variances given by μ t := 𝔼 ⁡ ( Y t ∣ Y 1 t − 1 ) \mu_{t}:=\mathbb{E}(Y_{t}\mid Y_{1}^{t-1}) and σ t 2 := var ⁡ ( Y t ∣ Y 1 t − 1 ) \sigma_{t}^{2}:=\Var(Y_{t}\mid Y_{1}^{t-1}) , respectively where we use the shorthand Y 1 t − 1 Y_{1}^{t-1} for { Y 1 , … , Y t − 1 } \{Y_{1},\dots,Y_{t-1}\} . First, we require that the average conditional variance σ ~ t 2 := 1 t ​ ∑ i = 1 t σ i 2 \widetilde{\sigma}_{t}^{2}:=\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=1}^{t}\sigma_{i}^{2} either does not vanish, or does so superlinearly; equivalently, we require that the cumulative conditional variance diverges almost surely. Eq. 23 can also be interpreted as saying that the average conditional variance σ ~ t 2 := 1 t ​ ∑ i = 1 t σ i 2 \widetilde{\sigma}_{t}^{2}:=\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=1}^{t}\sigma_{i}^{2} does not vanish faster than 1 / t 1/t (if at all), meaning σ ~ t 2 = ω a . s . ​ ( 1 / t ) \widetilde{\sigma}_{t}^{2}=\omega_{\mathrm{a.s.}}(1/t) . For example, Condition L-1 would hold if σ ~ t 2 → a . s . σ ⋆ 2 \widetilde{\sigma}_{t}^{2}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{a.s.}}\sigma_{\star}^{2} for some σ ⋆ 2 > 0 \sigma_{\star}^{2}>0 or in the i.i.d. case where σ 1 2 = σ 2 2 = ⋯ = σ ⋆ 2 \sigma^{2}_{1}=\sigma^{2}_{2}=\cdots=\sigma_{\star}^{2} . Second, we require a Lindeberg-type uniform integrability condition on the tail behavior of ( Y t ) t = 1 ∞ (Y_{t})_{t=1}^{\infty} . Notice that Eq. 24 is satisfied if all conditional q th q^{\mathrm{th}} moments are almost surely uniformly bounded for some q > 2 q>2 , meaning 1 / K ≤ 𝔼 ⁡ ( | Y t − μ t | q ∣ Y 1 t − 1 ) < K 1/K\leq\mathbb{E}(|Y_{t}-\mu_{t}|^{q}\mid Y_{1}^{t-1})<K a.s. for all t ≥ 1 t\geq 1 and for some constant K > 0 K>0 , or more generally under a Lyapunov-type condition that states ∑ t = 1 ∞ [ 𝔼 ⁡ ( | Y t − μ t | 2 + δ ∣ Y 1 t − 1 ) / V t 2 + δ ] < ∞ \sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\left[\mathbb{E}(|Y_{t}-\mu_{t}|^{2+\delta}\mid Y_{1}^{t-1})/\sqrt{V_{t}}^{2+\delta}\right]<\infty a.s. for some δ > 0 \delta>0 . 6 6 6 We show that the Lyapunov-type condition implies Condition L-2 in Section B.5 . Third and finally, we require a consistent variance estimator. Note that in the i.i.d. case, ( 25 ) would hold using the sample variance by the SLLN. More generally for independent but non-identically distributed data, Condition L-3 holds as long as the variation in means vanishes — i.e. 1 t ​ ∑ i = 1 t ( μ i − μ ~ t ) 2 = o ⁡ ( 1 ) \frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=1}^{t}(\mu_{i}-\widetilde{\mu}_{t})^{2}=o(1) — but we will expand on this later in Corollary 2.6 . Given Condition s L-1 , L-2 , and L-3 , we have the following AsympCS for the time-varying conditional mean μ ~ t := 1 t ​ ∑ i = 1 t μ i \widetilde{\mu}_{t}:=\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=1}^{t}\mu_{i} . At a high level, the proof of Proposition 2.5 (found in Section A.3 ) follows from the general AsympCS procedure of Theorem 2.4 by using Condition L-2 and Strassen’s 1967 strong approximation [ 53 ] (not to be confused with his 1964 result that we used in Theorem 2.2 ) to satisfy Conditions G-1 and G-3 , and a variant of Robbins’ mixture martingale for non- i.i.d. random variables along with Conditions L-1 and L-3 to satisfy Conditions G-2 and G-4 . Notice that if the data happen to be i.i.d. , then ( C ~ t ) t = 1 ∞ (\widetilde{C}_{t})_{t=1}^{\infty} is asymptotically equivalent to ( C ¯ t 𝒢 ) t = 1 ∞ (\widebar{C}_{t}^{\mathcal{G}})_{t=1}^{\infty} given in Theorem 2.2 (here, “asymptotic equivalence” simply means that the ratio of the two boundaries converges a.s. to 1). In other words, Proposition 2.5 is valid in a more general (non- i.i.d. ) setting, but will essentially recover Theorem 2.2 in the i.i.d. case. Fig. 2 illustrates what C ~ t \widetilde{C}_{t} may look like in practice. Note that when ( Y t ) t = 1 ∞ (Y_{t})_{t=1}^{\infty} are independent with μ 1 = μ 2 = ⋯ = μ ⋆ \mu_{1}=\mu_{2}=\cdots=\mu_{\star} , and σ 1 2 = σ 2 2 = ⋯ = σ ⋆ 2 \sigma_{1}^{2}=\sigma_{2}^{2}=\cdots=\sigma_{\star}^{2} , it is nevertheless the case that C ~ t \widetilde{C}_{t} forms a ( 1 − α ) (1-\alpha) -AsympCS for μ ⋆ \mu_{\star} under the same assumptions as Theorem 2.2 . In this sense, we can view ( C ~ t ) t = 1 ∞ (\widetilde{C}_{t})_{t=1}^{\infty} as ‘‘robust’’ to deviations from independence and stationarity. 7 7 7 Here, the term “robust” should not be interpreted in the same spirit as “doubly robust”, where the latter is specific to the discussions surrounding functional estimation and causal inference in Section 3 . A one-sided analogue of Proposition 2.5 is presented in Proposition B.2 within Section B.1 . As a near-immediate corollary of Proposition 2.5 , we have the following Lyapunov-type AsympCS under independent but non-identically distributed random variables. Clearly, the conditions of ( 27 ) are trivially satisfied if the means and the ( 2 + 2 ​ β ) th (2+2\beta)^{\text{th}} absolute central moments are uniformly bounded over time. Since Condition s L-1 and L-2 hold by the assumptions of Corollary 2.6 , the proof in Section A.4 simply shows how the conditions in ( 27 ) imply Condition L-3 . As suggested by Section 2.3 , we can combine Theorem 2.4 with essentially any other Gaussian boundary, and indeed there are others that can yield Lindeberg- and Lyapunov-type AsympCSs but we do not enumerate any more here, though we do mention one inspired by Robbins [43, Eq. (20)] in passing in Section 2.6 . The next section discusses how all of the aforementioned AsympCSs satisfy a certain formal asymptotic coverage guarantee.

<sub>§2.4 Lindeberg- and Lyapunov-type AsympCSs for time-varying means</sub>

### 7. Asymptotic coverage and type-I error control

While the AsympCSs derived thus far serve as sequential analogues of CLT-based CIs, it is not immediately obvious whether the bounds introduced in the previous section enjoy similar asymptotic coverage (equivalently, type-I error) guarantees. We will now give a positive answer to this question by showing that after appropriate tuning, our AsympCSs have asymptotic ( 1 − α ) (1-\alpha) -coverage uniformly for all t ≥ m t\geq m as m → ∞ m\to\infty (to be formalized in Definition 2.7 ). Recall that the coverage of CLT-based CIs is at least ( 1 − α ) (1-\alpha) in the limit: but what is the right time-uniform analogue of ( 28 )? Since any single AsympCS will simply have some coverage, we provide the following definition as a time-uniform analogue of ( 28 ) for sequences of AsympCSs that start later and later. To the best of our knowledge, the existing literature lacks a concrete definition of asymptotic time-uniform coverage (or type-I error control) like Definition 2.7 , but sequences of AsympCSs satisfying ( 29 ) have been implicit in Robbins [43] and Robbins and Siegmund [44] , and the followup work of Bibaut et al. [2] . In what follows, we provide (sharp) coverage guarantees for our AsympCSs. Furthermore, in Section 2.6 we strictly improve on aforementioned bounds by Robbins [43] and Robbins and Siegmund [44] . Furthermore, we note that a bound Bibaut et al. [2] is in a certain sense equivalent to one that we provide here. In order to obtain asymptotic time-uniform coverage, we need a stronger variant of Condition L-3 so that variances are estimated at polynomial rates (rather than at arbitrary rates). Note that while Condition - η is stronger than Condition L-3 , it is still quite mild. For instance, if σ ~ t 2 \widetilde{\sigma}_{t}^{2} is uniformly bounded, then ( 30 ) simply requires that σ ^ t 2 − σ ~ t 2 = o ⁡ ( t η − 1 ) \widehat{\sigma}_{t}^{2}-\widetilde{\sigma}_{t}^{2}=o(t^{\eta-1}) (i.e. strong consistency at any polynomial rate, potentially much slower than t − 1 / 2 t^{-1/2} ). Moreover, in the i.i.d. case with at least ( 2 + δ ) (2+\delta) finite absolute moments, Condition - η always holds by the SLLNs of Marcinkiewicz and Zygmund [33] . Our goal now is to show that sequences of AsympCSs given in Proposition 2.5 have asymptotic time-uniform coverage, and we will achieve this by effectively tuning them for later and later start times. Recall that Section B.2 allows us to choose the parameter ρ > 0 \rho>0 so that the AsympCS is tightest at some particular time — we will now choose ρ m \rho_{m} based on the first peeking time m m as Then, let ( C ~ t ​ ( m ) ) t = m ∞ (\widetilde{C}_{t}(m))_{t=m}^{\infty} be the Gaussian mixture AsympCS with ρ m \rho_{m} plugged into the expression of the boundary for all t ≥ m t\geq m : In other words, ( C ~ t ​ ( m ) ) t = m ∞ (\widetilde{C}_{t}(m))_{t=m}^{\infty} should be thought of as an AsympCS that only starts after time m m , and is vacuous beforehand. The following theorem formalizes the coverage guarantees satisfied by this sequence of AsympCSs as m → ∞ m\to\infty . The proof can be found in Section A.7 . Notice that in the i.i.d. setting, as m → ∞ m\to\infty , ( C ~ t ​ ( m ) ) t = m ∞ (\widetilde{C}_{t}(m))_{t=m}^{\infty} is asymptotically equivalent to ( C ¯ t 𝒢 ​ ( m ) ) t = m ∞ (\widebar{C}_{t}^{\mathcal{G}}(m))_{t=m}^{\infty} given by where ρ ¯ m := ρ ⁡ ( m ​ log ⁡ ( m ∨ e ) ) \widebar{\rho}_{m}:=\rho(m\log(m\lor e)) . A quick inspection of the proof will reveal that ( 34 ) also satisfies the coverage guarantee provided in Theorem 2.8 under the condition that σ ~ t 2 → σ ⋆ 2 > 0 \widetilde{\sigma}_{t}^{2}\to\sigma_{\star}^{2}>0 almost surely. In summary, ( 34 ) can be thought of as an analogue of ( 32 ) for the AsympCSs that were derived in Theorem 2.2 .

<sub>§2.5 Asymptotic coverage and type-I error control</sub>

### 8. Asymptotic confidence sequences using Robbins’ delayed start

As is clear from Theorem 2.4 , virtually any boundary for Gaussian observations can be used to derive an AsympCS as long as an appropriate strong invariance principle can be applied under the given assumptions — indeed, Theorem 2.2 , Proposition 2.3 , Proposition 2.5 , and Corollary 2.6 are all instantiations of the general phenomenon outlined in Theorem 2.4 . Another AsympCS that may be of interest to practitioners is one that leverages Robbins’ CS for means of Gaussian random variables with a delayed start time [ 43 , Eq. (20)] . In a nutshell, Robbins calculated a lower bound on the probability that a centered Gaussian random walk would remain within a particular two-sided boundary for all times t ≥ m t\geq m given some starting time m ≥ 1 m\geq 1 . That is, he showed that for i.i.d. Gaussians ( Z t ) t = 1 ∞ (Z_{t})_{t=1}^{\infty} with mean zero and variance σ 2 \sigma^{2} , letting G t := ∑ i = 1 t Z i / σ G_{t}:=\sum_{i=1}^{t}Z_{i}/\sigma , and for any a > 0 a>0 , where Ψ ⁡ ( a ) := 2 ​ ( 1 − Φ ⁡ ( a ) + a ​ ϕ ​ ( a ) ) \Psi(a):=2(1-\Phi(a)+a\phi(a)) and Φ \Phi and ϕ \phi are the CDF and PDF of a standard Gaussian, respectively. In particular, setting a ∈ ( 0 , ∞ ) a\in(0,\infty) so that Ψ ⁡ ( a ) = α \Psi(a)=\alpha yields a two-sided ( 1 − α ) (1-\alpha) -boundary for the Gaussian random walk ( G t ) t = 1 ∞ (G_{t})_{t=1}^{\infty} , and indeed, a solution to Ψ ⁡ ( a ) = α \Psi(a)=\alpha always exists and is trivial to compute due to the fact that Ψ \Psi is strictly decreasing, starting at Ψ ⁡ ( 0 ) = 1 \Psi(0)=1 and lim a → ∞ Ψ ⁡ ( a ) = 0 \lim_{a\to\infty}\Psi(a)=0 . In a followup paper, Robbins and Siegmund [44] extended the ideas of Robbins [43] to a large class of boundaries for Wiener processes so that the probabilistic inequality in ( 35 ) can be shown to be an equality when | G t | |G_{t}| is replaced by the absolute value of a Wiener process (which would imply the inequality in ( 35 ) for i.i.d. standard Gaussians as a corollary). Using this fact within the general framework of Theorem 2.4 combined with the strong invariance principle of Strassen [53] and the techniques found in the proof of Theorem 2.8 yields the following result. The proof is provided in Section A.9 . Similar to the relationship between Theorem 2.8 and ( 34 ), we have that if variances happen to converge σ ~ t 2 → σ ⋆ 2 > 0 \widetilde{\sigma}_{t}^{2}\to\sigma_{\star}^{2}>0 almost surely, then as a corollary of Proposition 2.9 , the sequence ( C ~ t DS ⋆ ( m ) ) t = 1 ∞ (\widetilde{C}_{t}^{\mathrm{DS}\star}(m))_{t=1}^{\infty} given by has asymptotic time-uniform coverage as in Definition 2.7 . This can be seen as a generalization and improvement of results implied by Robbins [43] and Robbins and Siegmund [44] , and has connections to Bibaut et al. [2] . We elaborate on these below.

<sub>§2.6 Asymptotic confidence sequences using Robbins’ delayed start</sub>

### 9. Relationship to Robbins and Siegmund / Relationship to Bibaut et al. [2]

Informally, Robbins and Siegmund [44, Theorem 2(i)] show that for independent and identically distributed random variables ( Y t ) t = 1 ∞ ∼ ℙ (Y_{t})_{t=1}^{\infty}\sim\mathbb{P} with mean zero and unit variance (without loss of generality), the probability of their scaled partial sums S t := ∑ i = 1 t Y i S_{t}:=\sum_{i=1}^{t}Y_{i} exceeding a particular boundary behaves like a rescaled Wiener process exceeding that boundary. Consequently, for i.i.d. data with known variance, their result combined with Robbins’ delayed start [ 43 , Eq. (20)] implies an asymptotic coverage guarantee (in the sense of Definition 2.7 ) for C ~ t DS ⋆ \widetilde{C}_{t}^{\mathrm{DS}\star} given in ( 37 ) but with σ ^ t \widehat{\sigma}_{t} replaced by the true σ \sigma . As such, ( 37 ) should be thought of as a generalization of their boundary when variances are unknown under martingale dependence. Nevertheless, Proposition 2.9 is strictly more general, allowing for variances to never converge. In version 1 of Bibaut et al. [2] , the authors derive a particular AsympCS and show that sequences thereof satisfy an asymptotic coverage guarantee in the same sense as Definition 2.7 . That bound resembles — but is always looser than — a corollary of Proposition 2.9 in ( 37 ) for a fixed m ≥ 1 m\geq 1 ; see Claim B.1 . Nevertheless, it was their asymptotic type-I error results that inspired us to show that the same guarantees hold for the bounds in ( 32 ), ( 36 ), and ( 37 ), and without the assumption that σ ~ t 2 \widetilde{\sigma}_{t}^{2} converges almost surely for the former two. After our advances, the second version of their paper also weakened their almost-sure convergent variance assumption and introduced a bound called the “running maximum likelihood SPRT” (rmlSPRT) which is identical to the corollary of Proposition 2.9 found in ( 37 ) (modulo subtle differences in variance estimation techniques); see Claim B.2 . Since the exact connections may not be obvious to the reader, we derive them explicitly in Section B.7 . Despite their rmlSPRT being identical to ( 37 ), we remark that their paper focuses on testing guarantees and contains several additional interesting investigations including a sophisticated analysis of the expected rejection time, enriching the landscape of asymptotic anytime-valid methodology.

<sub>§2.6.1 Relationship to Robbins and Siegmund · §2.6.2 Relationship to Bibaut et al. [2]</sub>

### 10. Illustration: Causal effects and semiparametric estimation

Given the groundwork laid in Section 2 , we now demonstrate the use of AsympCSs for conducting anytime-valid causal inference. Since it is an important and thoroughly studied functional, we place a particular emphasis on the average treatment effect (ATE) for illustrative purposes but we discuss how these techniques apply to semiparametric functional estimation more generally in Section 3.5 . The literature on semiparametric functional inference often falls within the asymptotic regime and hence AsympCSs form a natural time-uniform extension thereof. It is important to note that obtaining AsympCSs for the ATE is not as simple as directly applying the theorems of Section 2.1 to some appropriately chosen augmented inverse-probability-weighted (AIPW) influence functions (otherwise the illustration of this section would have been trivial). Indeed, satisfying the conditions of the aforementioned theorems — Theorem 2.4 in particular — in the presence of infinite-dimensional nuisance parameters is nontrivial and the analysis proceeds rather differently from the fixed- n n setting. Nevertheless, after introducing and carefully analyzing sequential sample splitting and cross-fitting ( Section 3.1 ), we will see that asymptotic time-uniform inference for the ATE is possible. To solidify the notation and problem setup, suppose that we observe a (potentially infinite) sequence of i.i.d. variables Z 1 , Z 2 , … Z_{1},Z_{2},\dots from a distribution ℙ \mathbb{P} where Z t := ( X t , A t , Y t ) Z_{t}:=(X_{t},A_{t},Y_{t}) denotes the t th t^{\text{th}} subject’s triplet and X t ∈ ℝ d X_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d} are their measured baseline covariates, A t ∈ { 0 , 1 } A_{t}\in\{0,1\} is the treatment that they receive, and Y t ∈ ℝ Y_{t}\in\mathbb{R} is their measured outcome after treatment. Our target estimand is the average treatment effect (ATE) ψ \psi defined as where Y a Y^{a} is the counterfactual outcome for a randomly selected subject had they received treatment a ∈ { 0 , 1 } a\in\{0,1\} . The ATE ψ \psi can be interpreted as the average population outcome if everyone were treated 𝔼 ⁡ ( Y 1 ) \mathbb{E}(Y^{1}) versus if no one were treated 𝔼 ⁡ ( Y 0 ) \mathbb{E}(Y^{0}) . Under standard causal identification assumptions — typically referred to as consistency, positivity, and exchangeability (see e.g. Kennedy [24, §2.2] ) — we have that ψ \psi can be written as a (non-counterfactual) functional of the distribution ℙ \mathbb{P} : Throughout the remainder of this section, we will operate under these identification assumptions and aim to derive efficient AsympCSs for ψ \psi using tools from semiparametric theory. At a high level, we will construct AsympCSs for ψ \psi by combining the results of Section 2 with sample averages of influence functions for ψ \psi and in the ideal case, these influence functions will be efficient (in the semiparametric sense). Let ψ ^ t × \widehat{\psi}_{t}^{\times} be the cross-fit AIPW estimator as in ( 44 ). Suppose ‖ μ ^ t a ​ ( X ) − μ ¯ a ​ ( X ) ‖ L 2 ​ ( ℙ ) = o ⁡ ( 1 ) \|\widehat{\mu}^{a}_{t}(X)-\widebar{\mu}^{a}(X)\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{P})}=o(1) for each a ∈ { 0 , 1 } a\in\{0,1\} where μ ¯ a \widebar{\mu}^{a} is some function (but need not be μ a \mu^{a} ), and hence ‖ f ^ t − f ¯ ‖ L 2 ​ ( ℙ ) = o ⁡ ( 1 ) \|\widehat{f}_{t}-\widebar{f}\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{P})}=o(1) for some influence function f ¯ \widebar{f} . Suppose that propensity scores are bounded away from 0 and 1, i.e. π ⁡ ( X ) ∈ [ δ , 1 − δ ] \pi(X)\in[\delta,1-\delta] almost surely for some δ > 0 \delta>0 , and suppose that 𝔼 ​ | f ¯ ​ ( Z ) | 2 + ε < ∞ \mathbb{E}|\widebar{f}(Z)|^{2+\varepsilon}<\infty for some ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 . Then for any constant ρ > 0 \rho>0 , forms a ( 1 − α ) (1-\alpha) -AsympCS for ψ \psi . Consider the same setup as Theorem 3.1 but with π ⁡ ( x ) \pi(x) no longer known. ### 11. Sequential sample splitting and cross fitting

Following Robins et al. [45] , Zheng and van der Laan [69] , and Chernozhukov et al. [7] , we employ sample splitting to derive an estimate f ^ \widehat{f} of the influence function f f on a “training” sample, and evaluate f ^ \widehat{f} on values of Z t Z_{t} in an independent “evaluation” sample. Sample splitting sidesteps complications introduced from “double-dipping” (i.e. using Z t Z_{t} to both construct f ^ \widehat{f} and evaluate f ^ ​ ( Z t ) \widehat{f}(Z_{t}) ) and greatly simplifies the analysis of the downstream estimator. Since the aforementioned authors employed sample splitting in the batch (non-sequential) regime while we are concerned with settings where data are continually observed in an online stream over time, we modify the sample splitting procedure as follows. We will denote 𝒟 ∞ trn {\mathcal{D}}_{\infty}^{\mathrm{trn}} and 𝒟 ∞ eval {\mathcal{D}}_{\infty}^{\mathrm{eval}} as the “training” and “evaluation” sets, respectively. At time t t , we assign Z t Z_{t} to either group with equal probability: Note that at time t + 1 t+1 , Z t Z_{t} is not re-randomized into either split — once Z t Z_{t} is randomly assigned to one of 𝒟 ∞ trn {\mathcal{D}}_{\infty}^{\mathrm{trn}} or 𝒟 ∞ eval {\mathcal{D}}_{\infty}^{\mathrm{eval}} , they remain in that split for the remainder of the study. In this way, we can write 𝒟 ∞ trn = ( Z 1 trn , Z 2 trn , … ) {\mathcal{D}}_{\infty}^{\mathrm{trn}}=(Z_{1}^{\mathrm{trn}},Z_{2}^{\mathrm{trn}},\dots) and 𝒟 ∞ eval = ( Z 1 eval , Z 2 eval , … ) {\mathcal{D}}_{\infty}^{\mathrm{eval}}=(Z_{1}^{\mathrm{eval}},Z_{2}^{\mathrm{eval}},\dots) and think of these as independent, sequential observations from a common distribution ℙ \mathbb{P} . To keep track of how many subjects have been randomized to 𝒟 ∞ trn {\mathcal{D}}_{\infty}^{\mathrm{trn}} and 𝒟 ∞ eval {\mathcal{D}}_{\infty}^{\mathrm{eval}} at time t t , define where we have left the dependence on t t implicit.

### 12. The sequential sample-split estimators ( ψ ^ t split ) t = 1 ∞ (\widehat{\psi}_{t}^{\mathrm{split}})_{t=1}^{\infty} / The sequential cross-fit estimators ( ψ ^ t × ) t = 1 ∞ (\widehat{\psi}_{t}^{\times})_{t=1}^{\infty} / Asymptotic confidence sequences in observational studies

After employing sequential sample splitting, the sequence of sample-split estimators ( ψ ^ t split ) t = 1 ∞ (\widehat{\psi}_{t}^{\mathrm{split}})_{t=1}^{\infty} for ψ \psi are given by where f ^ T ′ \widehat{f}_{{T^{\prime}}} is given by the so-called efficient influence function (a brief review of semiparametric efficient estimators can be found in Section B.8 ), with η ≡ ( μ 1 , μ 0 , π ) \eta\equiv(\mu^{1},\mu^{0},\pi) replaced by η ^ T ′ ≡ ( μ ^ T ′ 1 , μ ^ T ′ 0 , π ¯ T ′ ) \widehat{\eta}_{{T^{\prime}}}\equiv(\widehat{\mu}^{1}_{{T^{\prime}}},\widehat{\mu}^{0}_{{T^{\prime}}},\widebar{\pi}_{{T^{\prime}}}) — where π ¯ T ′ \widebar{\pi}_{{T^{\prime}}} may be an estimator π ^ T ′ \widehat{\pi}_{{T^{\prime}}} of the propensity score π \pi , or the propensity score itself, depending on whether one is considering an observational study or randomized experiment — so that η ^ T ′ \widehat{\eta}_{T^{\prime}} is built solely from 𝒟 ∞ trn {\mathcal{D}}_{\infty}^{\mathrm{trn}} . The sample splitting procedure for constructing ψ ^ t split \widehat{\psi}_{t}^{\mathrm{split}} is summarized pictorially in Fig. 3 . In the batch setting for a fixed sample size, ( 42 ) is often referred to as the augmented inverse probability weighted (AIPW) estimator [ 47 , 48 ] (an instantiation of so-called “one-step correction” in the semiparametrics literature) and we adopt similar nomenclature here. A commonly cited downside of sample splitting is the loss in efficiency by using T ≈ t / 2 {T}\approx t/2 subjects instead of t t when evaluating the sample mean 1 T ​ ∑ i = 1 T f ^ T ′ ​ ( Z i eval ) \frac{1}{{T}}\sum_{i=1}^{T}\widehat{f}_{{T^{\prime}}}(Z_{i}^{\mathrm{eval}}) . An easy fix is to cross-fit : swap the two samples, using the evaluation set 𝒟 ∞ eval {\mathcal{D}}_{\infty}^{\mathrm{eval}} for training and the training set 𝒟 ∞ trn {\mathcal{D}}_{\infty}^{\mathrm{trn}} for evaluation to recover the full sample size of t ≡ T + T ′ t\equiv{T}+{T^{\prime}} [ 45 , 69 , 7 ] . That is, construct f ^ T \widehat{f}_{T} solely from 𝒟 ∞ eval {\mathcal{D}}_{\infty}^{\mathrm{eval}} and define the cross-fit estimator ψ ^ t × \widehat{\psi}_{t}^{\times} as and the associated cross-fit variance estimate where var ^ T ​ ( f ^ T ′ ) \widehat{\Var}_{T}(\widehat{f}_{T^{\prime}}) is the 𝒟 ∞ eval {\mathcal{D}}_{\infty}^{\mathrm{eval}} -sample variance of the pseudo-outcomes ( f ^ T ′ ​ ( Z i eval ) ) i = 1 T (\widehat{f}_{{T^{\prime}}}(Z_{i}^{\mathrm{eval}}))_{i=1}^{{T}} and similarly for var ^ T ′ \widehat{\Var}_{T^{\prime}} (we deliberately omit the subscript on f ^ \widehat{f} in the left-hand side of ( 45 )). All of the results that follow are stated in terms of the cross-fit estimators ( ψ ^ t × ) t = 1 ∞ (\widehat{\psi}_{t}^{\times})_{t=1}^{\infty} but they also hold using ( ψ ^ t split ) t = 1 ∞ (\widehat{\psi}_{t}^{\mathrm{split}})_{t=1}^{\infty} instead. With the setup of Section B.8 and Section 3.1 in mind, we are ready to derive AsympCSs for ψ \psi , first in randomized experiments. Consider now a sequential observational study (e.g. we are able to continuously monitor ( X t , A t , Y t ) t = 1 ∞ (X_{t},A_{t},Y_{t})_{t=1}^{\infty} but do not know π ⁡ ( x ) \pi(x) exactly, or we are in a sequentially randomized experiment with noncompliance, etc.). The only difference in this setting with respect to setup is the fact that π ⁡ ( x ) \pi(x) is no longer known and must be estimated. As in the fixed- n n setting, this complicates estimation and inference. The following theorem provides the conditions under which we can construct AsympCSs for ψ \psi using the cross-fit AIPW estimator in observational studies. The proof in Appendix A.5.2 proceeds similarly to the proof of Theorem 3.1 by combining Theorem 2.2 with an analysis of the almost-sure behavior of ( ψ ^ t × − ψ ) (\widehat{\psi}_{t}^{\times}-\psi) . Notice that the nuisance estimation rate of log ⁡ t / t \sqrt{\log t/t} is slower than 1 / t 1/\sqrt{t} which is usually required in the fixed- n n regime, but we do require almost-sure convergence rather than convergence in probability. Unlike the experimental setting of Section 3.2 , Theorem 3.2 requires that μ ^ t a \widehat{\mu}_{t}^{a} and π ^ t \widehat{\pi}_{t} consistently estimate μ a \mu^{a} and π \pi , respectively. As a consequence, the stacking-based AIPW AsympCS is both the tightest of the three and is uniquely consistent for ψ \psi (see Fig. 5 ).

### 13. The running average of individual treatment effects

The results in Sections 3.2 and 3.3 considered the classical regime where the ATE ψ \psi is a fixed functional that does not change over time. Consider a strict generalization where distributions — and hence individual treatment effects in particular — may change over time. In other words, where the equality ( ⋆ ) (\star) holds under the familiar causal identification assumptions discussed earlier. Despite the non-stationary and non- i.i.d. structure, it is nevertheless possible to derive AsympCSs for the running average of individual treatment effects ψ ~ t := 1 t ​ ∑ i = 1 t ψ i \widetilde{\psi}_{t}:=\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=1}^{t}\psi_{i} — or simply, the running average treatment effect — using the Lyapunov-type bounds of Corollary 2.6 . However, given this more general and complex setup, the assumptions required are more subtle (but no more restrictive) than those for Theorem s 3.1 and 3.2 ; as such, we explicitly describe their details here but handle the randomized and observational settings simultaneously for brevity. Condition ~ ATE -1 simply requires that the regression estimator μ ^ t a \widehat{\mu}_{t}^{a} must converge to some function μ ¯ a \widebar{\mu}^{a} , which need not coincide with true regression function μ a \mu^{a} . In the i.i.d. setting where X 1 , X 2 , … X_{1},X_{2},\dots all have the same distribution, we would simply drop the sup 1 ≤ i ≤ ∞ \sup_{1\leq i\leq\infty} , recovering the conditions for Theorems 3.1 and 3.2 . Note that Condition ~ ATE -2 would hold in two familiar scenarios. Firstly, in a randomized experiment (Theorem 3.3 ) where π ^ t = π \widehat{\pi}_{t}=\pi is known by design, we have that ( 53 ) is always zero, satisfying Condition ~ ATE -2 trivially. Second, in an observational study where the product of errors ‖ π ^ t ​ ( X i ) − π ⁡ ( X i ) ‖ L 2 ​ ( ℙ ) ​ ‖ μ ^ t a ​ ( X i ) − μ a ​ ( X i ) ‖ L 2 ​ ( ℙ ) \|\widehat{\pi}_{t}(X_{i})-\pi(X_{i})\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{P})}\|\widehat{\mu}_{t}^{a}(X_{i})-\mu^{a}(X_{i})\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{P})} vanishes at a rate faster than log ⁡ t / t \sqrt{\log t/t} , for each i i and for both a ∈ { 0 , 1 } a\in\{0,1\} , we also have that their average product errors vanish at the same rate ( 53 ). With these assumptions in mind, let us summarize how running average treatment effects can be captured in randomized experiments. The proof can be found in Section A.6 , and it is not hard to see that both Theorem s 3.1 and 3.2 are particular instantiations of Theorem 3.3 . The important takeaway from Theorem 3.3 is that under some rather mild conditions on the moments of ( f ¯ ​ ( Z t ) ) t = 1 ∞ (\widebar{f}(Z_{t}))_{t=1}^{\infty} , it is possible to derive an AsympCS for a running average treatment effect ψ ~ t \widetilde{\psi}_{t} (see Fig. 6 for what these look like in practice). Nevertheless, under the commonly considered regime where the treatment effect is constant ψ 1 = ψ 2 = ⋯ = ψ \psi_{1}=\psi_{2}=\cdots=\psi , we have that ( 54 ) forms a ( 1 − α ) (1-\alpha) -AsympCS for ψ \psi . Note that unlike Theorem s 3.1 and 3.2 , Theorem 3.3 actually does require the use of the cross-fit AIPW estimator ψ ^ t × \widehat{\psi}_{t}^{\times} and would not capture ψ ~ t \widetilde{\psi}_{t} if the sample-split version were used in its place.

<sub>§3.4 The running average of individual treatment effects</sub>

### 14. Extensions to general semiparametric estimation and the delta method

The discussion thus far has been focused on deriving confidence sequences for the ATE in the context of causal inference. However, the tools presented in this paper are more generally applicable to any pathwise differentiable functional with positive (and finite) semiparametric information bound. Some prominent examples in causal inference include modified interventions, complier-average effects, time-varying effects, and controlled mediation effects, among others. Examples outside causal inference include the expected density, entropy, the expected conditional variance, and the expected conditional covariance, to list a few. All of the aforementioned problems, including estimation of the ATE in Section 3 can be written in the following general form. Suppose Z 1 , Z 2 , ⋯ ∼ ℚ Z_{1},Z_{2},\dots\sim\mathbb{Q} and let θ ⁡ ( ℚ ) \theta(\mathbb{Q}) be some functional (such as those listed above) of the distribution ℚ \mathbb{Q} . In the case of a finite sample size n n , θ ^ n \widehat{\theta}_{n} is said to be an asymptotically linear estimator [ 55 ] for θ \theta if where ϕ \phi is the influence function of θ ^ n \widehat{\theta}_{n} . When the sample size is not fixed in advance, we may analogously say that θ ^ t \widehat{\theta}_{t} is an asymptotically linear time-uniform estimator if instead, with ϕ \phi being the same influence function as before. For example, in the case of the ATE with ( Z t ) t = 1 ∞ ∼ ℙ (Z_{t})_{t=1}^{\infty}\sim\mathbb{P} , we presented an efficient estimator ψ ^ t \widehat{\psi}_{t} which took the form, where f f is the uncentered efficient influence function (EIF) defined in ( 43 ). In order to justify that the remainder term is indeed o ⁡ ( log ⁡ t / t ) o(\sqrt{\log t/t}) , we used sequential sample splitting and additional analysis in the randomized and observational settings (see the proofs in Sections A.5 and A.5.2 for more details). In general, as long as an estimator θ ^ t \widehat{\theta}_{t} for θ \theta has the form ( 56 ), we may derive AsympCSs for θ \theta as a simple corollary of Theorem 2.2 . Clearly, the boundaries of Section s 2.2.4 or 2.6 could be used here in place of Theorem 2.2 , though for the boundary in Proposition 2.3 , we would need to strengthen the o ⁡ ( log ⁡ t / t ) o(\sqrt{\log t/t}) rate in ( 56 ) to o ⁡ ( log ⁡ log ⁡ t / t ) o(\sqrt{\log\log t/t}) . If computing θ ^ t \widehat{\theta}_{t} additionally involves the estimation of a nuisance parameter η \eta such as in Theorems 3.1 and 3.2 , this must be handled carefully on a case-by-case basis where sequential sample splitting and cross fitting ( Section 3.1 ) may be helpful, and higher moments on ϕ ⁡ ( Z i ) \phi(Z_{i}) may be needed. We now derive an analogue of the delta method for asymptotically linear time-uniform estimators. In particular, Proposition 3.5 can be combined with Corollary 3.4 to obtain delta method-like AsympCSs with asymptotic time-uniform coverage guarantees. The short proof in Section A.8 is similar to the proof of the classical delta method but with the almost-sure continuous mapping theorem used in place of the in-probability one, and with the law of the iterated logarithm used in place of the central limit theorem.

<sub>§3.5 Extensions to general semiparametric estimation and the delta method</sub>

## 总结与展望
这是 PACE 那套赌注 e-process 的统计学邻居：只要均值与方差有限，就能构造「时间一致」的置信序列——任意时刻停下来看都有效，而宽度只比固定样本的 CLT 区间略宽。它对我们的意义是给出第二条可选路线：如果不想用赌注（λ 无先验），可以改用这条闭式的置信序列，代价是需要估计方差、且是渐近结论。

**It connects to**

- `2009.03167` — 它引用的那篇给了非渐近的边界（经验 Bernstein），两者是「小样本保守」与「大样本紧凑」的两端；我们只有 8 对不一致时属于小样本那端。
- `2402.14992` — tinybenchmarks 的置信区间用的是固定样本 CLT，恰好是这篇指出的「不能边看边停」的那种；换成置信序列，边看边停才合法。

## 不足之处
**What a reader should still not conclude**

- 渐近：n 很小时边界不可信，而我们的战役多半只有个位数的配对。
- 机制段读到的是定义与构造思路，常数项（混合边界的具体参数）在定理里，摘录里看不到，直接照抄会算错。

# Qwen3 Technical Report

arXiv:2505.09388 · 2025 · [source](https://arxiv.org/html/2505.09388)

> Written by an agent that read the body (DeepSeek Harness, 2026-11-24) after reading the paper; the mechanism quotes are the paper’s own words.

## 出发点
The pursuit of artificial general intelligence (AGI) or artificial super intelligence (ASI) has long been a goal for humanity. Recent advancements in large foundation models, e.g., GPT-4o ( OpenAI, 2024 ) , Claude 3.7 ( Anthropic, 2025 ) , Gemini 2.5 ( DeepMind, 2025 ) , DeepSeek-V3 ( Liu et al., 2024a ) , Llama-4 ( Meta-AI, 2025 ) , and Qwen2.5 ( Yang et al., 2024b ) , have demonstrated significant progress toward this objective. These models are trained on vast datasets spanning trillions of tokens across diverse domains and tasks, effectively distilling human knowledge and capabilities into their parameters. Furthermore, recent developments in reasoning models, optimized through reinforcement learning, highlight the potential for foundation models to enhance inference-time scaling and achieve higher levels of intelligence, e.g., o3 ( OpenAI, 2025 ) , DeepSeek-R1 ( Guo et al., 2025 ) . While most state-of-the-art models remain proprietary, the rapid growth of open-source communities has substantially reduced the performance gap between open-weight and closed-source models. Notably, an increasing number of top-tier models ( Meta-AI, 2025 ; Liu et al., 2024a ; Guo et al., 2025 ; Yang et al., 2024b ) are now being released as open-source, fostering broader research and innovation in artificial intelligence.
