Instead, in this setting, the REGROW approach is a sensible generalisation of successful existing approaches. We first establish this for simple nulls:

<sub>§4.1 Composite ℋ 1 \mathcal{H}_{1} , no effect size known</sub>

### 3. Simple Nulls

If Θ 0 = { 0 } \Theta_{0}=\{0\} is simple, we have inf W 0 D ( P θ 1 ∥ P W 0 ) = D ( P θ 1 ∥ P 0 ) \inf_{W_{0}}D(P_{\theta_{1}}\|P_{W_{0}})=D(P_{\theta_{1}}\|P_{0}) and D ( P W 1 ∗ ∥ P W 0 ∗ ) = D ( P W 1 ∗ ∥ P 0 ) D(P_{W_{1}^{*}}\|P_{W_{0}^{*}})=D(P_{W_{1}^{*}}\|P_{0}) , and terms in ( 21 ) involving − log ⁡ p 0 ​ ( 𝐘 ) -\log p_{0}(\mathbf{Y}) cancel. Further using the 1-to-1 mapping ( 9 ) between probability densities and e -variables for the case of point 0 0 ’s to rewrite ( 21 ) and using E f = p W 1 ∗ ​ ( 𝐘 ) / p 0 ​ ( 𝐘 ) E^{f}=p_{W^{*}_{1}}({\bf Y})/p_{0}({\bf Y}) , ( 21 ) simplifies to: where the infimum is over all sub-probability densities q q over 𝐘 \mathbf{Y} . ( 22 ) is just the redundancy-capacity theorem ( Cover and Thomas, 1991 ) of information theory, and it has a data-compression interpretation. In a nutshell, for any e -variable of the form p W 1 ​ ( 𝐘 ) / p 0 ​ ( 𝐘 ) p_{W_{1}}(\mathbf{Y})/p_{0}(\mathbf{Y}) , the log \log evidence log ⁡ p W 1 ​ ( 𝐘 ) / p 0 ​ ( 𝐘 ) \log p_{W_{1}}(\mathbf{Y})/p_{0}(\mathbf{Y}) is thought of as a difference between the code length needed to code the data using two lossless codes, one with lengths − log ⁡ p W 1 -\log p_{W_{1}} , associated with ℋ 1 \mathcal{H}_{1} , and one with lengths − log ⁡ p 0 -\log p_{0} , associated with ℋ 0 \mathcal{H}_{0} . ( 22 ) expresses that when choosing W 1 = W 1 ∗ W_{1}=W^{*}_{1} , one associates ℋ 1 \mathcal{H}_{1} with the code that minimises worst-case redundancy (the additional expected number of bits needed compared to an encoder that knows θ 1 ∗ \theta^{*}_{1} ). This is in accordance with the MDL (Minimum Description Length) Principle, in which code length difference between the same two codes is used to measure evidence ( Barron et al., 1998 , Grünwald and Roos, 2020 ) .

<sub>§4.2 Composite ℋ 1 \mathcal{H}_{1} , nuisance parameters present</sub>

### 5. Theorem 1 in Full: Application to Bayesian and Sequential t t -test

If we try to apply Theorem 1 as above to the t t -test, a prototypical nuisance setting, we run into the issue that the minimum KL is not achieved. This problem can be solved by extending the theorem further, allowing for densities on a coarsening of 𝐘 \mathbf{Y} . This is any random variable 𝐕 \mathbf{V} that can be written as a function of 𝐘 \mathbf{Y} , i.e. 𝐕 = g ⁡ ( 𝐘 ) \mathbf{V}=g(\mathbf{Y}) for some function g g ; we retrieve the previous version of Theorem 1 if we take g g the identity and 𝐕 = 𝐘 \mathbf{V}=\mathbf{Y} . We now present Theorem 1 in full generality, allowing for such coarsening and additionally for considering the best e -variable on a modified ℋ 1 \mathcal{H}_{1} , consisting of any convex set of Bayes marginal distributions with priors on Θ 1 \Theta_{1} . This is needed for accommodating the t t -test. It also allows us to incorporate robust Bayesian settings ( Berger, 1985 , Grünwald and Dawid, 2004 ) , but we will not further pursue those here. In the theorem we use the following notation: for (sub-) distribution P P for 𝐘 \mathbf{Y} , P [ 𝐕 ] P^{[\mathbf{V}]} denotes the marginal (sub-) distribution of P P for 𝐕 \mathbf{V} , and p ′ p^{\prime} denotes its density. Theorem 1 , Full Generality Let f ⁡ ( θ ) f(\theta) be a function that is bounded on Θ 1 \Theta_{1} . Suppose all P θ , P θ ′ P_{\theta},P_{\theta^{\prime}} with θ , θ ′ ∈ Θ 1 \theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta_{1} satisfy D ( P θ ∥ P θ ′ ) < ∞ D(P_{\theta}\|P_{\theta^{\prime}})<\infty , and have full support. Let 𝒲 1 ⊆ 𝒲 ⁡ ( Θ 1 ) \mathcal{W}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{W}(\Theta_{1}) be convex. If for some coarsening 𝐕 \mathbf{V} of 𝐘 \mathbf{Y} we have: p ∗ ′ 0 p^{*^{\prime}}_{0} being the density of P 0 ∗ [ 𝐕 ] P^{*[\mathbf{V}]}_{0} , a (potentially sub-) distribution for 𝐕 \mathbf{V} that satisfies inf W 0 ∈ 𝒲 ⁡ ( Θ 0 ) D ( P W 1 ∗ ∥ P W 0 ) = D ( P W 1 ∗ [ 𝐕 ] ∥ P 0 ∗ [ 𝐕 ] ) \inf_{W_{0}\in\mathcal{W}(\Theta_{0})}D(P_{W^{*}_{1}}\|P_{W_{0}})=D(P^{[\mathbf{V}]}_{W_{1}^{*}}\|P^{*[\mathbf{V}]}_{0}) . E f E^{f} satisfies, essentially uniquely: If further D ( P W 1 ∗ [ 𝐕 ] ∥ P 0 ∗ [ 𝐕 ] ) = D ( P W 1 ∗ [ 𝐕 ] ∥ P W 0 ∗ [ 𝐕 ] ) D(P_{W_{1}^{*}}^{[\mathbf{V}]}\|P^{*[\mathbf{V}]}_{0})=D(P_{W_{1}^{*}}^{[\mathbf{V}]}\|P_{W_{0}^{*}}^{[\mathbf{V}]}) for some W 0 ∗ ∈ 𝒲 ⁡ ( Θ 0 ) W_{0}^{*}\in\mathcal{W}(\Theta_{0}) , then P 0 ∗ [ 𝐕 ] = P W 0 ∗ [ 𝐕 ] P^{*[\mathbf{V}]}_{0}=P_{W_{0}^{*}}^{[\mathbf{V}]} . The previous version of Theorem 1 is the special case obtained by setting 𝒲 1 = 𝒲 ⁡ ( Θ 1 ) \mathcal{W}_{1}=\mathcal{W}(\Theta_{1}) , 𝐘 = 𝐕 \mathbf{Y}=\mathbf{V} and using linearity of expectation. We call E f E^{f} as in ( 27 ) the REGROW e -variable relative to offset f f and set of priors 𝒲 1 \mathcal{W}_{1} . If f f is constant (no offset), we call it 𝒲 1 \mathcal{W}_{1} -GROW , noting that it gives worst-case optimal growth rate under all priors in 𝒲 1 \mathcal{W}_{1} .

<sub>§4.3 Theorem 1 in Full: Application to Bayesian and Sequential t t -test</sub>

### 6. The t t -test Setting

We return to the setting with a nuisance parameter with notation as in Section 4.2 . Jeffreys (1961) proposed a Bayesian version of the t t -test; see also ( Rouder et al., 2009 ) . We start with the models ℋ 0 \mathcal{H}_{0} and ℋ 1 \mathcal{H}_{1} for data 𝐘 = ( Y 1 , … , Y n ) \mathbf{Y}=(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}) given as ℋ 0 = { P 0 , σ ​ ( 𝐘 ) ∣ σ ∈ Γ } \mathcal{H}_{0}=\{P_{0,\sigma}(\mathbf{Y})\mid\sigma\in\Gamma\} ; ℋ 1 = { P δ , σ ​ ( 𝐘 ) ∣ ( δ , σ ) ∈ Θ 1 } \mathcal{H}_{1}=\{P_{\delta,\sigma}(\mathbf{Y})\mid(\delta,\sigma)\in\Theta_{1}\} , where Δ = ℝ , Γ = ℝ + \Delta=\mathbb{R},\Gamma=\mathbb{R}^{+} , Θ 1 ≔ Δ × Γ \Theta_{1}\coloneqq\Delta\times\Gamma and Θ 0 = { ( 0 , σ ) : σ ∈ Γ } \Theta_{0}=\{(0,\sigma):\sigma\in\Gamma\} , and P δ , σ P_{\delta,\sigma} has density (with y ¯ = 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n y i \overline{y}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i} ) Jeffreys proposed to equip ℋ 1 \mathcal{H}_{1} with a Cauchy prior W c ​ [ δ ] W^{\text{\sc c}}[\delta] on the effect size δ \delta , and both ℋ 1 \mathcal{H}_{1} and ℋ 0 \mathcal{H}_{0} with the scale-invariant prior measure with density w H ​ ( σ ) ∝ 1 / σ w^{H}(\sigma)\propto 1/\sigma on the variance. The same formula with the same prior on σ \sigma but other priors on δ \delta was suggested by Lai (1976) with a non-Bayesian, martingale interpretation. Below we will see that, even though w H ​ ( σ ) w^{H}(\sigma) is improper (whereas the priors appearing in Theorem 1 are invariably proper), the resulting Bayes factor E ∗ E^{*} is an e -variable. We then present Theorem 2 which shows that, for priors W ⁡ [ δ ] W[\delta] with more than 2 moments, E ∗ E^{*} in fact even is 𝒲 1 \mathcal{W}_{1} -GROW with 𝒲 1 \mathcal{W}_{1} the set of all product priors on δ × σ \delta\times\sigma with marginal W ⁡ [ δ ] W[\delta] on δ \delta , i.e. it has a worst-case optimal growth rate property relative to all distributions in ℋ 1 \mathcal{H}_{1} compatible with W ⁡ [ δ ] W[\delta] . Thus, a form of GROW-optimality holds for most priors W ⁡ [ δ ] W[\delta] one might want to use, including standard choices (such as a standard normal) or the point prior we will suggest further below — but we do not know if it holds for the moment-less Cauchy proposed by Jeffreys.

<sub>§The t t -test Setting</sub>

### 7. Almost Bayesian Case: prior on δ \delta available

For any proper prior distribution W ⁡ [ δ ] W[\delta] on δ \delta and any proper prior distribution W ⁡ [ σ ] W[\sigma] on σ \sigma , we define p W ⁡ [ δ ] , W ⁡ [ σ ] ​ ( y ) = ∫ δ ∈ Δ ∫ σ ∈ Γ p δ , σ ​ ( y ) ​ d ​ W ​ [ δ ] ​ d ​ W ​ [ σ ] , p_{W[\delta],W[\sigma]}(y)=\int_{\delta\in\Delta}\int_{\sigma\in\Gamma}p_{\delta,\sigma}(y)\,\textnormal{d}W{[\delta]}\,\textnormal{d}W{[\sigma]}, as the Bayes marginal density under the product prior W ⁡ [ δ ] × W ⁡ [ σ ] W[\delta]\times W[\sigma] . For convenience later on we set the sample space to be 𝒴 n = ( ℝ ∖ { 0 } ) × ℝ n − 1 \mathcal{Y}^{n}=(\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\})\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1} , assuming beforehand that the first outcome will not be 0. Now we define 𝐕 ≔ ( V 1 , … , V n ) \mathbf{V}\coloneqq(V_{1},\ldots,V_{n}) with V i = Y i / | Y 1 | V_{i}=Y_{i}/|Y_{1}| . We have that 𝐘 \mathbf{Y} determines 𝐕 \mathbf{V} , and ( 𝐕 , Y 1 ) (\mathbf{V},Y_{1}) determines 𝐘 = ( Y 1 , Y 2 , … , Y n ) \mathbf{Y}=(Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{n}) . The distributions in ℋ 0 ∪ ℋ 1 \mathcal{H}_{0}\cup\mathcal{H}_{1} can thus alternatively be thought of as distributions on the pair ( 𝐕 , Y 1 ) (\mathbf{V},Y_{1}) . 𝐕 \mathbf{V} is “ 𝐘 \mathbf{Y} with the scale divided out”: as is well-known ( Lai, 1976 , Berger et al., 1998 ) and easily shown (Appendix B.3 ), under all P ∈ ℋ 0 P\in\mathcal{H}_{0} , i.e. all P 0 , σ P_{0,\sigma} with σ > 0 \sigma>0 , 𝐕 \mathbf{V} has the same distribution P 0 ​ [ 𝐕 ] P_{0}[\mathbf{V}] with density p 0 ′ p^{\prime}_{0} . In the same way, one shows that under all P W ⁡ [ δ ] , σ {P}_{W[\delta],\sigma} with σ > 0 \sigma>0 , 𝐕 \mathbf{V} has the same pdf p W ⁡ [ δ ] ′ p^{\prime}_{W[\delta]} (which therefore does not depend on the prior on σ \sigma ). We now get that, with we must have 𝐄 𝐕 ∼ P ​ [ E ∗ ] = 1 \mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{V}\sim P}[E^{*}]=1 for all P ∈ ℋ 0 P\in\mathcal{H}_{0} , hence it is an e variable. Remarkably, this ‘scale-free’ e -variable coincides with the Bayes factor one gets if one uses, for σ \sigma , the prior w H ​ ( σ ) = 1 / σ w^{H}(\sigma)=1/\sigma suggested by Jeffreys, and treats σ \sigma and δ \delta as independent. That is ( Lai, 1976 , page 273) (a full derivation is in Appendix B.3 ), we have Despite its improperness, w H w^{H} induces a valid e -variable when used in the Bayes factor. The equivalence of this Bayes factor to E ∗ E^{*} simply means that it manages to ignore the ‘nuisance’ part of the model and models the likelihood of the scale-free 𝐕 \mathbf{V} instead. The reason this is possible is that w H w^{H} coincides with the right-Haar prior for this problem ( Eaton, 1989 , Berger et al., 1998 ) , about which we will say more below. Amazingly, it turns out that the e -variable ( 29 ) has a GROW property (among all e -variables for data 𝐘 \mathbf{Y} , not just the coarsened 𝐕 \mathbf{V} ) under the weak condition that the prior W ⁡ [ δ ] W[\delta] has a ( 2 + ϵ ) (2+\epsilon) th moment. This follows from a special case of Theorem 4.2. of ( Perez et al., 2022 ) (for the case that W ⁡ [ δ ] W[\delta] puts all its mass on a single δ \delta ) and Corollary 8.3. (for general W ⁡ [ δ ] W[\delta] . For convenience we re-state this special case here. Let, for priors W ⁡ [ δ ] , W ⁡ [ σ ] W[\delta],W[\sigma] , P W ⁡ [ δ ] , W ⁡ [ σ ] [ 𝐕 ] P_{W[\delta],W[\sigma]}^{[\mathbf{V}]} be the marginal distribution with density p W ⁡ [ δ ] , W ⁡ [ σ ] ′ p^{\prime}_{W[\delta],W[\sigma]} . We have: The theorem allows us to use Theorem 1 as above with constant f ⁡ ( δ , σ ) = 0 f(\delta,\sigma)=0 (note that 𝒲 1 \mathcal{W}_{1} is convex) to conclude that E ∗ E^{*} as in ( 28 ) is equal to E f E^{f} as in ( 27 ): the Bayes factor based on the right Haar prior, is not just an e -variable, but is even GROW relative to the set of all priors on δ × σ \delta\times\sigma that are compatible with W ⁡ [ δ ] W[\delta] .

<sub>§Almost Bayesian Case: prior on δ \delta available</sub>

### 8. REGROW-GROW safe t t -test with minimum effect sizes

Suppose we want to test Θ 1 \Theta_{1} vs. Θ 0 \Theta_{0} as in ( 23 ) with fixed effect sizes δ + \delta^{+} and δ − \delta^{-} and with σ 2 \sigma^{2} in the role of γ \gamma . We proceed exactly as we did underneath ( 23 ): we first consider the test { ( δ + , σ 2 ) : σ 2 > 0 } \{(\delta^{+},\sigma^{2}):\sigma^{2}>0\} vs. { ( δ − , σ 2 ) : σ 2 > 0 } \{(\delta^{-},\sigma^{2}):\sigma^{2}>0\} for the fixed given δ + \delta^{+} using the REGROW criterion with f ⁡ ( ( δ , σ ) ) = GRO ​ ( δ , σ ) f((\delta,\sigma))=\text{GRO}(\delta,\sigma) . We have ( Koolen and Grünwald, 2021 , Section 4.3) that f ⁡ ( δ + , σ ) = ( n / 2 ) ​ log ⁡ ( 1 + δ + 2 ) f(\delta^{+},\sigma)=(n/2)\log(1+\delta^{+2}) is constant on σ \sigma . Therefore we can use Theorem 1 in its most general form above in combination with Theorem 2 (applied with point prior W ⁡ [ δ ] W[\delta] on δ + \delta^{+} ) to conclude that (both the GROW and) the REGROW e -variable are given by E δ + ∗ := p δ + ′ ​ ( 𝐕 ) / p δ − ′ ​ ( 𝐕 ) E^{*}_{\delta^{+}}:=p^{\prime}_{\delta^{+}}(\mathbf{V})/p^{\prime}_{\delta^{-}}(\mathbf{V}) . Since Proposition 3 is applicable to sets of distributions defined on 𝐕 \mathbf{V} rather than 𝐘 \mathbf{Y} (details in Appendix B.3 ), we find that, with ℰ ≥ δ + := { E δ ∗ : δ ≥ δ − } \mathcal{E}_{\geq\delta^{+}}:=\{E^{*}_{\delta}:\delta\geq\delta^{-}\} that sup E ∈ ℰ ≥ δ + inf σ > 0 , δ ≥ δ + 𝐄 𝐘 ∼ P δ , σ ​ [ log ⁡ E ] = inf σ > 0 , δ ≥ δ + 𝐄 𝐘 ∼ P δ , σ ​ [ log ⁡ E δ + ∗ ] \sup_{E\in\mathcal{E}_{\geq\delta^{+}}}\inf_{\sigma>0,\delta\geq\delta^{+}}\mathbf{E}_{{\bf Y}\sim P_{\delta,\sigma}}[\log E]=\inf_{\sigma>0,\delta\geq\delta^{+}}\mathbf{E}_{{\bf Y}\sim P_{\delta,\sigma}}[\log E^{*}_{\delta^{+}}] so E δ + ∗ E^{*}_{\delta^{+}} may be thought of as first applying REGROW, to get rid of the nuisance parameter, and then applying GROW — just like in the 2 × 2 2\times 2 Example 7 .

<sub>§REGROW-GROW safe t t -test with minimum effect sizes</sub>

### 9. Extension to General Group Invariant Bayes Factors

In a series of papers ( Berger et al., 1998 , Dass and Berger, 2003 , Bayarri et al., 2012 ) , Berger and collaborators developed a theory of Bayes factors for ℋ 0 = { P 0 , γ : γ ∈ Γ } \mathcal{H}_{0}=\{P_{0,\gamma}:\gamma\in\Gamma\} and ℋ 1 = { P δ , γ : δ ∈ Δ , γ ∈ Γ } \mathcal{H}_{1}=\{P_{\delta,\gamma}:\delta\in\Delta,\gamma\in\Gamma\} with a nuisance parameter (vector) γ \gamma that appears in both models and that satisfies a group invariance; the Bayesian t t -test is the special case with γ = σ , Γ = ℝ + \gamma=\sigma,\Gamma=\mathbb{R}^{+} and with the scalar multiplication group and δ \delta an ‘effect size’. Other examples include regression based on mixtures of g g -priors ( Liang et al., 2008 ) , testing a Weibull vs. the log-normal and many more ( Dass and Berger, 2003 ) . The reasoning of the first part of this section straightforwardly generalises to all such cases: the Bayes factor based on using the right Haar measure on γ \gamma in both models gives rise to an e -variable. Theorem 4.2. of Perez et al. (2022) shows that, if the underlying group satisfies a condition called amenability (which holds, e.g., for scaling as in the t-test, but also for e.g. rotations and affine transformations as in parametric linear regression models), then the resulting Bayes factor is GROW relative to a suitably defined set 𝒲 1 \mathcal{W}_{1} . Theorem 2 above is the very special case of their result when instantiated with γ \gamma instantiated to the variance in the t-test (scaling). Although its proof is quite different, the general result may be viewed as the ‘e-variant’ of the classical Hunt-Stein theorem ( Lehmann et al., 2005 , Section 8.5) , with ‘power’ in that theorem replaced by ‘GROW’. ( Perez et al., 2022 , Proposition 4.4) then implies that in all such cases, this GROW Bayes factor is in fact also REGROW. Remarkably therefore, with parameters representing group transformations, unlike e.g. for the 2 × 2 2\times 2 case, GROW and REGROW e-variables generally coincide.

<sub>§Extension to General Group Invariant Bayes Factors</sub>

### 10. (RE)GRO(W), Optional Continuation and Stopping

We focused on Type-I error safety under optional continuation (OC). Can we also get safety under optional stopping (OS), and what is the difference? The GRO-criteria were chosen to optimise expected capital (logarithmic) growth ‘locally’, within a study. How well do GRO-criteria go together with OC over several studies? To make the questions concrete, we consider a specific set-up with data stream Y j , 1 , Y j , 2 , … Y_{j,1},Y_{j,2},\ldots corresponding to the j j -th study to be performed. In the first study we observe batch of outcomes 𝐘 ( 1 ) = ( Y 1 , 1 , … , Y 1 , N ( 1 ) ) \mathbf{Y}_{(1)}=(Y_{1,1},\ldots,Y_{1,N_{(1)}}) ; in the second study (if it is performed at all) 𝐘 ( 2 ) = ( Y 2 , 1 , … , Y 2 , N ( 2 ) ) \mathbf{Y}_{(2)}=(Y_{2,1},\ldots,Y_{2,N_{(2)}}) ; and so on. For further simplicity we will assume that all Y j , i Y_{j,i} are i.i.d. The set-up slightly differs from Example 3 in which the second study’s data was part of the same stream as the first; at the expense of additional notation, everything that follows can be formalized in that setting as well. The conditional e -variables determining our test martingale are now determined by a sequence of stopping times N ( 1 ) , N ( 2 ) , … N_{(1)},N_{(2)},\ldots . The first stopping time N ( 1 ) N_{(1)} is defined as a stopping time on the first sequence Y 1 , 1 , Y 1 , 2 , … Y_{1,1},Y_{1,2},\ldots relative to filtration ( σ ⁡ ( Y 1 n ) ) n (\sigma(Y_{1}^{n}))_{n} and defines a stopped σ \sigma -algebra ℱ ( 1 ) := σ ⁡ ( Y 1 N ( 1 ) ) = σ ⁡ ( 𝐘 ( 1 ) ) \mathcal{F}_{(1)}:=\sigma(Y_{1}^{N_{(1)}})=\sigma(\mathbf{Y}_{(1)}) . N ( 2 ) N_{(2)} is defined on the second sequence Y 2 , 1 , Y 2 , 2 , … Y_{2,1},Y_{2,2},\ldots , but it is also allowed to depend on previous data 𝐘 ( 1 ) \mathbf{Y}_{(1)} , i.e it is a stopping time relative to filtration ( σ ⁡ ( 𝐘 ( 1 ) , Y 2 n ) ) n (\sigma({\bf Y}_{(1)},Y_{2}^{n}))_{n} , and defines a stopped σ \sigma -algebra ℱ ( 2 ) := σ ⁡ ( 𝐘 ( 1 ) , 𝐘 ( 2 ) ) \mathcal{F}_{(2)}:=\sigma(\mathbf{Y}_{(1)},\mathbf{Y}_{(2)}) . In general, N ( m ) N_{(m)} is defined relative to filtration σ ​ ( 𝐘 ( 1 ) , … , 𝐘 ( m − 1 ) , Y m n ) n \sigma(\mathbf{Y}_{(1)},\ldots,\mathbf{Y}_{(m-1)},Y_{m}^{n})_{n} , and defines a stopped σ \sigma -algebra ℱ ( m ) := σ ⁡ ( 𝐘 ( 1 ) , … , 𝐘 ( m ) ) \mathcal{F}_{(m)}:=\sigma(\mathbf{Y}_{(1)},\ldots,\mathbf{Y}_{(m)}) . We let 𝒩 ( m ) \mathcal{N}_{(m)} be the collection of all stopping times for the m m -th study, i.e. relative to σ ​ ( 𝐘 ( 1 ) , … , 𝐘 ( m − 1 ) , Y m , 1 , … , Y m , n ) n \sigma(\mathbf{Y}_{(1)},\ldots,\mathbf{Y}_{(m-1)},Y_{m,1},\ldots,Y_{m,n})_{n} . The sequence of stopped σ \sigma -algebras thus defines a new filtration ( ℱ ( m ) ) m (\mathcal{F}_{(m)})_{m} , which we call the filtration at the study level , since ℱ ( m ) \mathcal{F}_{(m)} denotes all information available after m m studies or trials have been completed — it is the filtration referred to in Proposition 2 (for the t-test we need to extend this set-up a little, see Example 8 ).

<sub>§5 (RE)GRO(W), Optional Continuation and Stopping</sub>

### 11. OC vs. OS

In the optional continuation setting, we assume that, after having observed and analyzed m − 1 m-1 studies, we either stop, or continue to the next study. In the latter case we need to specify a stopping time N ( m ) ∈ 𝒩 ( m ) N_{(m)}\in\mathcal{N}_{(m)} and a conditional e-variable E ( m ) ∈ ℰ ( m ) E_{(m)}\in\mathcal{E}_{(m)} where ℰ ( m ) \mathcal{E}_{(m)} is the set of all ℱ ( m − 1 ) \mathcal{F}_{(m-1)} -conditional e-variables. For example, in the setting of Example 1 , with a simple null ℋ 0 = { P 0 } \mathcal{H}_{0}=\{P_{0}\} and composite alternative ℋ 1 = { P θ : θ ∈ Θ 1 } \mathcal{H}_{1}=\{P_{\theta}:\theta\in\Theta_{1}\} and N ( m ) ∈ 𝒩 ( m ) N_{(m)}\in\mathcal{N}_{(m)} an arbitrary stopping time for the m m -th study, e N ( m ) , W ​ ( Y m , 1 , … , Y m , N ( m ) ) := p W ​ ( Y m , 1 , … , Y m , N ( m ) ) / p 0 ​ ( Y m , 1 , … , Y m , N ( m ) ) e_{N_{(m)},W}(Y_{m,1},\ldots,Y_{m,N_{(m)}}):=p_{W}(Y_{m,1},\ldots,Y_{m,N_{(m)}})/p_{0}(Y_{m,1},\ldots,Y_{m,N_{(m)}}) is an e -variable for every ‘prior’ distribution W W on Θ 1 \Theta_{1} (here we generalize the notation of Example 1 to allow for data-dependent stopping times). In this setting the analyst can freely choose (or somebody else can impose) any N ( m ) N_{(m)} and any W W and use the corresponding e -variable e N ( m ) , W e_{N_{(m)},W} ; all such e -variables are contained in the set ℰ ( m ) \mathcal{E}_{(m)} . As explained in Example 1 , this includes the choice to set W ( m ) := W ( 1 ) W_{(m)}:=W_{(1)} (re-use the original prior) and the choice to set W ( m ) := W ( 1 ) ( ⋅ ∣ 𝐘 ( m − 1 ) ) W_{(m)}:=W_{(1)}(\cdot\mid\mathbf{Y}^{(m-1)}) , i.e. use the Bayes posterior based on the previous studies. We can now interpret our various GRO criteria as each providing a prescription to choose specific elements of ℰ N ( m ) \mathcal{E}_{N_{(m)}} , for all stopping times N ( m ) N_{(m)} that are constant given the outcomes of previous studies 𝐘 ( m − 1 ) \mathbf{Y}^{(m-1)} , i.e. that are ℱ ( m − 1 ) \mathcal{F}_{(m-1)} -measurable. To see this, note that each GRO criterion in combination with ℋ 0 \mathcal{H}_{0} and ℋ 1 \mathcal{H}_{1} defines, for each n n , an e -variable S [ n ] S^{[n]} for a single sequence Y 1 , … , Y n Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n} . In all cases, this can be written as S [ n ] = s [ n ] ​ ( Y n ) S^{[n]}=s^{[n]}(Y^{n}) for some function s [ n ] s^{[n]} ; the function s [ n ] s^{[n]} is what is really specified; we call s [ 1 ] , s [ 2 ] , … s^{[1]},s^{[2]},\ldots an e-specification. If N ( m ) := n m N_{(m)}:=n_{m} , we can thus simply set E ( m ) := s [ n m ] ​ ( Y m , 1 , … , Y m , n m ) E_{(m)}:=s^{[n_{m}]}(Y_{m,1},\ldots,Y_{m,n_{m}}) . We call this the plug-in method for constructing E ( m ) E_{(m)} from specification s [ 1 ] , s [ 2 ] , … s^{[1]},s^{[2]},\ldots (such specification may correspond to one of our GRO criteria, but in general it could be arrived at in different ways as well) . The running product of E ( m ) E_{(m)} thus constructed provides, via Proposition 2 , a test martingale at the study level. In contrast, optional stopping scenarios usually concern only a single data-level process Y 1 , Y 2 , … Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots , without any super-structure in terms of subsequent studies. The scenario is completely determined by a sequence of conditional e -variables S 1 , S 2 , … S_{1},S_{2},\ldots , i.e., applying Definition 1 at the data level, such that for all n ∈ ℕ n\in\mathbb{N} , for all P ∈ ℋ 0 : 𝐄 P [ S n ∣ ℱ n − 1 ] ≤ 1 a.s. \text{for all $P\in\mathcal{H}_{0}$:}\ \ {\bf E}_{P}[S_{n}\mid\mathcal{F}_{n-1}]\leq 1\text{\ a.s.} , with ℱ n = σ ⁡ ( Y n ) \mathcal{F}_{n}=\sigma(Y^{n}) . Their running product S [ 1 ] , S [ 2 ] , … S^{[1]},S^{[2]},\ldots (with S [ n ] = ∏ i = 1 n S i S^{[n]}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}S_{i} ) then forms a test martingale. Recall from Corollary 1 in Section 1.3 that for any nonnegative random process E ( 1 ) , E ( 2 ) , … E^{(1)},E^{(2)},\ldots at the study level (adapted to ( ℱ ( m ) ) m (\mathcal{F}_{(m)})_{m} ) we say that the corresponding threshold test is safe under OC (with respect to Type-I error) if the Ville-Robbins inequality ( 7 ) holds. Extending this definition in the natural way, we say, for a data-level process of nonnegative random variables S [ 1 ] , S [ 2 ] , … S^{[1]},S^{[2]},\ldots , i.e. with S [ j ] S^{[j]} adapted to ℱ j \mathcal{F}_{j} , that the corresponding threshold test is safe under OS (with respect to Type-I error) if again the Ville-Robbins inequality holds (with E ( n ) := S [ n ] E^{(n)}:=S^{[n]} ).

<sub>§OC vs. OS</sub>

### 12. Sequentially Decomposable e -Specifications

In many (not all) cases, the GRO-specification s [ 1 ] , s [ 2 ] , … s^{[1]},s^{[2]},\ldots forms itself a test martingale relative to some filtration ( 𝒢 n ) n (\mathcal{G}_{n})_{n} : there exist a sequence of functions s 1 , s 2 , … s_{1},s_{2},\ldots , with s i s_{i} a function on 𝒴 i \mathcal{Y}^{i} , such that, for all n n , s [ n ] ​ ( Y n ) = ∏ i = 1 n S i s^{[n]}(Y^{n})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}S_{i} with S i := s i ​ ( Y i ) S_{i}:=s_{i}(Y^{i}) and { S i } i \{S_{i}\}_{i} is a conditional e-variable collection relative to filtration ( 𝒢 n ) n (\mathcal{G}_{n})_{n} . We will say that such an e -variable specification is sequentially decomposable , or seqdec for short, relative to filtration ( 𝒢 n ) n (\mathcal{G}_{n})_{n} ; in all our examples except the t-test (Example 8 ) we can take 𝒢 n = σ ⁡ ( Y n ) \mathcal{G}_{n}=\sigma(Y^{n}) . Seqdec specifications have a direct link with the OS setting, resulting in three remarkable properties: first, assuming still that data are i.i.d., any study-level test martingale process we can construct via the plug-in method (see above) based on a seqdec specification also defines a sequence of conditional e -variables and hence a test martingale at the corresponding concatenated data level Y 1 ′ , Y 2 ′ , … Y^{\prime}_{1},Y^{\prime}_{2},\ldots where Y i ′ Y^{\prime}_{i} is arrived at by relabeling Y 1 , 1 , Y 1 , 2 , … , Y 1 , N ( 1 ) , Y 2 , 1 , Y 2 , 2 , … , Y 2 , N ( 2 ) , Y 3 , 1 , … Y_{1,1},Y_{1,2},\ldots,Y_{1,N_{(1)}},Y_{2,1},Y_{2,2},\ldots,Y_{2,N_{(2)}},Y_{3,1},\ldots in order (so that e.g. Y N ( 1 ) + N ( 2 ) ′ = Y 2 , N ( 2 ) Y^{\prime}_{N_{(1)}+N_{(2)}}=Y_{2,N_{(2)}} ). This defines a concatenated data-level filtration ( 𝒢 t ′ ) t (\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{t})_{t} with 𝒢 t ′ = σ ⁡ ( Y 1 ′ , … , Y t ′ ) \mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{t}=\sigma(Y^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,Y^{\prime}_{t}) . The corresponding sequence of conditional e -variables is then given by S 1 ′ , S 2 ′ , … S^{\prime}_{1},S^{\prime}_{2},\ldots where, for T ( 0 ) := 0 T_{(0)}:=0 , m ∈ ℕ m\in\mathbb{N} , 1 ≤ i < N ( m ) 1\leq i<N_{(m)} , T ( m ) := ∑ j = 1 m N ( j ) T_{(m)}:=\sum_{j=1}^{m}N_{(j)} , we set S T ( m ) + i ′ := s i ​ ( Y m , 1 , … , Y m , i ) S^{\prime}_{T_{(m)}+i}:=s_{i}(Y_{m,1},\ldots,Y_{m,i}) . This means that besides engaging in optional continuation, we can also safely do optional stopping at this concatenated-data level, since the Ville-Robbins inequality holds at this level by Corollary 1 . Second, we can use seqdec specifications to extend the plug-in method (which required constant stopping times N ( m ) N_{(m)} ) to prescribe conditional E-variables for stopping times N ( m ) N_{(m)} that are not constant given 𝐘 ( m − 1 ) \mathbf{Y}^{(m-1)} : for arbitrary N ( m ) ∈ 𝒩 ( m ) N_{(m)}\in\mathcal{N}_{(m)} , we set E ( m ) := ∏ i = 1 N ( m ) s i ​ ( Y m , 1 , … , Y m , i ) E_{(m)}:=\prod_{i=1}^{N_{(m)}}s_{i}(Y_{m,1},\ldots,Y_{m,i}) . By construction, this reduces to the plug-in method whenever N ( m ) N_{(m)} is constant given 𝐘 ( m − 1 ) \mathbf{Y}^{(m-1)} , so it is a proper extension, and it follows as a direct corollary of the fact that E ( m ) E_{(m)} can be rewritten as ∏ i = 1 N ( m ) S T ( m − 1 ) + i ′ \prod_{i=1}^{N_{(m)}}S^{\prime}_{T_{(m-1)}+i} , i.e. a product of factors in a test martingale, that any E ( m ) E_{(m)} constructed in this manner for any seqdec specification is a ℱ ( m − 1 ) \mathcal{F}_{(m-1)} -conditional e -variable. Third, seqdec specifications allow for alternative ways to create study-level processes from e -specifications beyond the plug-in method used thus far. For example, we can set E ( m ) := ∏ i = 1 N ( m ) s T ( m − 1 ) + i ​ ( 𝐘 ( m − 1 ) , Y m , 1 , … , Y m , i ) E_{(m)}:=\prod_{i=1}^{N_{(m)}}s_{T_{(m-1)}+i}(\mathbf{Y}^{(m-1)},Y_{m,1},\ldots,Y_{m,i}) for arbitrary stopping times N ( 1 ) , … , N ( m ) N_{(1)},\ldots,N_{(m)} . Once again, this also defines a martingale at the concatenated data-level — it is simply the martingale that arises if we view the m m studies as one single, long sequence of T ( m ) T_{(m)} data points. We call this the sequential application of the e -variable specification — see Figure 3 . Summarizing, the practical setting we have in mind when we speak about OS and OC respectively is quite different: OC concerns study-level martingales constructed by deciding, on the fly , after the m − 1 m-1 -st study, whether to continue to the m m -th study and if so, what new ℱ ( m − 1 ) \mathcal{F}_{(m-1)} -conditional e -variable to take from the set ℰ ( m ) \mathcal{E}_{(m)} of possible e -variables of use. OS is about data-level martingales with only a stop/continue choice. Nevertheless, the formal definitions of (Type-I error) ‘safety under OS’ and ‘safety under OC’ only differ in that ‘study-level’ is replaced by ‘data-level’. We may say that combining e-variables E ( m ) E_{(m)} by multiplication is always Type-I error safe under OC. If the e-variable prescription used to construct E ( m ) E_{(m)} has the seqdec property, then the stopping times N ( m ) N_{(m)} used in each study do not need to be specified before the study starts and can even be externally imposed, so that we have Type-I error safety not just under OC but also under OS within each individual study. There is one final subtlety to consider: in the OS setting, with a single stream of data Y 1 , Y 2 , … Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots and conditional e-variables S 1 , S 2 , … S_{1},S_{2},\ldots and test martingale S [ 1 ] , S [ 2 ] , … S^{[1]},S^{[2]},\ldots , the Ville-Robbins inequality ( 7 ) implies that our Type-I error bound α \alpha is guaranteed no matter when we stop — in particular, the actual stopping time does not have to be taken relative to the filtration ( 𝒢 n ) n (\mathcal{G}_{n})_{n} — we may even peek into the future to decide whether to stop now. This suggests that our care in specifying the correct filtrations for the t-test was unnecessary — it seems we can use any stopping rule we like! But this becomes incorrect once we move from OS at the data-level to OC at the study-level: if, in the t-test setting with the plug-in construction of the e -variable E ( m ) E_{(m)} for the m m -th study, we were to set the N ( m ) N_{(m)} so that they are not stopping times relative to ( ℱ ( m − 1 ) ∪ σ ⁡ ( V m , 1 , … , V m , n ) ) n (\mathcal{F}_{(m-1)}\cup\sigma(V_{m,1},\ldots,V_{m,n}))_{n} but only relative to the more refined ( ℱ ( m − 1 ) ∪ σ ⁡ ( Y m , 1 , … , Y m , n ) ) n (\mathcal{F}_{(m-1)}\cup\sigma(Y_{m,1},\ldots,Y_{m,n}))_{n} , we could end up creating fake conditional e -variables at the study-level, i.e. so that 𝐄 P 0 ​ [ E ( m ) ∣ ℱ ( m − 1 ) ] > 1 \mathbf{E}_{P_{0}}[E_{(m)}\mid\mathcal{F}_{(m-1)}]>1 for all m m ( ( Perez et al., 2022 , Appendix B) constructs such a random variable for the t-test). And then the Ville-Robbins inequality may not hold any more at the study-level, and we loose the Type-I error guarantee under optional continuation.

<sub>§Sequentially Decomposable e -Specifications</sub>

### 13. Local vs. Global GRO

Now consider two data streams, Y 1 , 1 , … , Y 1 , n 1 Y_{1,1},\ldots,Y_{1,n_{1}} and Y 2 , 1 , … , Y 2 , n 2 Y_{2,1},\ldots,Y_{2,n_{2}} of fixed lengths n 1 n_{1} and n 2 n_{2} . We may alternatively model these two streams as a single stream Y 1 ′ , … , Y n 1 + n 2 ′ Y^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,Y^{\prime}_{n_{1}+n_{2}} of length n 1 + n 2 n_{1}+n_{2} . If we use an e -variable specification with the seqdec property to generate a study-level test martingale in the sequential way (i.e. not the plug-in way) described above, we will get that, E ( 2 ) = E ( 1 ) ⋅ E ( 2 ) E^{(2)}=E_{(1)}\cdot E_{(2)} constructed for the first two data streams (with E ( 1 ) = ∏ i = 1 n 1 s i ​ ( Y i ) E_{(1)}=\prod_{i=1}^{n_{1}}s_{i}(Y^{i}) and E ( 2 ) = ∏ i = 1 n 2 s n 1 + i ​ ( Y ′ n 1 + i ) E_{(2)}=\prod_{i=1}^{n_{2}}s_{n_{1}+i}(Y^{\prime n_{1}+i}) ) coincides with E ( 1 ) ′ = ∏ i = 1 n 1 + n 2 s [ i ] ​ ( Y i ′ ) E^{\prime}_{(1)}=\prod_{i=1}^{n_{1}+n_{2}}s^{[i]}(Y^{\prime}_{i}) constructed for the single alternative stream. We may thus say that the sequential application of seqdec e -specifications is always coherent : applying the specification sequentially-‘locally’ (separately for both studies) or sequentially-globally (for the concatenated data viewed as one study) gives the same result. For example, the Bayesian W W -GRO specification of Section 2.2 , the GROW specification of Proposition 3 and Example 4 and the GROW and REGROW specifications in the t-test example are all seqdec and hence all have this coherence property when applied sequentially. Sometimes the sequential application of an E-prescription is not feasible or desirable; for example, not all details of previous data may be known. We may then prefer the plug-in application of the E-prescription. Unfortunately, the seqdec property is not sufficient to get coherence for the plug-in method: clearly, if we use the same prior W ( 1 ) W_{(1)} as prior in the Bayesian Example 3 for the first and the second study, this leads to different E ( 2 ) E^{(2)} and E ( 1 ) ′ E^{\prime}_{(1)} , the latter being equivalent to using the posterior of the first study as prior in the second. A sufficient condition for a plug-in application to satisfy coherence after all is that it satisfies both the seqdec property, and further that s i s_{i} , with S i = s i ​ ( Y i ) S_{i}=s_{i}(Y^{i}) as in the definition of seqdec, can be rewritten as s i ​ ( Y i ) = s ′ ​ ( Y i ) s_{i}(Y^{i})=s^{\prime}(Y_{i}) for a single function s ′ s^{\prime} , for all i i . Then in fact the plug-in and the sequential application of the e -prescription will coincide, and coherence is guaranteed. This happens in the subset of our examples in which s [ i ] ​ ( Y i ) s^{[i]}(Y^{i}) takes the form q ⁡ ( Y i ) / p ⁡ ( Y i ) q(Y^{i})/p(Y^{i}) for the same p p and q q , for all i i , as happens e.g. in Example 4 .

<sub>§Local vs. Global GRO</sub>

### 14. An Open Question concerning GRO

In practice we may very well be in a situation in which OS at the data-level is desirable (see the next section for why it would be), so we want to use a seqdec specification, yet the GRO criterion we are interested in does not give one — Example 9 illustrates this for the 2 × 2 2\times 2 case. We may then try the following approach, which for simplicity we only describe for the REGROW criterion: let E n f E^{f}_{n} be the REGROW e -variable ( 19 ) achieving ( 20 ) with f ​ ( θ ) = GRO ​ ( θ ) f(\theta)=\text{GRO}(\theta) for samples of size n n . We try to find a sequence of e -variables E 1 , E 2 , … E_{1},E_{2},\ldots such that E i E_{i} is a σ ⁡ ( Y i − 1 ) \sigma(Y^{i-1}) -conditional e -variable for Y i Y_{i} and the product e -variable E [ n ] := ∏ i = 1 n E i E^{[n]}:=\prod_{i=1}^{n}E_{i} achieves ( 20 ) to within some fixed ϵ \epsilon for all n n larger than some minimal n 0 n_{0} , i.e. By construction, the sequence E [ 1 ] , E [ 2 ] , … E^{[1]},E^{[2]},\ldots is seqdec and allows for optional stopping, and if we can find E 1 , E 2 , … E_{1},E_{2},\ldots such that ϵ \epsilon is small for all n n larger than or equal to the n 0 n_{0} corresponding to the smallest sample we’d ever be interested in analyzing, we can say that the full process (and not just an instance at a fixed n n ) is ‘almost’ REGROW in the desired sense. The example raises an important question: under what conditions (on model, minimal batch sizes and the like) can we create a seqdec specification that behaves optimally for our desired GRO criterion (as in Section 2.2 with W W -GRO, and in Example 4 , with the GROW criterion) or almost optimally (as in the 2 × 2 2\times 2 example above with batch size 2, with the REGROW criterion)?

<sub>§An Open Question concerning GRO</sub>

## 总结与展望
Safe testing 主张用「安全检验」（e-value 与测试鞅）取代 p 值，好处是可以一直看数据、随时决定停、还可以把证据累积起来。它对本方向的启示是：证据应该是可累积的量，而不是一次性的判决。

**It connects to**

- `2210.01948` — 同一理论的两个入口，一个走游戏论一个走贝叶斯。
- `2009.03167` — 那篇证明非负性来自一个非负鞅，是同一理论的基石。

## 不足之处
**The authors say:** In our examples, we have used (and will keep using in the next section) the REGROW approach to first eliminate nuisance parameters, if they are present, followed by a GROW approach for the parameters of interest. ( Turner et al., 2021 , ter Schure et al., 2021 ) find that this gives intuitive e -variables that perform well in practical applications, not just directly in the GRO sense but also in terms of secondary measures such as a power analysis (Section 6 ) or as the basis of anytime-valid confidence intervals ( Turner et al., 2021 ) . Still, it may not always be the best way to go. For example, a REGROW approach for the parameter of interest when a minimum effect size is given may sometimes be sensible as well. Let us consider this a bit further for (for simplicity) the case with a minimum effect size δ + \delta^{+} but without nuisance parameters, as in Example 4 . REGROW would amount here to using E W 1 ∗ = p W 1 ​ ( 𝐘 ) / p δ − ​ ( 𝐘 ) E^{*}_{W_{1}}=p_{W_{1}}(\mathbf{Y})/p_{\delta^{-}}(\mathbf{Y}) with some prior W 1 W_{1} spread out on the set Θ 1 = { δ : δ ≥ δ + } \Theta_{1}=\{\delta:\delta\geq\delta^{+}\} . Then we would get 𝐄 P δ ​ [ log ⁡ E W 1 ∗ ] < GROW ​ ( Θ 1 ) ​ for δ close to δ + ; 𝐄 P δ ​ [ log ⁡ E W 1 ∗ ] ≫ GROW ​ ( Θ 1 ) ​ for δ ≫ δ + \mathbf{E}_{P_{\delta}}[\log E^{*}_{W_{1}}]<\text{GROW}(\Theta_{1})\text{\ for $\delta$ close to $\delta^{+}$\ };\mathbf{E}_{P_{\delta}}[\log E^{*}_{W_{1}}]\gg\text{GROW}(\Theta_{1})\text{\ for $\delta\gg\delta^{+}$\ } so we would win if we are ‘lucky’ and it turns out that δ ≫ δ + \delta\gg\delta^{+} . However, in practice we often deal with small sample sizes, and δ \delta ’s that may very well be very close to δ + \delta^{+} . Then (as our experiments done for the papers above indicate) the difference in ‘ < < ’ above is non-negligible, and the GROW approach seems safer, since for the GROW e -variable we automatically have 𝐄 P δ ​ [ log ⁡ E W 1 ∗ ] ≥ GROW ​ ( Θ 1 ) \mathbf{E}_{P_{\delta}}[\log E^{*}_{W_{1}}]\geq\text{GROW}(\Theta_{1}) for all δ ∈ Θ 1 \delta\in\Theta_{1} .

**What a reader should still not conclude**

- 贝叶斯安全检验依赖先验，我们的 α 与 λ 没有先验依据。

# Self-Consistency Improves Chain of Thought Reasoning in Language Models

arXiv:2203.11171 · 2022 · [source](https://arxiv.org/html/2203.11171)

> Written by an agent that read the body (DeepSeek Harness, 2026-11-24) after reading the paper; the mechanism quotes are the paper’s own words.

## 出发点
Although language models have demonstrated remarkable success across a range of NLP tasks, their ability to demonstrate reasoning is often seen as a limitation, which cannot be overcome solely by increasing model scale ( Rae et al., 2021 ; BIG-bench collaboration, 2021 , inter alia ) . In an effort to address this shortcoming, Wei et al. (2022) have proposed chain-of-thought prompting , where a language model is prompted to generate a series of short sentences that mimic the reasoning process a person might employ in solving a task. For example, given the question “If there are 3 cars in the parking lot and 2 more cars arrive, how many cars are in the parking lot?” , instead of directly responding with “5” , a language model would be prompted to respond with the entire chain-of-thought: “There are 3 cars in the parking lot already. 2 more arrive. Now there are 3 + 2 = 5 cars. The answer is 5.” . It has been observed that chain-of-thought prompting significantly improves model performance across a variety of multi-step reasoning tasks ( Wei et al., 2022 ) .

<sub>§1 Introduction</sub>

## Pipeline
```mermaid
flowchart LR
  N0["§2 Self-Consistency over Diverse Reasoning Paths"]
```

![The self-consistency method contains three steps: (1) prompt a language model using chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting; (2) replace the “greedy decode” in CoT prompting by sampling from the language model’s decoder to generate a diverse set of reasoning paths; and (3) marginalize out the reasoning paths and aggregate by choosing the most consistent answer in the final answer set.](https://arxiv.org/html/2203.11171v4/self_consistency_fig.svg)

*The self-consistency method contains three steps: (1) prompt a language model using chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting; (2) replace the “greedy decode” in CoT prompting by sampling from the language model’s decoder to generate a diverse set of reasoning paths; and (3) marginalize out the reasoning paths and aggregate by choosing the most consistent answer in the final answer set.*

<sub>figure: `2203.11171v4/self_consistency_fig.svg`</sub>

## 机制
### 1. Self-Consistency over Diverse Reasoning Paths

A salient aspect of humanity is that people think differently. It is natural to suppose that in tasks requiring deliberate thinking, there are likely several ways to attack the problem. We propose that such a process can be simulated in language models via sampling from the language model’s decoder. For instance, as shown in Figure 1 , a model can generate several plausible responses to a math question that all arrive at the same correct answer (Outputs 1 and 3). Since language models are not perfect reasoners, the model might also produce an incorrect reasoning path or make a mistake in one of the reasoning steps (e.g., in Output 2), but such solutions are less likely to arrive at the same answer. That is, we hypothesize that correct reasoning processes, even if they are diverse, tend to have greater agreement in their final answer than incorrect processes. We leverage this intuition by proposing the following self-consistency method. First, a language model is prompted with a set of manually written chain-of-thought exemplars ( Wei et al., 2022 ) . Next, we sample a set of candidate outputs from the language model’s decoder, generating a diverse set of candidate reasoning paths. Self-consistency is compatible with most existing sampling algorithms, including temperature sampling ( Ackley et al., 1985 ; Ficler & Goldberg, 2017 ) , top- k k sampling ( Fan et al., 2018 ; Holtzman et al., 2018 ; Radford et al., 2019 ) , and nucleus sampling ( Holtzman et al., 2020 ) . Finally, we aggregate the answers by marginalizing out the sampled reasoning paths and choosing the answer that is the most consistent among the generated answers. In more detail, assume the generated answers 𝐚 i \mathbf{a}_{i} are from a fixed answer set, 𝐚 i ∈ 𝔸 \mathbf{a}_{i}\in\mathbb{A} , where i = 1 , … , m i=1,\ldots,m indexes the m m candidate outputs sampled from the decoder. Given a prompt and a question, self-consistency introduces an additional latent variable 𝐫 i \mathbf{r}_{i} , which is a sequence of tokens representing the reasoning path in the i i -th output, then couples the generation of ( 𝐫 i , 𝐚 i ) (\mathbf{r}_{i},\mathbf{a}_{i}) where 𝐫 i → 𝐚 i \mathbf{r}_{i}\rightarrow\mathbf{a}_{i} , i.e., generating a reasoning path 𝐫 i \mathbf{r}_{i} is optional and only used to reach the final answer 𝐚 i \mathbf{a}_{i} . As an example, consider Output 3 from Figure 1 : the first few sentences “ She eats 3 for breakfast … So she has 9 eggs * $2 = $18. ” constitutes 𝐫 i \mathbf{r}_{i} , while the answer 18 from the last sentence, “ The answer is $18 ”, is parsed as 𝐚 i \mathbf{a}_{i} . 1 1 1 The parser is task dependent. For arithmetic reasoning, we parse the first numerical part as the final answer after the model generates “The answer is ”. For commonsense reasoning, we parse the full string answer as the final answer after the model generates “The answer is ”. Most generated outputs have a consistent format of “{Reasoning paths}. The answer is X.” if we prompt the language model in this format. After sampling multiple ( 𝐫 i , 𝐚 i ) (\mathbf{r}_{i},\mathbf{a}_{i}) from the model’s decoder, self-consistency applies a marginalization over 𝐫 i \mathbf{r}_{i} by taking a majority vote over 𝐚 i \mathbf{a}_{i} , i.e., arg ​ max a ∑ i = 1 m 𝟙 ( 𝐚 i = a ) \argmax_{a}\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{m}\mathbbm{1}(\mathbf{a}_{i}=a) , or as we defined as the most “consistent” answer among the final answer set. In Table 1 , we show the test accuracy over a set of reasoning tasks by using different answer aggregation strategies. In addition to majority vote, one can also weight each ( 𝐫 i , 𝐚 i ) (\mathbf{r}_{i},\mathbf{a}_{i}) by P ( 𝐫 i , 𝐚 i ∣ prompt , question ) P(\mathbf{r}_{i},\mathbf{a}_{i}\mid\text{prompt},\text{question}) when aggregating the answers. Note to compute P ( 𝐫 i , 𝐚 i ∣ prompt , question ) P(\mathbf{r}_{i},\mathbf{a}_{i}\mid\text{prompt},\text{question}) , we can either take the unnormalized probability of the model generating ( 𝐫 i , 𝐚 i ) (\mathbf{r}_{i},\mathbf{a}_{i}) given ( prompt , question ) (\text{prompt},\text{question}) , or we can normalize the conditional probability by the output length ( Brown et al., 2020 ) , i.e., where log ⁡ P ⁡ ( t k ∣ prompt , question , t 1 , … , t k − 1 ) \log P(t_{k}\mid\text{prompt},\text{question},t_{1},\ldots,t_{k-1}) is the log probability of generating the k k -th token t k t_{k} in ( 𝐫 i , 𝐚 i ) (\mathbf{r}_{i},\mathbf{a}_{i}) conditioned on the previous tokens, and K K is the total number of tokens in ( 𝐫 i , 𝐚 i ) (\mathbf{r}_{i},\mathbf{a}_{i}) . In Table 1 , we show that taking the “unweighted sum”, i.e., taking a majority vote directly over 𝐚 i \mathbf{a}_{i} yields a very similar accuracy as aggregating using the “normalized weighted sum”. We took a closer look at the model’s output probabilities and found this is because for each ( 𝐫 i , 𝐚 i ) (\mathbf{r}_{i},\mathbf{a}_{i}) , the normalized conditional probabilities P ( 𝐫 i , 𝐚 i ∣ prompt , question ) P(\mathbf{r}_{i},\mathbf{a}_{i}\mid\text{prompt},\text{question}) are quite close to each other, i.e., the language model regards those generations as ‘‘similarly likely’’. 2 2 2 This also means that the language model is not well calibrated and thus cannot distinguish well between correct solutions and wrong solutions, which also explains why additional re-rankers were trained to better judge the quality of the solutions in previous work ( Cobbe et al., 2021 ; Thoppilan et al., 2022 ) . Additionally, when aggregating the answers, the results in Table 1 show that the “normalized” weighted sum (i.e., Equation 1 ) yields a much higher accuracy compared to its unnormalized counterpart. For completeness, in Table 1 we also report the results by taking a “weighted average”, i.e., each a a gets a score of its weighted sum divided by ∑ i = 1 m 𝟙 ​ ( 𝐚 i = a ) \sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{m}\mathbbm{1}(\mathbf{a}_{i}=a) , which results in a much worse performance. Self-consistency explores an interesting space between open-ended text generation and optimal text generation with a fixed answer. Reasoning tasks typically have fixed answers, which is why researchers have generally considered greedy decoding approaches ( Radford et al., 2019 ; Wei et al., 2022 ; Chowdhery et al., 2022 ) . However, we have found that even when the desired answer is fixed, introducing diversity in the reasoning processes can be highly beneficial; therefore we leverage sampling, as commonly used for open-ended text generation ( Radford et al., 2019 ; Brown et al., 2020 ; Thoppilan et al., 2022 ) , to achieve this goal. One should note that self-consistency can be applied only to problems where the final answer is from a fixed answer set, but in principle this approach can be extended to open-text generation problems if a good metric of consistency can be defined between multiple generations, e.g., whether two answers agree or contradict each other.

<sub>§2 Self-Consistency over Diverse Reasoning Paths</sub>

## 总结与展望
自一致性用多数投票把多次采样合成一个答案，是「重复换稳定」的最朴素做法。它在预算方向的含义是：重复的收益随次数递减，而边际收益从未被显式建模。

**It connects to**

- `2110.14168` — 一个用验证器挑，一个用投票合，都需要多次采样。

## 不足之处
**The authors say:** We introduced a simple yet effective method called self-consistency, and observed that it significantly improves accuracy in a range of arithmetic and commonsense reasoning tasks, across four large language models with varying scales. Beyond accuracy gains, self-consistency is also useful for collecting rationales when performing reasoning tasks with language models, and for providing uncertainty estimates and improved calibration of language model outputs.

**What a reader should still not conclude**

- 投票假设错误是独立的，这个假设在同一个模型上通常不成立。

## 机制
### 1. Validity: E-values under the null hypothesis / Testing using Markov’s inequality / Testing using distributional knowledge

In this chapter, we study fundamental issues of e-values, in particular, on their connection to p-values, validity, and tests. These properties concern the behavior of the e-variables under the null hypothesis 𝒫 \mathcal{P} . For this reason, the alternative hypothesis 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} does not appear and plays no role. Let E E be an e-variable for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} . We have ℙ ⁡ ( E ≥ 1 / α ) ≤ α \mathbb{P}(E\geq 1/\alpha)\leq\alpha for all ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} and α ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] \alpha\in(0,1] . Hence, 1 / E 1/E is a p-variable, ( α ​ E ) ∧ 1 (\alpha E)\wedge 1 is a level- α \alpha test, and 𝟙 { E ≥ 1 / α } \mathds{1}_{\{E\geq 1/\alpha\}} is a level- α \alpha binary test. The function f : [ 0 , ∞ ] → [ 0 , 1 ] f:[0,\infty]\to[0,1] defined by f ⁡ ( t ) = min ⁡ ( 1 , 1 / t ) f(t)=\min(1,1/t) is an e-to-p calibrator. It dominates every other e-to-p calibrator. In particular, it is the only admissible e-to-p calibrator. A decreasing function f : [ 0 , ∞ ) → [ 0 , ∞ ] f:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty] with f = 0 f=0 on ( 1 , ∞ ) (1,\infty) is a p-to-e calibrator if and only if ∫ 0 1 f ⁡ ( p ) ​ 𝑑 p ≤ 1 \int_{0}^{1}f(p)\mathrm{d}p\leq 1 . It is admissible if and only if f f is upper semicontinuous, f ⁡ ( 0 ) = ∞ f(0)=\infty , and ∫ 0 1 f ⁡ ( p ) ​ 𝑑 p = 1 \int_{0}^{1}f(p)\mathrm{d}p=1 . If f f is a calibrator or admissible calibrator, then so is the mapping x ↦ a ​ f ​ ( a ​ x ) x\mapsto af(ax) for any a ≥ 1 a\geq 1 . Let E E be an e-variable for an atomless probability measure ℙ \mathbb{P} . Then, there exists a calibrator f f and an exact p-variable P P for ℙ \mathbb{P} such that E = f ⁡ ( P ) E=f(P) , ℙ \mathbb{P} -almost surely. Moreover, if E E is exact, then we can require the calibrator f f to be admissible. In Section 1.6 , for testing ℙ = N ⁡ ( 0 , 1 ) \mathbb{P}=\mathrm{N}(0,1) against ℚ = N ⁡ ( μ , 1 ) \mathbb{Q}=\mathrm{N}(\mu,1) with n n observations, the likelihood ratio e-value in ( 1.2 ) is E = exp ⁡ ( δ ​ Z − δ 2 / 2 ) , E=\exp\left(\delta Z-\delta^{2}/2\right), and the Neyman–Pearson p-variable is P = Φ ⁡ ( − Z ) , P=\Phi(-Z), where Z Z is N ⁡ ( 0 , 1 ) \mathrm{N}(0,1) -distributed, Φ \Phi is the standard normal cdf, and δ = μ ​ n \delta=\mu\sqrt{n} . Both E E and P P are exact. It is straightforward to see E = f ⁡ ( P ) E=f(P) with the admissible calibrator f f given by Fix ℙ ∈ ℳ 1 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{M}_{1} . Let Y Y be a nonnegative random variable and U ∼ d U ⁡ [ 0 , 1 ] U\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\sim}\limits^{\mathrm{d}}}\mathrm{U}[0,1] be independent of Y Y . Then, for any α > 0 \alpha>0 , In particular, if Y Y is an e-variable for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} , then ℙ ⁡ ( Y ≥ U / α ) ≤ α \mathbb{P}(Y\geq U/\alpha)\leq\alpha for any ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} . For any hypothesis 𝒫 \mathcal{P} , if a p-variable P P and an e-variable E E are independent, then P / E P/E is a p-variable. In particular, if E E is an e-variable, then U / E U/E is a p-variable, where U ∼ d U ⁡ [ 0 , 1 ] U\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\sim}\limits^{\mathrm{d}}}\mathrm{U}[0,1] is independent of E E . An e-variable E E for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} is admissible if for any other e-variable E ′ E^{\prime} for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} satisfying E ′ ≥ E E^{\prime}\geq E , we have ℙ ⁡ ( E ′ = E ) = 1 \mathbb{P}(E^{\prime}=E)=1 for each ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} . Then, E E is an admissible e-variable for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} if and only if sup ℙ ∈ 𝒫 A 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ E ] = 1 \sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}}\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[E]=1 for all A ∈ ℱ A\in\mathcal{F} with 𝒫 A ≠ ∅ \mathcal{P}_{A}\neq\varnothing . Let E E and E ′ E^{\prime} be strongly admissible e-variables for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} and 𝒫 ′ \mathcal{P}^{\prime} , respectively. Then E × E ′ E\times E^{\prime} is a strongly admissible e-variable for 𝒫 × 𝒫 ′ \mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{P}^{\prime} . Under the monotone likelihood ratio assumption, for any θ ′ > θ 0 \theta^{\prime}>\theta_{0} , the random variable As mentioned before, e-values can be interpreted in their own right as evidence against the null hypothesis without resort to other concepts. However, the reader may naturally be curious how exactly e-values may be transformed (perhaps with some loss of efficiency) into the more classical concepts of level- α \alpha tests and p-values. It is in this context that a central role will be played by Markov’s inequality (though it apparently was discovered by Chebyshev, Markov’s advisor). Markov’s inequality guarantees that a test rejecting an e-value larger than 1 / α 1/\alpha yields a level- α \alpha test. The proof follows directly from the simple inequality 𝟙 { x ≥ 1 } ≤ x \mathds{1}_{\{x\geq 1\}}\leq x for all x ≥ 0 x\geq 0 , by observing that For an e-variable E E and a p-variable P P , even though 1 / E 1/E is a p-variable, 1 / P 1/P is generally not an e-variable; in fact 1 / P 1/P has infinite mean if P P is exact. The p-variable 1 / E 1/E is conservative since it cannot be uniformly distributed on [ 0 , 1 ] [0,1] , and it is typically far away from being so. As a consequence, the type-I error of the test 𝟙 { E ≥ 1 / α } \mathds{1}_{\{E\geq 1/\alpha\}} for ℙ \mathbb{P} is usually smaller than α \alpha unless E E is distributed on the two points 0 0 and 1 / α 1/\alpha with mean 1 1 under ℙ \mathbb{P} . Proposition 2.1 can be generalized in several different directions relevant for testing with e-values; some are presented in Section 2.4 and Chapters 5 and 15 . A sequential generalization, called Ville’s inequality, is presented in Chapter 7 . With a p-variable P P and an e-variable E E for the same hypothesis, the tests 𝟙 { E ≥ 1 / α } \mathds{1}_{\{E\geq 1/\alpha\}} and 𝟙 { P ≤ α } \mathds{1}_{\{P\leq\alpha\}} are in general different, except for the special case E = 𝟙 { P ≤ α } / α E=\mathds{1}_{\{P\leq\alpha\}}/\alpha (see Section 3.1 for more details on such e-variables). To understand how these two tests differ, we consider the two-sided normal test in Section 1.6 , where a family of e-variables E n E_{n} is given by ( 1.3 ), indexed by δ > 0 \delta>0 , and the p-variable P n P_{n} is given by ( 1.4 ). We treat both the e-variables and the p-variable as functions of the value x x taken by the statistic Z Z . Figure 2.2 illustrates two intervals [ A 1 , A 2 ] [A_{1},A_{2}] and [ B 1 , B 2 ] [B_{1},B_{2}] , which correspond to P n ​ ( x ) = 0.01 P_{n}(x)=0.01 for x = A 1 x=A_{1} or x = A 2 x=A_{2} and E n ​ ( x ) = 100 E_{n}(x)=100 for x = B 1 x=B_{1} or x = B 2 x=B_{2} , with δ = 3 \delta=3 . Here, A 2 = | A 1 | ≈ 2.58 A_{2}=|A_{1}|\approx 2.58 and B 2 = | B 1 | ≈ 3.27 B_{2}=|B_{1}|\approx 3.27 . Other choices of δ \delta lead to different intervals, as shown in Figure 2.2 . We can see that it is harder for an e-value to reach 100 100 compared to a p-value to reach 0.01 0.01 , and this is because the Markov inequality is generally conservative. It is generally unfair to directly compare the values of E E and 1 / P 1/P . See Section 2.7 for more discussions on this point. Proposition 2.1 implies that for an e-variable E E and α ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \alpha\in(0,1) , we can always reject the null hypothesis by using the level- α \alpha test 𝟙 { E ≥ 1 / α } \mathds{1}_{\{E\geq 1/\alpha\}} . If the distribution of E E under the null hypothesis is known or computable (there may be multiple distributions if the null is composite), then we can find thresholds that are smaller than 1 / α 1/\alpha to test based on E E . Denote by S ⁡ ( ⋅ , ℙ ) S(\cdot;\mathbb{P}) the left-continuous survival function of E E under ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} , that is, for x ∈ ℝ . x\in\mathbb{R}. If S ⁡ ( ⋅ , ℙ ) S(\cdot;\mathbb{P}) is the same across ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} , then E E is called pivotal (this will appear again in Section 14.3 ). The function S ⁡ ( ⋅ , ℙ ) S(\cdot;\mathbb{P}) can be continuous or discontinuous, depending on both E E and ℙ \mathbb{P} . For a threshold t > 0 t>0 , we would like the test 𝟙 { E ≥ t } \mathds{1}_{\{E\geq t\}} to have level α \alpha . Clearly, choosing t ≥ 1 / α t\geq 1/\alpha is sufficient, but we can do better. Indeed, by definition, as long as sup ℙ ∈ 𝒫 S ⁡ ( t | ℙ ) ≤ α \sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}}S(t|\mathbb{P})\leq\alpha , t t is a valid threshold for the test 𝟙 { E ≥ t } \mathds{1}_{\{E\geq t\}} with level α \alpha . If sup ℙ ∈ 𝒫 S ⁡ ( t | ℙ ) \sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}}S(t|\mathbb{P}) is continuous, then the smallest t t that satisfies this condition is which is also equal to the largest left α \alpha -quantile of E E over ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} (Lemma 15.1 in Section 15.1 ). In particular, if 𝒫 = { ℙ } \mathcal{P}=\{\mathbb{P}\} is simple, then we can easily see that which is the left α \alpha -quantile of E E under ℙ \mathbb{P} . The level- α \alpha guarantee of 𝟙 { E ≥ t ∗ } \mathds{1}_{\{E\geq t^{*}\}} requires t ↦ sup ℙ ∈ 𝒫 S ⁡ ( t | ℙ ) t\mapsto\sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}}S(t|\mathbb{P}) to be continuous at t ∗ t^{*} ; otherwise we can use the test 𝟙 { E > t ∗ } \mathds{1}_{\{E>t^{*}\}} , or a suitable randomization between 𝟙 { E > t ∗ } \mathds{1}_{\{E>t^{*}\}} and 𝟙 { E ≥ t ∗ } \mathds{1}_{\{E\geq t^{*}\}} (analogously to the Neyman–Pearson lemma). Reducing the threshold in the above manner is in fact no different from treating the e-value as any other test statistic and picking the threshold appropriately to derive a level- α \alpha test, as is standard in classical statistics. Even in situations where S ⁡ ( ⋅ , ℙ ) S(\cdot;\mathbb{P}) is difficult to fully specify or compute, bounds on t ∗ t^{*} may be obtained based on partial information of the distribution of the e-value. Several concrete cases will be studied in Chapter 15 .

### 2. Relating p-values and e-values using calibrators / Randomized tests and calibrators / Admissible e-variables / Admissible e-variables for a simple null are likelihood ratios / Characterization of admissibility

P-values and e-values can be converted between each other, via calibrators. Below, when we say “any hypothesis”, we mean any specification of the sample space ( Ω , ℱ ) (\Omega,\mathcal{F}) and hypothesis 𝒫 \mathcal{P} on the space. A p-to-e calibrator is a decreasing function f : [ 0 , ∞ ) → [ 0 , ∞ ] f:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty] satisfying f = 0 f=0 on ( 1 , ∞ ) (1,\infty) such that for any hypothesis, f ⁡ ( P ) f(P) is an e-variable for any p-variable P P . An e-to-p calibrator is a decreasing function g : [ 0 , ∞ ] → [ 0 , ∞ ) g:[0,\infty]\to[0,\infty) such that for any hypothesis, g ⁡ ( E ) g(E) is a p-variable for any e-variable E E . A calibrator f f is said to dominate a calibrator g g if f ≥ g f\geq g (p-to-e) or f ≤ g f\leq g (e-to-p), and the domination is strict if further f ≠ g f\neq g . A calibrator is admissible if it is not strictly dominated by any other calibrator. We often omit “p-to-e” in “p-to-e calibrators” and simply call them calibrators, for a reason that will be made clear soon in Propositions 2.4 and 2.5 . The interval [ 0 , ∞ ) [0,\infty) can be safely replaced by [ 0 , 1 ] [0,1] in the above definitions since p-values with values larger than 1 1 are not interesting for testing. A probability measure ℙ \mathbb{P} (or the corresponding probability space) is called atomless if there exists a continuously distributed random variable (e.g., uniform, normal) under ℙ \mathbb{P} . Hence, when we encounter a continuously distributed random variable, implicitly the probability measure is assumed atomless. In Definition 2.3 , the property of calibrators needs to hold for any hypothesis. Nevertheless, it suffices to consider one atomless probability measure ℙ \mathbb{P} on some ( Ω , ℱ ) (\Omega,\mathcal{F}) , because if for the simple hypothesis ℙ \mathbb{P} , f ⁡ ( P ) f(P) is an e-variable for any p-variable P P , then it holds also for any other, simple or composite, hypotheses. This observation will be useful in several places throughout. A formal justification of this simplification is presented in Appendix A.1 . In the next two results we characterize all admissible calibrators in both directions. We first look at the simpler direction. The following proposition says that there is, essentially, only one e-to-p calibrator, f : t ↦ min ⁡ ( 1 , 1 / t ) f:t\mapsto\min(1,1/t) , that is offered by Markov’s inequality. Instead of working with the set of all e-variables, we can also consider conditional e-to-p calibrators that are valid only on some subset ℰ \mathcal{E} of e-variables, which is a weaker requirement, and such calibrators depend on the choice of ℰ \mathcal{E} . This topic is treated in Chapter 15 . In sharp contrast to the uniqueness of the admissible e-to-p calibrator, the class of p-to-e calibrators is much richer. In the context of this proposition, being upper semicontinuous is equivalent to being left-continuous. Below we present some simple examples of admissible p-to-e calibrators. We only specify them on ( 0 , 1 ] (0,1] , as they are ∞ \infty at 0 0 and 0 0 on ( 1 , ∞ ) (1,\infty) . These calibrators include the power form Converting a p-value to an e-value using a p-to-e calibrator and then back to p-value using an e-to-p calibrator generally loses quite a lot of evidence. For instance, starting with p = 0.01 p=0.01 , a conversion with the p-to-e calibrator p ↦ p − 1 / 2 − 1 p\mapsto p^{-1/2}-1 gives e = 9 e=9 , and another conversion with the e-to-p calibrator e ↦ min ⁡ ( 1 / e , 1 ) e\mapsto\min(1/e,1) yields p ′ = 1 / 9 p^{\prime}=1/9 . Therefore, it is not recommended in practice to calibrate iteratively in both directions. A possible interpretation of the above results on calibrators is that e-variables and p-variables are connected via a very rough relation 1 / e ∼ p 1/e\sim p . In one direction, the statement is precise: the reciprocal (truncated to 1 if needed) of an e-variable is a p-variable by Proposition 2.4 . On the other hand, using a calibrator f ⁡ ( p ) = κ ​ p κ − 1 f(p)=\kappa p^{\kappa-1} in ( 2.1 ) with a small κ > 0 \kappa>0 and ignoring positive constant factors, we can see that the reciprocal of a p-variable is approximately an e-variable; this is also the case of ( 2.2 ). In fact, f ⁡ ( p ) ≤ 1 / p f(p)\leq 1/p for all p p when f f is a calibrator; otherwise ∫ 0 1 f ⁡ ( q ) ​ 𝑑 q ≥ p ​ f ​ ( p ) > 1 \int_{0}^{1}f(q)\mathrm{d}q\geq pf(p)>1 , conflicting the condition in Proposition 2.5 . However, f ⁡ ( p ) = 1 / p f(p)=1/p for a fixed p p is only possible in the extreme case f : q ↦ 𝟙 { q ∈ [ 0 , p ] } / p f:q\mapsto\mathds{1}_{\{q\in[0,p]\}}/p . In the rest of the book, we omit “p-to-e” when mentioning calibrators, unless we want to stress the direction. Although all calibrators in ( 2.1 )–( 2.5 ) are admissible, some are more useful. In hypothesis testing, very small p-values and very large e-values are interesting. Calibrators in ( 2.3 ), ( 2.4 ) and ( 2.5 ) penalize very small p-values more than ( 2.2 ), which can be seen from their asymptotic behaviour as p ↓ 0 p\downarrow 0 , and therefore they are less likely to produce very large e-values; the calibrator in ( 2.3 ) is even bounded by 2 2 for p ≠ 0 p\neq 0 . Nevertheless, they may be useful when we take the product of many independent e-values. We end this section with a simple property of admissible calibrators. It also explains why we choose the domain of the calibrators as [ 0 , ∞ ) [0,\infty) instead of [ 0 , 1 ] [0,1] , although its value is set to 0 0 for input p-values larger than 1 1 . This result will be useful for later results in Chapter 12 . The next result states that any e-variable E E for an atomless probability measure ℙ \mathbb{P} can be seen as some calibrator applied to an exact p-variable. Indeed, as we see from the proof of the result below, this calibrator is a transform of the quantile function of E E . A randomized test is a test that utilizes independent external randomization. It is possible to improve Markov’s inequality using external randomization, and this has direct implications for converting e-variables into tests or p-variables. The proof is simple. Since U U is uniform and independent of Y Y , we get yielding the claim. The first equality in ( 2.6 ) becomes an inequality ≤ \leq if U U is stochastically larger than standard uniform, and in particular U = 1 U=1 yields an alternative proof of Markov’s inequality. Also, if Y Y is bounded, that is Y ∈ [ 0 , C ] Y\in[0,C] almost surely for some C > 0 C>0 , then the inequality in ( 2.6 ) holds with equality for any α ≤ 1 / C \alpha\leq 1/C . In contrast, Markov’s inequality holds with equality only for distributions supported on { 0 , 1 / α } \{0,1/\alpha\} . The following corollary is a direct consequence of Theorem 2.9 , which offers additional interpretations of the relation between p-variables, e-variables, and randomization. Since U < 1 U<1 almost surely, U / E U/E is a strictly smaller p-value than 1 / E 1/E in all but degenerate cases. Thus, the corresponding randomized test is typically strictly more powerful than the non-randomized one. Said differently, e ↦ ( U / e ) ∧ 1 e\mapsto(U/e)\wedge 1 can be seen as a randomized e-to-p calibrator . In fact, this randomized e-to-p calibrator is admissible (treated in Section 11.3 ) and it dominates e ↦ ( 1 / e ) ∧ 1 e\mapsto(1/e)\wedge 1 , the latter only being admissible among non-randomized e-to-p calibrators (Proposition 2.4 ). A more general treatment of combining a p-value and an e-value is presented in Section 11.3 . In this section, we consider admissible e-variables, which are, intuitively, e-variables that cannot be enlarged, formally defined below. Equivalently, we may say that an e-variable is admissible if it is not dominated by another e-variable, where by domination of E ′ E^{\prime} over E E , we mean E ′ ≥ E E^{\prime}\geq E and ℙ ⁡ ( E ′ > E ) > 0 \mathbb{P}(E^{\prime}>E)>0 for some ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} . A trivial example is that the constant 1 1 is an admissible e-variable for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} . The above definition does not use any alternative ℚ \mathbb{Q} , but if all ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} and ℚ \mathbb{Q} are mutually absolutely continuous, then E E being admissible means that one cannot find another e-variable E ′ E^{\prime} such that ℚ ⁡ ( E ′ ≥ E ) = 1 \mathbb{Q}(E^{\prime}\geq E)=1 and ℚ ⁡ ( E ′ > E ) > 0 \mathbb{Q}(E^{\prime}>E)>0 . Indeed, if ℚ ⁡ ( E ′ ≥ E ) = 1 \mathbb{Q}(E^{\prime}\geq E)=1 and ℚ ⁡ ( E ′ > E ) > 0 \mathbb{Q}(E^{\prime}>E)>0 hold for an e-variable E ′ E^{\prime} , then E ′ ∨ E E^{\prime}\vee E is an e-variable that dominates E E . for each A ∈ ℱ A\in\mathcal{F} with 𝒫 A ≠ ∅ \mathcal{P}_{A}\neq\varnothing , there exists ℙ ∈ 𝒫 A \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{A} such that 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ E ] = 1 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[E]=1 ; for each ℙ ′ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P} , there exists ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} such that ℙ ′ ≪ ℙ \mathbb{P}^{\prime}\ll\mathbb{P} and 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ E ] = 1 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[E]=1 ; We first make a straightforward but important observation: For simple nulls, every admissible e-variable can be written as a likelihood ratio. We now characterize admissible e-variables for general 𝒫 \mathcal{P} . For A ∈ ℱ A\in\mathcal{F} and δ > 0 \delta>0 , let 𝒫 A δ = { ℙ ∈ 𝒫 : ℙ ⁡ ( A ) > δ } \mathcal{P}^{\delta}_{A}=\{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}:\mathbb{P}(A)>\delta\} and 𝒫 A = { ℙ ∈ 𝒫 : ℙ ⁡ ( A ) > 0 } \mathcal{P}_{A}=\{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}:\mathbb{P}(A)>0\} . The first result gives a simple necessary condition for admissible e-variables, which boils down to the exactness condition in Proposition 2.12 when 𝒫 = { ℙ } . \mathcal{P}=\{\mathbb{P}\}. Then next result gives a full characterization of admissible e-variables using its expectations under ℙ ∈ 𝒫 A δ \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}^{\delta} for A ∈ ℱ A\in\mathcal{F} . In this result, we use the convention inf ∅ = 0 \inf\varnothing=0 . Several sufficient conditions for ( 2.8 ) are easier to check, summarized in the next corollary. The condition (a) in Corollary 2.15 deserves some further attention. We will say that an e-variable is strongly admissible if condition (a) in Corollary 2.15 holds; note that (a) is implied by either of (b) and (c). This condition is strictly stronger than admissibility for infinite 𝒫 \mathcal{P} as characterized in Theorem 2.14 (for which there need not be any ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} with 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ E ] = 1 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[E]=1 ). When 𝒫 \mathcal{P} is finite, strong admissibility is equivalent to admissibility, as shown in the next result.

### 3. Admissible e-variables on product spaces / More examples of e-values / Monotone likelihood ratio / Testing symmetry

Next, we consider e-variables defined on product spaces. Let 𝒫 ′ \mathcal{P}^{\prime} be a set of probabilities on another measurable space ( Ω ′ , ℱ ′ ) (\Omega^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime}) . We write 𝒫 × 𝒫 ′ \mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{P}^{\prime} for the set { ℙ × ℙ ′ : ℙ ∈ 𝒫 , ℙ ′ ∈ 𝒫 ′ } \{\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{P}^{\prime}:\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P},~\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\} of product probabilities. For E E on Ω \Omega and E ′ E^{\prime} on Ω ′ \Omega^{\prime} , let E × E ′ E\times E^{\prime} be defined by ( ω , ω ′ ) ↦ E ⁡ ( ω ) ​ E ′ ​ ( ω ′ ) (\omega,\omega^{\prime})\mapsto E(\omega)E^{\prime}(\omega^{\prime}) . Clearly, if E E is an e-variable for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} and E ′ E^{\prime} is an e-variable for 𝒫 ′ \mathcal{P}^{\prime} , then E × E ′ E\times E^{\prime} is an e-variable for 𝒫 × 𝒫 ′ \mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{P}^{\prime} , because for all ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} and ℙ ′ ∈ 𝒫 ′ \mathbb{P}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime} . The next result further shows that E × E ′ E\times E^{\prime} is strongly admissible when E E and E ′ E^{\prime} are strongly admissible. Combining Propositions 2.16 and 2.17 , we obtain that E × E ′ E\times E^{\prime} is an admissible e-variable for admissible e-variables E E and E ′ E^{\prime} when 𝒫 \mathcal{P} and 𝒫 ′ \mathcal{P}^{\prime} are finite. Continuing from the examples in Chapter 1 , we now provide several simple examples of e-values for different testing problems. Some of these e-values also enjoy a notion of optimality, introduced in Chapter 3 and analyzed in detail in Chapter 6 , so these examples will be revisited later in the book. Let ( ℙ θ ) θ ∈ Θ (\mathbb{P}_{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta} be a parametric family of distributions with their density functions of the test statistic Z Z given by ( p θ ) θ ∈ Θ (p_{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta} , where Θ ⊆ ℝ \Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R} and 𝒵 = ℝ \mathcal{Z}=\mathbb{R} . Assume that monotone likelihood ratio holds: for all θ < θ ′ \theta<\theta^{\prime} , the likelihood ratio p θ ′ ​ ( z ) / p θ ​ ( z ) {p_{\theta^{\prime}}(z)}/{p_{\theta}(z)} exists and is increasing (or decreasing) in z z . The following short proof uses Chebyshev’s association inequality, which states, assuming integrability, 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ f ⁡ ( X ) ​ g ​ ( X ) ] ≥ 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ f ⁡ ( X ) ] ​ 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ g ⁡ ( X ) ] \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[f(X)g(X)]\geq\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[f(X)]\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[g(X)] for any increasing functions f , g f,g and random variable X X . The same conclusion in Proposition 2.18 holds true for testing 𝒫 = { ℙ θ : θ ≥ θ 0 } \mathcal{P}=\{\mathbb{P}_{\theta}:\theta\geq\theta_{0}\} against 𝒬 = { ℙ θ : θ ≤ θ 1 } \mathcal{Q}=\{\mathbb{P}_{\theta}:\theta\leq\theta_{1}\} with θ 0 > θ 1 \theta_{0}>\theta_{1} ; moreover, either inequality (or both) in 𝒫 \mathcal{P} and 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} can be replaced by a strict inequality. The same e-variable can also be used to test 𝒫 = { ℙ θ : θ ≤ θ 0 } \mathcal{P}=\{\mathbb{P}_{\theta}:\theta\leq\theta_{0}\} against 𝒬 = { ℙ θ : θ > θ 0 } . \mathcal{Q}=\{\mathbb{P}_{\theta}:\theta>\theta_{0}\}. The example of normal distributions in Section 1.6 satisfies the monotone likelihood ratio assumption. Therefore, the e-variable in ( 1.2 ) for any μ > 0 \mu>0 is an e-variable for testing { N ⁡ ( θ , 1 ) : θ ≤ 0 } \{\mathrm{N}(\theta,1):\theta\leq 0\} against { N ⁡ ( θ , 1 ) : θ > 0 } \{\mathrm{N}(\theta,1):\theta>0\} . Take 𝒵 = ℝ \mathcal{Z}=\mathbb{R} . A different approach is to use the signed-rank e-variables, based on Wilcoxon’s signed-rank test. The idea is to arrange the magnitudes | X i | |X_{i}| of the observations in the ascending order and assigning to each its rank, which is a number in [ n ] [n] . Under the null hypothesis, any subset of [ n ] [n] has the same probability 2 − n 2^{-n} to be the set of ranks of the positive observations. This determines the distribution of Wilcoxon’s statistic V n V_{n} defined as the sum of the ranks of the positive observations.
