### 4. Testing the mean and the variance / Testing sub-Gaussian means / Constrained hypotheses / Testing likelihood ratio bounds / Testing exchangeability

Let 𝒵 ⊆ ℝ + \mathcal{Z}\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{+} so that the statistic Z Z takes nonnegative values. Fix μ > 0 \mu>0 and consider the null hypothesis that the mean of Z Z is at most μ \mu , The alternative hypothesis 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} is that mean of Z Z is larger than μ \mu . Two natural e-variables are Z / μ Z/\mu and the constant one. All convex combinations of these are also e-variables; therefore, is an e-variable for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} . This construction works outside ℝ + \mathbb{R}_{+} as long as Z Z is bounded from below, by using ( Z − z ) / ( μ − z ) (Z-z)/(\mu-z) in place of Z / μ Z/\mu , where z z is a lower bound on Z Z . An optimal choice of λ \lambda against a specific ℚ \mathbb{Q} is discussed in Section 6.7.4 in the case that 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} is a bounded interval. Corollary 2.15 (b) can be used to deduce that the above e-variable is admissible, despite not being exact. We can also consider the null hypothesis that the mean of Z Z is at most μ \mu and the variance is at most σ 2 \sigma^{2} , In this case, because 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ Z 2 ] ≤ μ 2 + σ 2 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[Z^{2}]\leq\mu^{2}+\sigma^{2} for ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} , each of is an e-variable for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} , in addition to those in ( 2.10 ), as well as the mixtures of ( 2.10 ) and ( 2.11 ). If 𝒵 = ℝ \mathcal{Z}=\mathbb{R} , meaning that Z Z may take negative values, then ( 2.11 ) is an e-variable for As a further example, suppose that we observe nonnegative data Z = ( X 1 , … , X n ) Z=(X_{1},\dots,X_{n}) with the same marginal distribution ℙ \mathbb{P} and possibly dependent components, and we wish to test whether ℙ \mathbb{P} has a mean at most μ \mu . Then, ( X 1 + ⋯ + X n ) / ( n ​ μ ) (X_{1}+\dots+X_{n})/(n\mu) is an e-value for any dependence structure of ( X 1 , … , X n ) (X_{1},\dots,X_{n}) , and does not require making any distributional or independence assumption. An e-variable can be constructed similarly for the case of testing the mean and variance. This example shows the advantage of e-values when it comes to dependent data, and this will be further explored in Chapter 9 . Now take 𝒵 = ℝ \mathcal{Z}=\mathbb{R} and consider the null hypothesis that the observation Z Z has a 1-sub-Gaussian distribution with nonpositive mean. This class is a nonparametric generalization of Gaussians with nonpositive mean and unit variance. Formally, we set Here the sub-Gaussian restriction is only placed on the right tail. For ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} and λ ∈ ℝ \lambda\in\mathbb{R} , let M ℙ ​ ( λ ) = 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ e λ ​ Z ] M_{\mathbb{P}}(\lambda)=\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[e^{\lambda Z}] , which is the moment generating function of Z Z . The constraint in 𝒫 \mathcal{P} means A Taylor expansion in a neighborhood of λ = 0 \lambda=0 yields, Therefore, for λ ≥ 0 \lambda\geq 0 small enough, and this implies 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ Z ] ≤ 0 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[Z]\leq 0 . Since the standard Gaussian distribution (zero mean, unit variance) satisfy the above with equality (inferred via their moment generating function) for every real λ \lambda , the constraint expands the class to account for two aspects: the mean can be negative, and the right tails of the random variable only have to be lighter than that of a unit-variance Gaussian. It is well-known that any bounded distribution on [ − 1 , 1 ] [-1,1] with zero mean is 1-sub-Gaussian; so the class 𝒫 \mathcal{P} clearly does not have any common reference measure, containing discrete distributions with uncountable support as well as continuous unbounded distributions. A class of e-variables for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} is given by: and certainly any convex combinations (mixtures) of its elements are also e-variables. The determination of the optimal e-variable against a specific ℚ \mathbb{Q} is studied in Section 6.7.5 . The determination of all possible e-variables for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} is a nontrivial task. We also consider the closely related null hypothesis that the observation Z Z has a 1-sub-Gaussian distribution with zero (instead of nonpositive) mean: Here, the sub-Gaussian restriction is placed on both the left and the right tails. Every ℙ ∈ 𝒫 0 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}^{0} has zero mean, because now ( 2.13 ) holds for both positive and negative λ \lambda . Again, a natural class of e-variables for 𝒫 0 \mathcal{P}^{0} is and certainly any convex combinations (mixtures) of its elements are also e-variables. It turns out that every ℙ ∈ 𝒫 0 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}^{0} also has variance bounded by one, and this follows from ( 2.13 ) with 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ Z ] = 0 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[Z]=0 . Thus, Z 2 Z^{2} is also an e-variable, as is any convex combination with the aforementioned class. Indeed, 𝒫 0 \mathcal{P}^{0} is a subset of the hypothesis in ( 2.12 ) with μ = 0 \mu=0 and σ = 1 \sigma=1 , and also a subset of the one-sided sub-Gaussian class 𝒫 \mathcal{P} . However, Z 2 Z^{2} is not an e-variable for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} , because those could potentially have very heavy left tails, meaning that their variance is not necessarily bounded. We note in passing that both the above classes of e-variables are admissible, but the rather technical proof is omitted. Many of the above examples of 𝒫 \mathcal{P} , and many others we encounter in this book, can be called “constrained hypotheses”, meaning that they are defined via integral constraints. For such classes, we can sometimes cleanly characterize the set of all e-variables (and all admissible ones) in terms of those constraints. Let ℒ \mathcal{L} denote the set of all real-valued measurable functions on the underlying probability space. A constraint set is any nonempty set of functions Ψ ⊆ ℒ \Psi\subseteq\mathcal{L} . Define the null hypothesis generated by Ψ \Psi as We say that a property holds 𝒫 \mathcal{P} -quasi surely if it holds everywhere outside of a set A A such that sup ℙ ∈ 𝒫 ℙ ⁡ ( A ) = 0 \sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{P}(A)=0 . We start with the case where Ψ = { g 1 , … , g K } \Psi=\{g_{1},\dots,g_{K}\} is finite, as in the case of testing a mean, or mean and variance. One can show that the set of all e-variables 𝔈 \mathfrak{E} for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} is given by all [ 0 , ∞ ] [0,\infty] -valued measurable functions which are 𝒫 \mathcal{P} -quasi surely dominated by 1 + ∑ k = 1 K π k ​ g k 1+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\pi_{k}g_{k} for some π ∈ ℝ + K \pi\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{K} . As a corollary, every admissible e-variable for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} is 𝒫 \mathcal{P} -quasi surely equal to 1 + ∑ k = 1 K π k ​ g k 1+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\pi_{k}g_{k} for some π \pi in the set Clearly, the zero vector 𝟎 \mathbf{0} is in Π Ψ \Pi^{\Psi} . But for some choices of Ψ \Psi , Π Ψ \Pi^{\Psi} may be equal to { 𝟎 } \{\mathbf{0}\} , in which case all e-variables for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} are dominated by the constant 1 1 . In settings where Ψ \Psi is infinite, such as the sub-Gaussian example in the previous subsection, the corresponding statement is more complex. Define where cone ​ ( Ψ ) \text{cone}(\Psi) is the conic hull of Ψ \Psi . Then, the set of all e-variables 𝔈 \mathfrak{E} for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} is given by all [ 0 , ∞ ] [0,\infty] -valued measurable functions that are 𝒫 \mathcal{P} -quasi surely equal to 1 + f 1+f for some f ∈ 𝒞 ¯ f\in\overline{\mathcal{C}} , where 𝒞 ¯ \overline{\mathcal{C}} is the weak closure of 𝒞 \mathcal{C} (which is the closure with respect to a particular weak topology that is omitted here for brevity). The constraints in ( 2.14 ) are formulated via integrals of f ∈ Ψ f\in\Psi over ℙ \mathbb{P} . In some applications, the constraints are formulated via other functionals, such as risk measures, and some of them are studied in Chapter 16 . Let 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} be a general sample space. Let ℙ 0 \mathbb{P}_{0} be a fixed probability measure and γ ∈ [ 1 , ∞ ) \gamma\in[1,\infty) , and consider the null hypothesis against an alternative ℚ ≪ ℙ 0 \mathbb{Q}\ll\mathbb{P}_{0} . To interpret, testing 𝒫 \mathcal{P} means to test whether the true data-generating distribution is close to a given benchmark distribution ℙ 0 \mathbb{P}_{0} in terms of likelihood ratio being bounded by γ \gamma . The case γ = 1 \gamma=1 corresponds to the simple null hypothesis { ℙ 0 } \{\mathbb{P}_{0}\} . Clearly, the likelihood ratio Z ∗ = d ​ ℚ / d ​ ℙ 0 Z^{*}=\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}/\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{0} is not an e-variable for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} unless γ = 1 \gamma=1 , but if we modify it to E = Z ∗ / γ E=Z^{*}/\gamma , then 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ E ] ≤ γ ​ 𝔼 ℙ 0 ​ [ Z ∗ / γ ] ≤ 1 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[E]\leq\gamma\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}_{0}}[Z^{*}/\gamma]\leq 1 . ### 5. Informal grids for realized e-values / Bibliographical note

In testing scientific hypotheses, thresholds for p-values are often chosen as 0.01 0.01 or 0.05 0.05 which correspond to type-I errors controlled at these levels. The e-to-p calibrator e ↦ min ⁡ ( 1 / e , 1 ) e\mapsto\min(1/e,1) implies that thresholds of 100 100 and 20 20 for e-values also have the above type-I error control. However, it is not recommended in practice to directly use these thresholds as the conversion e ↦ min ⁡ ( 1 / e , 1 ) e\mapsto\min(1/e,1) is typically wasteful. In Section 1.5 , and also in Section 4.1 later, we see that for a ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) a\in(0,1) , an e-value of 1 / a 1/a carries more information than a p-value of a a . In order to judge how significant results of testing using e-values are, the type-I error, based on which p-values are defined, may not be the desirable metric. Although there are no universally agreed thresholds to use for e-values, the rule of thumb of Jeffreys, originally designed for likelihood ratios, may offer some insight, as e-values are generalizations of likelihood ratios (for example, recall the result presented in Section 2.5.1 ). We summarize this rule of thumb in Table 2.19 . For instance, if e > 3.16 e>3.16 , we recommend to associate it with “substantial” (similar to p < 0.05 p<0.05 ), and if e > 10 e>10 , we recommend to associate it with “strong” (similar to p < 0.01 p<0.01 ). One should keep in mind that p-values and e-values do not have a one-to-one correspondence with each other, and the interpretation of the informal grid for e-values does not rely on converting into p-values. Because Jeffreys focused on likelihood ratios (or Bayes factors), the above rule of thumb applies well to e-values that are obtained “naturally”, such as likelihood ratios or their generalizations in the subsequent chapters. At the other extreme, for all-or-nothing e-variables (see Definition 3.5 ), such as E = 𝟙 { P ≤ α } / α E=\mathds{1}_{\{P\leq\alpha\}}/\alpha for some p-variable P P , one should not refer to the grids in Table 2.19 , because for such an e-variable, E = 10 E=10 is equivalent to P ≤ 0.1 P\leq 0.1 . Finally, Section 6.6 draws deeper connections between e-values and Bayes factors, but it builds on some more advanced material. It is important to note that in general, p-values and e-values need not even “point in the same direction”. We can easily have situations where the p-value appears to provide strong evidence against the null, but the e-value supports the null, or vice versa. Let us elaborate with a simple example. Consider testing μ = 0 \mu=0 against μ = δ \mu=\delta (for some δ > 0 \delta>0 ) when we see data Z ∼ N ⁡ ( μ , 1 ) Z\sim N(\mu,1) . Since the classic z z -test is uniformly most powerful in this setting, we would get the same p-value P = 1 − Φ ⁡ ( Z ) P=1-\Phi(Z) , regardless of the alternative mean δ \delta , while the (arguably natural) likelihood ratio e-value E = exp ⁡ ( δ ​ Z − δ 2 / 2 ) E=\exp(\delta Z-\delta^{2}/2) depends strongly on δ \delta . For instance, with δ = 5 \delta=5 and X = 2 X=2 , the p-value P ≈ 0.023 P\approx 0.023 (suggesting evidence against the null), but the e-value E ≈ 0.08 E\approx 0.08 (suggesting evidence for the null as a likelihood ratio). Thus the realized p-value and e-value could “point in different directions” depending on δ \delta and the realized data. This is another reason that we do not like to in general compare e-value and p-value based methods: while calibration can often preserve the direction (large/small e-values yield small/large p-values and vice versa), natural p-values and e-values that we may directly construct from the data need not have any natural concordance with each other, and so the resulting methods are not always directly comparable. This chapter largely resembles results and concepts in various important papers on e-values, but also it contains many results that are derived for the book to make a coherent piece. The example in Section 2.1 is based on Vovk and Wang (2023) . The concept of a calibrator in Section 2.3 dates back, at least, to Vovk (1993) ; the admissibility results are in Vovk and Wang (2021) . Bickel (2025) discussed p-to-e calibrators that depend on the sample size based on Bayes factors. Section 2.4 is based on Ignatiadis et al. (2024b) and Ramdas and Manole (2025) . The definition of admissibility in Section 2.5 is derived from Ramdas et al. (2020) , which studies it more generally in sequential settings. Some sufficient conditions in Corollary 2.15 also follow from the aforementioned paper. Among the examples in Section 2.6 , exponential families and monotone likelihood ratios were studied by Grünwald et al. (2024a) ; Grünwald et al. (2024b) . The testing symmetry example was first studied by Ramdas et al. (2020) , with follow-up work in Koning (2023b) ; Vovk and Wang (2024b) ; Larsson et al. (2025a) . The bounded mean example is a variant of one studied in Waudby-Smith and Ramdas (2024) , revisited by Larsson et al. (2025a) . The mean-and-variance example was studied by Fan et al. (2025) . Sub-Gaussian e-values were studied by Howard et al. (2020) ; Ramdas et al. (2020) ; Larsson et al. (2025a) . The example on constrained hypotheses was studied by Clerico (2024) ; Larsson et al. (2025b) ; our presentation is based on the latter. The exchangeability example was studied in Vovk (2021) and Ramdas et al. (2022) in a sequential context. In the batch context, the soft-rank e-value was proposed in the first arXiv preprint of Wang and Ramdas (2022) (but omitted in the journal version), and revisited by Koning (2023b) . The grids in Section 2.7 can be found in ( Jeffreys, 1961 , Appendix B) , with a variant proposed in Kass and Raftery (1995) .

### 6. Efficiency: E-values under the alternative hypothesis

In Chapter 2 we focused only on the properties of the e-values under the null hypothesis. In this chapter, we describe its desired properties under the alternative hypothesis. This requires the notions of powered e-values, e-power, and log-optimality. The power of a test ϕ \phi against an alternative ℚ \mathbb{Q} is simply 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ ϕ ] \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\phi] . The type-II error (rate) of ϕ \phi equals one minus its power. When testing a null hypothesis 𝒫 \mathcal{P} , a test ϕ \phi is powered at level α ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \alpha\in(0,1) against 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} if and it is uniformly powered if further inf ℚ ∈ 𝒬 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ ϕ ] > α \inf_{\mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\phi]>\alpha . An e-variable E E is powered against 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} if 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ E ] > 1 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[E]>1 for every ℚ ∈ 𝒬 \mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} , and it is uniformly powered against 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} if inf ℚ ∈ 𝒬 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ E ] > 1 \inf_{\mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[E]>1 . A closely related concept is that of level- α \alpha unbiased tests, i.e., tests ϕ \phi satisfying 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ ϕ ] ≤ α \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[\phi]\leq\alpha for every ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} and 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ ϕ ] ≥ α \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\phi]\geq\alpha for every ℚ ∈ 𝒬 \mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} . For the purpose of this chapter, our definition of powered tests is needed, and it excludes trivial cases such as the constant test ϕ = α \phi=\alpha . Suppose that iid data X 1 , … , X n X_{1},\dots,X_{n} are sampled from N ⁡ ( μ , 1 ) N(\mu,1) with unknown μ \mu . If we test H 0 : μ ≤ 0 H_{0}:\mu\leq 0 against H 1 : μ > 0 H_{1}:\mu>0 , then exp ⁡ ( X 1 + ⋯ + X n − n / 2 ) \exp(X_{1}+\dots+X_{n}-n/2) is a powered e-variable (but not uniformly e-powered). This e-variable is uniformly powered against H 1 : μ ≥ ε H_{1}:\mu\geq\varepsilon for any ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 . If we test H 0 : μ < 0 H_{0}:\mu<0 against H 1 : μ ≥ 0 H_{1}:\mu\geq 0 , then there does not exist a powered e-variable (or test) because it is impossible to have power against the boundary point 0 0 . Example 3.3 and its proof demonstrate a more general phenomenon. When testing a parametric model (like the one in the example), if the null hypothesis is an open subset of the parameter space, the alternative is its complement, and densities exist and are continuous in the parameter, then powered tests and e-variables do not exist. An e-variable E E is called an all-or-nothing e-variable if E = 𝟙 A / c E=\mathds{1}_{A}/c for some event A A and c > 0 c>0 . For any α ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \alpha\in(0,1) , there is a one-to-one correspondence between [ 0 , 1 / α CLOSE [0,1/\alpha ]-valued e-variables and level- α \alpha tests. Specifically, for any α ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] \alpha\in(0,1] , we can use the relationship to move between a level- α \alpha test ϕ \phi and a corresponding e-variable E E . If ϕ \phi is binary, then E E is an all-or-nothing e-variable, which takes values in { 0 , 1 / α } \{0,1/\alpha\} . In particular, for any binary test ϕ \phi that rejects the null hypothesis when a realized p-value is smaller than or equal to α \alpha , ϕ \phi can be expressed in terms of the all-or-nothing e-variable 𝟙 { P ≤ α } / α \mathds{1}_{\{P\leq\alpha\}}/\alpha . Thus, there exists a powered level- α \alpha binary test if and only if there exists a powered e-variable taking values in { 0 , 1 / α } \{0,1/\alpha\} . We will expand this observation to include p-variables in Theorem 3.9 . For β ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] \beta\in(0,1] , a p-variable P P is powered on [ 0 , β ] [0,\beta] against 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} if for every ℚ ∈ 𝒬 \mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} , we have ℚ ⁡ ( P ≤ t ) ≥ t \mathbb{Q}(P\leq t)\geq t for all t ∈ [ 0 , β ] t\in[0,\beta] and strict inequality holds for some t ∈ [ 0 , β ] t\in[0,\beta] . We say P P is powered if the above holds for β = 1 \beta=1 ; this means that P P is strictly stochastically smaller than a uniform random variable on [ 0 , 1 ] [0,1] under each ℚ ∈ 𝒬 \mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} . To satisfy Assumption ( U ), it may be helpful to consider a lifted sample space Ω × [ 0 , 1 ] \Omega\times[0,1] from Ω \Omega and product probability measures ℙ × λ \mathbb{P}\times\lambda and ℚ × λ \mathbb{Q}\times\lambda from ℙ \mathbb{P} and ℚ \mathbb{Q} , where λ \lambda is the Lebesgue measure. Then, choose U U with U ⁡ ( ω 1 , ω 2 ) = ω 2 U(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})=\omega_{2} , and extend random variables X X in the original space to the mappings ( ω 1 , ω 2 ) ↦ X ⁡ ( ω 1 ) (\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\mapsto X(\omega_{1}) , so that U U is independent of the other random variables that we consider. Suppose that Assumption ( U ) holds. For testing 𝒫 \mathcal{P} against 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} (without restriction), the following are equivalent: In the directions (i) ⇔ \Leftrightarrow (ii), (iii) ⇒ \Rightarrow (i) and (v) ⇒ \Rightarrow (i), we do not need ( U ). Consider a finite sample space Ω \Omega and testing ℙ \mathbb{P} against ℚ \mathbb{Q} with ℙ ≠ ℚ \mathbb{P}\neq\mathbb{Q} and ℙ ⁡ ( { ω } ) > 0 \mathbb{P}(\{\omega\})>0 for all ω ∈ Ω \omega\in\Omega (as in the Bernoulli setting in Section 1.6 ). A powered e-variable is the likelihood ratio ℚ / ℙ \mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{P} . Any p-variable P P defined on Ω \Omega takes finitely many positive values. Therefore, ℚ ⁡ ( P ≤ α ) = 0 \mathbb{Q}(P\leq\alpha)=0 for α > 0 \alpha>0 small enough, showing that P P cannot be [ 0 , β ] [0,\beta] powered against ℚ \mathbb{Q} for any β ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] \beta\in(0,1] . The e-power of an e-variable E E against an alternative ℚ \mathbb{Q} is 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ E ] \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log E] , assuming that the expectation is well-defined. An e-variable E E has positive e-power against 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} if 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ E ] > 0 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log E]>0 for each ℚ ∈ 𝒬 \mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} . It has uniformly positive e-power against 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} if inf ℚ ∈ 𝒬 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ E ] > 0 \inf_{\mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log E]>0 . Let E 1 , E 2 , … E_{1},E_{2},\dots be a sequence of e-variables that are iid under ℚ \mathbb{Q} , and M M be given by ( 3.1 ). If 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ E 1 ] > 0 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log E_{1}]>0 , then ℚ ⁡ ( M t > 1 / α ) → 1 \mathbb{Q}(M_{t}>1/\alpha)\to 1 as t → ∞ t\to\infty for every α > 0 \alpha>0 . If 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ E 1 ] < 0 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log E_{1}]<0 , then ℚ ⁡ ( M t > 1 / α ) → 0 \mathbb{Q}(M_{t}>1/\alpha)\to 0 as t → ∞ t\to\infty for every α > 0 \alpha>0 . For testing ℙ = N ⁡ ( 0 , 1 ) \mathbb{P}=\mathrm{N}(0,1) against ℚ = N ⁡ ( μ , 1 ) \mathbb{Q}=\mathrm{N}(\mu,1) with n n observations, the likelihood ratio e-value in ( 1.1 ) is E n = ∏ i = 1 n exp ⁡ ( μ ​ X i − μ 2 / 2 ) E_{n}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\exp(\mu X_{i}-\mu^{2}/2) . Its e-power is thus 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ E n ] = n ​ 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ μ ​ X 1 − μ 2 / 2 ] = n ​ μ 2 / 2 . \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log E_{n}]=n\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\mu X_{1}-\mu^{2}/2]=n\mu^{2}/2. This e-variable has positive e-power against ℚ \mathbb{Q} and the e-power grows linearly in n n . Indeed, these two properties hold for all likelihood ratio e-values with iid observations. Let ℚ θ = N ⁡ ( θ , 1 ) \mathbb{Q}_{\theta}=\mathrm{N}(\theta,1) for θ ∈ ℝ \theta\in\mathbb{R} . Noting that 𝔼 ℚ θ ​ [ log ⁡ E n ] = n ⁡ ( μ ​ θ − μ 2 / 2 ) \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}_{\theta}}[\log E_{n}]=n(\mu\theta-\mu^{2}/2) , the e-variable E n E_{n} has positive e-power against { ℚ θ : θ > ν } \{\mathbb{Q}_{\theta}:\theta>\nu\} for ν ≥ μ / 2 \nu\geq\mu/2 (uniformly if ν > μ / 2 \nu>\mu/2 ), and it does not have positive e-power against any ℚ θ \mathbb{Q}_{\theta} with θ ≤ μ / 2 \theta\leq\mu/2 . Let X ≥ 0 X\geq 0 be a nonnegative random variable. For any ℚ ∈ ℳ 1 \mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{M}_{1} , In fact, either condition in ( 3.2 ) implies the existence of a bounded nonnegative Y Y with 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ Y ] > 1 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[Y]>1 and 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ ( 1 − λ + λ ​ Y ) ] > 0 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log(1-\lambda+\lambda Y)]>0 . For a general 𝒬 ⊆ ℳ 1 \mathcal{Q}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{1} , if X X is bounded or 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} is finite, then As a consequence, for a finite 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} , an e-variable with positive e-power against 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} exists if and only if a powered e-variable against 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} exists. ### 7. Powered tests, e-values, and p-values / E-power and powered e-values

We first introduce the concept of power for tests and e-values. The power of a binary test ϕ \phi against ℚ \mathbb{Q} is defined as the probability of rejecting the null hypothesis under the alternative ℚ \mathbb{Q} . This concept can be naturally generalized to non-binary tests as we present below. When we mention a powered test ϕ \phi without specifying the level, it should be understood as α = sup ℙ ∈ 𝒫 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ ϕ ] \alpha=\sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[\phi] . Tests that are not powered can still be practically useful. For instance, the existence of powered tests and e-variables requires that the alternative 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} does not contain a “boundary point” of the null 𝒫 \mathcal{P} , as shown by Example 3.3 below. Nevertheless, although powered tests do not exist for testing H 0 : μ < 0 H_{0}:\mu<0 against H 1 : μ ≥ 0 H_{1}:\mu\geq 0 in that example, any powered test for testing H 0 H_{0} against H 1 ′ : μ > 0 H^{\prime}_{1}:\mu>0 can still be useful to test H 0 H_{0} against H 1 H_{1} in practical applications. Next, we discuss the natural connection between e-values and tests. Given any level- α \alpha binary test, one can always reproduce its decision by combining an “all-or-nothing e-value” with Markov’s inequality, as we explain (and generalize) below. All-or-nothing e-variables take on only two values: zero or 1 / c 1/c for some c c . Further, it is clear that A A must satisfy ℙ ⁡ ( A ) ≤ c \mathbb{P}(A)\leq c for all ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} . For such e-variables, Markov’s inequality at level c c holds with equality, so there is no loss in translating between e-values, tests, and p-values. As a consequence of Fact 3.6 , all binary tests based on p-values — routinely used in the sciences — can be replicated using e-values. A non-binary test ϕ \phi is usually provided with the interpretation that it outputs a rejection with probability ϕ \phi , realized as a number in [ 0 , 1 ] [0,1] . In other words, one can convert a non-binary test ϕ \phi to a binary test ϕ ′ \phi^{\prime} as ϕ ′ = 𝟙 { U ≤ ϕ } \phi^{\prime}=\mathds{1}_{\{U\leq\phi\}} , where U U is an independent uniform random variable on [ 0 , 1 ] [0,1] . This is closely related to the randomized Markov’s inequality discussed in Section 2.4 . An e-variable E E taking values in [ 0 , 1 / α ] [0,1/\alpha] is associated with a level- α \alpha test ϕ = α ​ E \phi=\alpha E ; in fact, it also yields a level- β \beta test for any β < α \beta<\alpha . A single e-variable yields a family of level- α \alpha tests (indexed by α \alpha ), but in the other direction, every level- α \alpha test yields a different e-variable. The above discussion highlights an important conceptual point — e-values suffice for testing: If a problem is testable (i.e., a powered test exists), then it is testable with e-values. This also applies to a notion of powered p-variables, although it is conceptually slightly more complicated, as we define below. For practical purposes, it is more than sufficient to consider β = 0.25 \beta=0.25 , for example, as rejections via p-values are always done at small levels. In the next section, we will connect powered tests, powered e-variables, and powered p-variables. Given this high-level equivalence between tests and bounded e-values discussed in Fact 3.6 , one may be tempted to ask: What is new here? Are we just simply working with tests in disguise? The answer is this: While all-or-nothing e-values are interesting conceptually in order to relate them to tests and p-values, these are not typically how one would construct e-values in practice. Recall the example from Section 1.6 on likelihood ratios. They will commonly have support on the entire positive real line (not just on two points), and never take on the value zero if ℙ \mathbb{P} and ℚ \mathbb{Q} are absolutely continuous with respect to each other. To explain this, the following concept of e-power is useful. The e-power of an e-variable could equal ∞ \infty or − ∞ -\infty (these still count as being well-defined). Indeed, whenever we make statements involving expected logarithms, we require them to be well-defined. The e-power (higher is better) of all-or-nothing e-variables is − ∞ -\infty . The concept of e-power is closely related to the Kelly criterion in betting and investment. The logarithm reflects the fact that in many applications, e-values are multiplied together. For a simplest example, consider a sequence of iid e-variables E 1 , E 2 , … E_{1},E_{2},\dots with finite e-power and the process M = ( M t ) t ∈ ℕ M=(M_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}} defined by The e-power of E 1 E_{1} against ℚ \mathbb{Q} equals the asymptotic growth rate of M M , that is, the ℚ \mathbb{Q} -almost sure limit of ( log ⁡ M t ) / t (\log M_{t})/t as t → ∞ t\to\infty (see Definition 7.4 ), justified by the strong law of large numbers. The asymptotic growth rate will be important in the methodology of testing by betting, formally treated in Chapter 7 . When converting M t M_{t} into the level- α \alpha test 𝟙 { M t ≥ 1 / α } \mathds{1}_{\{M_{t}\geq 1/\alpha\}} for α ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \alpha\in(0,1) , the role of e-power is clear from the following proposition. The proof of Proposition 3.12 is a simple application of the strong law of large numbers. Note that for each t ∈ ℕ t\in\mathbb{N} , E 1 E_{1} has positive e-power against ℚ \mathbb{Q} is and only if M t M_{t} has positive e-power against ℚ \mathbb{Q} . Proposition 3.12 implies that in the simple iid setting, a positive e-power leads to asymptotic power- 1 1 tests, and a negative e-power leads to asymptotic power- 0 0 tests. An axiomatic justification of e-power is presented in Section 3.8 , which uses an axiom based on an idea that is similar to Proposition 3.12 . The condition 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ E ] > 0 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log E]>0 in Definition 3.11 is equivalent to saying that the geometric mean of X X is larger than one: exp ⁡ 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ E ] > 1 \exp\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log E]>1 , which is a stronger constraint than requiring the (arithmetic) mean of E E to be larger than 1, that is, 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ E ] > 1 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[E]>1 in Definition 3.1 (this can be seen from Jensen’s inequality). Hence, if E E has positive e-power, then E E is a powered e-variable, but the reverse implication is not true in general. These two conditions are closely related. Indeed, for a simple alternative ℚ \mathbb{Q} , from any powered e-variable one can easily find an e-variable with positive e-power, through the transform E ↦ 1 − λ + λ ​ E E\mapsto 1-\lambda+\lambda E for some small λ > 0 \lambda>0 that depends on E E and ℚ \mathbb{Q} . This is formalized in the following result. The forward direction in ( 3.3 ) fails if 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} is infinite and X X is unbounded. Below, we give an example of an unbounded e-variable E E satisfying inf ℚ ∈ 𝒬 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ E ] > 1 \inf_{\mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[E]>1 (that is, uniformly powered against 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} ), but for all λ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] . \lambda\in(0,1]. Moreover, this example also illustrates that a bounded powered e-variable may not exist even if a uniformly powered e-variable exists. Let E E be given by E ⁡ ( n ) = 2 ​ n E(n)=2n for n ∈ ℕ 0 n\in\mathbb{N}_{0} . This gives 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ E ] = 1 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[E]=1 and 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ E ] = 2 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[E]=2 for all ℚ ∈ 𝒬 \mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} . Thus, E E is a uniformly powered e-variable, and it is unbounded. For any λ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] \lambda\in(0,1] , we have Since ( 1 − λ ) n − 1 ​ ( 1 + ( 2 ​ n − 1 ) ​ λ ) < 1 (1-\lambda)^{n-1}(1+(2n-1)\lambda)<1 for n n large enough, we know that inf ℚ ∈ 𝒬 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ ( 1 − λ + λ ​ E ) ] < 0 \inf_{\mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log(1-\lambda+\lambda E)]<0 for all λ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] \lambda\in(0,1] . There does not exist any bounded e-variable that is powered against 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} . To see this, for an e-variable E E to be powered against ℚ 1 \mathbb{Q}_{1} , we have E ⁡ ( 1 ) > 1 E(1)>1 . For E E to be powered against ℚ n \mathbb{Q}_{n} , we need ( 1 / n ) ​ E ​ ( n ) + ( 1 − 1 / n ) ​ E ​ ( 0 ) > 1 (1/n)E(n)+(1-1/n)E(0)>1 for all n ∈ ℕ n\in\mathbb{N} . Since E E is bounded, this means E ⁡ ( 0 ) ≥ 1 E(0)\geq 1 . Together with E ⁡ ( 1 ) > 1 E(1)>1 , we have 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ E ] > 1 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[E]>1 , conflicting the fact that E E is an e-variable for ℙ \mathbb{P} . As hinted above, the e-power 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ E ] \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log E] may not always be well-defined, due to the unbounded nature of the logarithm function. Moreover, there are some special (perhaps pathological) examples in which the e-power, although well-defined, may be infinite, and does not have a useful interpretation. We give a stylized, unrealistic, example here.

### 8. Conditions for the existence of powered e-variables / Log-optimality and likelihood ratios / Conditional log-optimality and log-optimal calibrators / Asymptotic log-optimality: methods of mixtures and plug-in / Mixture method. / Plug-in method.

In this section, we address the existence of powered e-variables and p-variables by means of two characterization results. These results connect the existence of p-variables and e-variables to the structure of the null and alternative hypotheses, different from Theorem 3.9 , which does not discuss how to check the existence. In these results, let L L and M M be two positive integers and we continue to assume ( U ) in Section 3.2 . The following result uses the total variation distance between sets of distributions, which we now define. For any two distributions ℙ , ℚ \mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q} , define where the supremum is taken over all measurable sets A A . Then, given two sets of distributions 𝒫 , 𝒬 \mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q} , we can define The following result is closely connected to a classic theorem of Kraft (1955) , proved by Le Cam in private communication. The Kraft–Le Cam theorem states that for general 𝒫 \mathcal{P} and 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} (not necessarily finite) that have a common reference measure, the following holds: for any ε ≥ 0 \varepsilon\geq 0 , there exists a test ϕ \phi such that inf ℚ ∈ 𝒬 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ ϕ ] − sup ℙ ∈ 𝒫 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ ϕ ] > ε \inf_{\mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\phi]-\sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[\phi]>\varepsilon if and only if the total variation distance between the closed convex hulls of 𝒫 \mathcal{P} and 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} is greater than 2 ​ ε 2\varepsilon . there exists a test ϕ \phi such that inf ℚ ∈ 𝒬 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ ϕ ] > sup ℙ ∈ 𝒫 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ ϕ ] \inf_{\mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\phi]>\sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[\phi] ; there exists a uniformly powered bounded e-variable E E for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} against 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} ; d TV ​ ( Conv ⁡ ( 𝒫 ) , Conv ⁡ ( 𝒬 ) ) > 0 d_{\text{TV}}(\mathrm{Conv}(\mathcal{P}),\mathrm{Conv}(\mathcal{Q}))>0 . If there is no common reference measure, then condition (iii) is not equivalent to the earlier ones. In fact, even when 𝒬 = { ℚ } \mathcal{Q}=\{\mathbb{Q}\} is simple, d TV ​ ( Conv ⁡ ( 𝒫 ) , ℚ ) > 0 d_{\text{TV}}(\mathrm{Conv}(\mathcal{P}),\mathbb{Q})>0 does not imply the existence of a powered e-variable. The appropriate condition actually involves an object called the bipolar of 𝒫 \mathcal{P} , and is formally presented in Theorem 6.10 . Separately, note that when 𝒫 \mathcal{P} and 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} are finite, condition (iii) above reduces to Conv ⁡ ( ℚ 1 , … , ℚ M ) ∩ Conv ⁡ ( ℙ 1 , … , ℙ L ) = ∅ \mathrm{Conv}(\mathbb{Q}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{Q}_{M})\cap\mathrm{Conv}(\mathbb{P}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{P}_{L})=\varnothing . The existence of exact p-variables and e-variables is technically more advanced, and studied in Section 14.3 , where we obtain a similar result (Theorem 14.6 ) to Theorem 3.17 . Next, we turn to the key concept of log-optimality for a simple alternative { ℚ } \{\mathbb{Q}\} , which is defined essentially via the comparison of e-power. Definitions 3.11 and 3.18 are obviously connected. If the maximum e-power, defined as is finite, then the log-optimal e-variable maximizes the e-power, and the definition of log-optimality reduces to saying 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ E ′ ] ≤ 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ E ] \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log E^{\prime}]\leq\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log E] . The current definition allows the maximum e-power to be infinite, which could occur even in simple cases such as testing a Gaussian against a Cauchy because the tails of the Cauchy are much heavier than that of the Gaussian; see also Example 3.16 . Note that 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ ( E ′ / E ) ] ≤ 0 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log(E^{\prime}/E)]\leq 0 and 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ ( E ′ ) ] ≤ 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ ( E ) ] \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log(E^{\prime})]\leq\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log(E)] are equivalent when E E and E ′ E^{\prime} have finite e-power, but the two conditions are not equivalent in general. We do not prove the first part in this book, since the proof is quite technical, and defer the second part to Chapter 6 . However, in the case of a simple null hypothesis ℙ \mathbb{P} with ℚ ≪ ℙ \mathbb{Q}\ll\mathbb{P} , existence and uniqueness of the log-optimal e-variable can be easily argued, as we will see in Theorem 3.20 . In this section, we present an important result on the log-optimal e-values for a simple null hypothesis 𝒫 = { ℙ } \mathcal{P}=\{\mathbb{P}\} and a simple alternative 𝒬 = { ℚ } \mathcal{Q}=\{\mathbb{Q}\} . We will assume that ℚ ≪ ℙ \mathbb{Q}\ll\mathbb{P} . Let q q and p p represent the densities of ℚ \mathbb{Q} and ℙ \mathbb{P} with respect to some reference measure 𝕃 \mathbb{L} , by which we can write ( d ​ ℚ / d ​ ℙ ) ​ ( x ) = q ⁡ ( x ) / p ⁡ ( x ) ({\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}/{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}})(x)={q(x)}/{p(x)} . The classic Neyman–Pearson lemma for hypothesis testing states that when testing a simple null ℙ \mathbb{P} against a simple alternative ℚ \mathbb{Q} , the most powerful level- α \alpha test involves thresholding the likelihood ratio. We now present a result that can be seen as the e-analog of the Neyman–Pearson lemma. Importantly, we are not interested in designing tests (which can be obtained via Markov’s inequality as discussed in Chapter 2 ), but simply in designing e-variable that is log-optimal (as e-powerful as possible). To prepare for the statement, define the Kullback–Leibler divergence between ℚ \mathbb{Q} and ℙ \mathbb{P} as where it is defined as ∞ \infty if ℚ \mathbb{Q} is not absolutely continuous with respect to ℙ \mathbb{P} . Gibbs’ inequality, or the Shannon–Kolmogorov information inequality, states that the Kullback–Leibler divergence between any two distributions is nonnegative: KL ⁡ ( ℚ , ℙ ) ≥ 0 \mathrm{KL}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{P})\geq 0 . Further, equality holds if and only if ℙ = ℚ \mathbb{P}=\mathbb{Q} . In Chapter 6 , we present a more general result encompassing Theorem 3.20 , which even relaxes the assumption ℚ ≪ ℙ \mathbb{Q}\ll\mathbb{P} . In statistical applications, e-variables and p-variables are constructed based on available data, and thus they belong to a σ \sigma -algebra 𝒢 ⊆ ℱ \mathcal{G}\subseteq\mathcal{F} generated by the data. With this restriction, we need to consider the log-optimal e-variable with respect to 𝒢 \mathcal{G} , that is, an e-variable E E measurable with respect to 𝒢 \mathcal{G} that satisfies 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ ( E ′ / E ) ] ≤ 0 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log(E^{\prime}/E)]\leq 0 for all e-variables E ′ E^{\prime} that are measurable with respect to 𝒢 \mathcal{G} . In what follows, for ℙ ∈ ℳ 1 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{M}_{1} , ℙ | 𝒢 \mathbb{P}|_{\mathcal{G}} is the restriction of ℙ \mathbb{P} to 𝒢 \mathcal{G} , that is, ℙ | 𝒢 ​ ( A ) = ℙ ​ ( A ) \mathbb{P}|_{\mathcal{G}}(A)=\mathbb{P}(A) for A ∈ 𝒢 A\in\mathcal{G} . A direct consequence of Proposition 3.22 is the log-optimal calibrator. Suppose that we know a p-variable P P is uniformly distributed on [ 0 , 1 ] [0,1] under ℙ \mathbb{P} ; thus it is exact. For a given simple alternative ℚ \mathbb{Q} , we can define the log-optimal calibrator , that is, a calibrator f f such that for any other calibrator g g . In other words, f ⁡ ( P ) f(P) is the log-optimal e-variable among all possible e-variables that are calibrated from P P . Consider now the case of testing a simple null hypothesis ℙ \mathbb{P} against a composite alternative 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} . It is easiest to think about the case where all the distributions involved are iid product probability measures, in which case, with only a slight overload of notation, we can let ℙ \mathbb{P} and ℚ ∈ 𝒬 \mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} refer to the distributions of each individual observation, and let these be associated with densities p p and q q . Let the data be represented by a vector X n X^{n} . A reasonable objective is that of asymptotic log-optimality . Here, the goal is to define a sequence of e-variables E n = E ⁡ ( X n ) E_{n}=E(X^{n}) such that for any ℚ ∈ 𝒬 \mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} , The right-hand side of the above expression is the e-power of an oracle that knows which alternative distribution is generating the data. If the above limit holds, our e-variable is able to asymptotically achieve the same e-power without this knowledge. There are two broad classes of methods that do achieve these goals under weak assumptions (that we do not expand on here): the mixture and the plug-in methods. Pick a mixture distribution ν \nu over 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} , and define the e-variable Note the order of integral and product: we are calculating the mixture over likelihood ratios. The key limitation to applicability of this method is that the mixture integral should be analytically or computationally easy to evaluate. Let ℚ i − 1 ∈ 𝒬 \mathbb{Q}_{i-1}\in\mathcal{Q} be a distribution of the i i -th observation, picked based on the first i − 1 i-1 observations, and let q i − 1 q_{i-1} be its density. Define It is not hard to check that E n E_{n} is an e-variable. It is possible to prove the asymptotic log-optimality ( 3.8 ) of the above methods under some rather weak assumptions, but we omit these details. We only make some informal remarks here, which are still useful for the practitioner. For asymptotic log-optimality of the mixture method, it is necessary that ν \nu has full support over 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} ; for parametric problems with finite dimensional alternatives, this condition is often sufficient as well. For the plug-in method, a sensible choice of ℚ i − 1 \mathbb{Q}_{i-1} is the posterior mean of ℚ ∈ 𝒬 \mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} after seeing X i − 1 X^{i-1} , when starting with prior ν \nu . If the mixture method with ν \nu is asymptotically log-optimal, then one would typically expect the aforementioned plug-in method to also have the same property. The mixture method is also useful for composite nulls. Here we give an explicit example without derivation. Next, we demonstrate a setting with non-iid data where the mixture method is helpful and natural. In fact, the above example underlies the much more general, nonparametric, sequential change detection methodology of e-detectors , which is out of the scope of this book.

### 9. Axiomatic justification of the e-power / Bibliographical note

In this section we justify the definition of e-power in Definition 3.11 through an axiomatic approach. Let 𝒳 \mathcal{X} be the set of all nonnegative random variables that are bounded from above and away from 0 0 . We will restrict our consideration of e-power on 𝒳 \mathcal{X} to avoid issues with infinity, and it can be naturally extended to the set of all nonnegative random variables. As standard in the axiomatic approach in decision theory, axioms become weaker when they are formulated on a smaller set of objects, making the axiomatic characterization results stronger. Therefore, our restriction to 𝒳 \mathcal{X} is harmless. Let ℳ 1 \mathcal{M}_{1} be the set of all probability measures on ( Ω , ℱ ) (\Omega,\mathcal{F}) . We will fix the alternative hypothesis ℚ ∈ ℳ 1 \mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{M}_{1} , which is an atomless probability measure. The requirement of ℚ \mathbb{Q} being atomless is only used to justify the existence of a non-degenerate iid sequence. In general, e-variables for the null hypothesis 𝒫 \mathcal{P} may be arbitrarily distributed under the alternative hypothesis ℚ \mathbb{Q} , and the e-power is a concept concerning the performance of an e-variable under the alternative hypothesis. Therefore, the choice of the null hypothesis 𝒫 \mathcal{P} is irrelevant for defining e-power. For this reason, without loss of generality, we directly consider the candidates for the e-power as mappings Λ ℚ : 𝒳 → ℝ \Lambda^{\mathbb{Q}}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R} , without specifying that e-variables need to satisfy 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ E ] ≤ 1 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[E]\leq 1 for ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} . For Λ ℚ \Lambda^{\mathbb{Q}} to represent the e-power under ℚ \mathbb{Q} , the natural interpretation of Λ ℚ ​ ( E 1 ) > Λ ℚ ​ ( E 2 ) \Lambda^{\mathbb{Q}}(E_{1})>\Lambda^{\mathbb{Q}}(E_{2}) is that E 1 E_{1} is considered as more powerful than E 2 E_{2} . We keep this important interpretation in mind in the following discussions. Suppose that a statistician has designed a two-stage experiment, and observes an e-value of e 0 e_{0} at the first stage. She needs to choose between two e-variables E 1 E_{1} and E 2 E_{2} at second stage. If E 1 E_{1} is more powerful than E 2 E_{2} , then she should choose E 1 E_{1} over E 2 E_{2} . However, the effective e-variables to compare in this procedure are e 0 ​ E 1 e_{0}E_{1} and e 0 ​ E 2 e_{0}E_{2} . Hence, there should be a consistency between the pair ( E 1 , E 2 ) (E_{1},E_{2}) and the pair ( e 0 ​ E 1 , e 0 ​ E 2 ) (e_{0}E_{1},e_{0}E_{2}) , which is formalized by the following axiom. The second axiom concerns the design of e-variables in an asymptotic sense. It states that, to choose between two configurations of iid e-variables, if the product e-process of less powerful configuration has asymptotic power 1 1 , then that from the more powerful configuration should also have asymptotic power 1 1 . This axiom reflects the consideration of the Kelly criterion, which we discussed in Section 3.3 . It turns out that Axioms 3.8 and 3.8 are sufficient to jointly characterize the e-power, which has the form 𝔼 Q ​ [ log ⁡ E ] \mathbb{E}^{Q}[\log E] as we saw in Definition 3.11 . Theorem 3.28 implies that if Axioms 3.8 and 3.8 are reasonable for a notion of e-power, then the only possible way to quantify the e-power is to use a strictly increasing transform of 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ E ] \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log E] , which generates the same ordinal structure as using 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ E ] \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log E] directly. It also shows what the e-power essentially guarantees: It is the most suitable when considering the product e-value with asymptotic consistency (also discussed in Section 3.3 ). A simple example of a strictly increasing transform of the e-power is the geometric mean of the e-variable E E , given by exp ⁡ 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ E ] \exp\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log E] . It is nonnegative, and it is larger than 1 1 if the e-power is positive. In the above result, the e-power 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ E ] \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log E] in Definition 3.11 is justified as a comparative notion. This serves a very similar purpose to the classic notion of power of tests (Definition 3.1 ) when we compare e-variables to determine which ones are more “powerful”. When it comes to the meaning of the actual value, its natural interpretation is the asymptotic growth rate in a multiplicative setting, which is different from the classic notion of power, whose interpretation is the probability of rejection under the alternative. We can easily extend the mapping Λ ℚ \Lambda^{\mathbb{Q}} to the set of all nonnegative random variables, provided that 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ E ] \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log E] is well-defined, that is, at least one of 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ ( log ⁡ E ) + ] \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[(\log E)_{+}] and 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ ( log ⁡ E ) − ] \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[(\log E)_{-}] is finite. for any nonnegative random variable X X . We treat Λ ℚ ​ ( X ) \Lambda^{\mathbb{Q}}(X) as undefined if 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ X ] \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log X] is not well-defined. The next proposition gives some convenient properties of Λ ℚ \Lambda^{\mathbb{Q}} . If Λ ℚ ​ ( E ) > 0 \Lambda^{\mathbb{Q}}(E)>0 , then Λ ℚ ​ ( 1 − λ + λ ​ E ) > 0 \Lambda^{\mathbb{Q}}(1-\lambda+\lambda E)>0 for all λ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] \lambda\in(0,1] . If Λ ℚ ​ ( E ) ≥ 0 \Lambda^{\mathbb{Q}}(E)\geq 0 , then Λ ℚ ​ ( 1 − λ + λ ​ E ) ≥ 0 \Lambda^{\mathbb{Q}}(1-\lambda+\lambda E)\geq 0 for all λ ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] \lambda\in[0,1] . Λ ℚ ​ ( 1 − λ + λ ​ E ) \Lambda^{\mathbb{Q}}(1-\lambda+\lambda E) is always well-defined for λ ∈ [ 0 , 1 ) \lambda\in[0,1) . If Λ ℚ ​ ( E ) < ∞ \Lambda^{\mathbb{Q}}(E)<\infty , then Λ ℚ ​ ( 1 − λ + λ ​ E ) ∈ ℝ \Lambda^{\mathbb{Q}}(1-\lambda+\lambda E)\in\mathbb{R} for all λ ∈ [ 0 , 1 ) \lambda\in[0,1) . The connection between tests and bounded e-variables described in Fact 3.6 was discussed in Koning (2024) in detail. The e-power in Section 3.3 is formally defined in Vovk and Wang (2024b) . Example 3.16 was discussed in Wang (2024a) . The notion of log-optimality was proposed by Kelly (1956) in the context of betting and investment. Larsson et al. (2025a) showed the existence and uniqueness of a log-optimal e-variable with no conditions on 𝒫 , ℚ \mathcal{P},\mathbb{Q} . The result in Section 3.5 essentially dates back to Breiman (1961) , though its presentation here mirrors that in Shafer (2021) . The mixture and plug-in methods to handle composite alternatives, as presented in Section 3.7 , are very well known; in fact, both ideas were already mentioned by Wald (1947) . Later, Robbins and Siegmund (1974) derived some strong connections between these methods. The t-test e-variable is derived and studied in, for example, Lai (1976) , Pérez-Ortiz et al. (2024) , and Wang and Ramdas (2025) , with the latter paper having the e-variable for n = 1 n=1 . The asymptotic optimality of the method of mixtures is strongly related to information-theoretic topics such as redundancy and description length, and many more such optimality results can be inferred using a plethora of results on the latter topic in Grünwald (2007) . The changepoint e-value in Example 3.25 is borrowed from Shin et al. (2024) .

### 10. The log-optimal e-value and reverse information projection / The numeraire e-variable

While the universal inference e-variable (or e-process) exists in quite some generality and is relatively straightforward to construct, it is only known to be asymptotically log-optimal but does not have a finite-sample log-optimality guarantee. It turns out that in even more generality, a log-optimal e-variable exists and is unique. In fact, it always dominates universal inference, rendering the latter method inadmissible. The catch, of course, is that it may not be numerically as easy to construct, but we will be able to characterize it analytically in many cases. This chapter is dedicated to describing a duality theory that is central to designing log-optimal e-variables. The two main characters in this duality are a special e-variable, called the numeraire, and a special (sub-probability) distribution called the reverse information projection (RIPr). Our most general results make no assumptions on ℚ \mathbb{Q} and 𝒫 \mathcal{P} whatsoever, not even the existence of a common reference measure or the minimum KL divergence between ℚ \mathbb{Q} and 𝒫 \mathcal{P} being finite. However, some of these results have sophisticated proofs that are omitted. We will instead prove some of the results in simpler special cases, making some simplifying assumptions. When we use the term e-variable below, we always mean e-variable for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} . We let 𝔈 \mathfrak{E} denote the set of e-variables: A numeraire e-variable (or just numeraire for short) is a ℚ \mathbb{Q} -almost surely strictly positive e-variable E ∗ E^{*} such that 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ E / E ∗ ] ≤ 1 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[E/E^{*}]\leq 1 for every e-variable E ∈ 𝔈 E\in\mathfrak{E} . Let E ∗ E^{*} denote the numeraire for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} , and consider a larger null hypothesis 𝒫 ′ ⊇ 𝒫 \mathcal{P}^{\prime}\supseteq\mathcal{P} . If E ∗ E^{*} is still an e-variable for 𝒫 ′ \mathcal{P}^{\prime} , then it is also the numeraire for 𝒫 ′ \mathcal{P}^{\prime} . Let E ∗ E^{*} be a ℚ \mathbb{Q} -almost surely strictly positive e-variable. Then E ∗ E^{*} is a numeraire if and only if it is log-optimal. In particular, a log-optimal e-variable is unique up to ℚ \mathbb{Q} -nullsets. A numeraire always exists. Moreover, the following conditions are equivalent: For all e-variables E ∈ 𝔈 E\in\mathfrak{E} and distributions ℙ ∈ Conv ⁡ ( 𝒫 ) \mathbb{P}\in\mathrm{Conv}(\mathcal{P}) , we have 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ E ] ≤ KL ⁡ ( ℚ , ℙ ) \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log E]\leq\mathrm{KL}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{P}) (where if 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ E ] \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log E] is undefined, we treat it as − ∞ -\infty ), and thus Suppose 𝒫 = { ℙ 1 , … , ℙ K } \mathcal{P}=\{\mathbb{P}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{P}_{K}\} with ℙ k ≪ ℚ \mathbb{P}_{k}\ll\mathbb{Q} for all k ∈ [ K ] k\in[K] , and min ℙ ∈ Conv ⁡ ( 𝒫 ) ⁡ KL ⁡ ( ℚ , ℙ ) < ∞ \min_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathrm{Conv}(\mathcal{P})}\mathrm{KL}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{P})<\infty . Then, where E ∗ E^{*} is the numeraire and ℙ ∗ \mathbb{P}^{*} achieves the minimum. Let E ∗ E^{*} be the numeraire for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} against ℚ \mathbb{Q} . The reverse information projection of ℚ \mathbb{Q} onto 𝒫 \mathcal{P} , denoted ℙ ∗ \mathbb{P}^{*} , is defined via where the right-hand side is understood to equal zero on { E ∗ = ∞ } \{E^{*}=\infty\} . Suppose the null 𝒫 \mathcal{P} consists of all unit variance Gaussians with mean less than 0 0 . Then the standard Gaussian is not in the convex hull of 𝒫 \mathcal{P} , but it is in 𝒫 ∘ ⁣ ∘ ∩ ℳ 1 \mathcal{P}^{\circ\circ}\cap\mathcal{M}_{1} , which contains all unit variance Gaussians with nonnegative mean (and their mixtures). Let Ω = [ 0 , 1 ] \Omega=[0,1] , and let where δ x \delta_{x} is a point mass at x x . Clearly, 𝒫 \mathcal{P} has no common reference measure. The total variation closure of the convex hull of 𝒫 \mathcal{P} still contains only atomic measures. However, 𝒫 ∘ ⁣ ∘ ∩ ℳ 1 \mathcal{P}^{\circ\circ}\cap\mathcal{M}_{1} contains every bounded distribution on [ 0 , 0.5 ] [0,0.5] . A powered test (or e-variable) for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} against ℚ \mathbb{Q} exists if and only if ℚ ∉ 𝒫 ∘ ⁣ ∘ \mathbb{Q}\notin\mathcal{P}^{\circ\circ} . The reverse information projection of ℚ \mathbb{Q} onto 𝒫 \mathcal{P} lies in the bipolar of the null: ℙ ∗ ∈ 𝒫 ∘ ⁣ ∘ \mathbb{P}^{*}\in\mathcal{P}^{\circ\circ} . Assume ℚ ≪ 𝒫 \mathbb{Q}\ll\mathcal{P} . Letting E ∗ E^{*} be the numeraire and ℙ ∗ \mathbb{P}^{*} the RIPr , one has the strong duality relation where these quantities may equal ∞ \infty . Assume ℚ ≪ 𝒫 \mathbb{Q}\ll\mathcal{P} and let ℙ ∗ \mathbb{P}^{*} be an element of 𝒫 ∘ ⁣ ∘ \mathcal{P}^{\circ\circ} equivalent to ℚ \mathbb{Q} . Let ℙ a \mathbb{P}^{a} denote the absolutely continuous part of ℙ \mathbb{P} with respect to ℚ \mathbb{Q} . The following statements imply each other: If we assume further that all elements of 𝒫 \mathcal{P} are absolutely continuous with respect to a probability measure 𝕃 \mathbb{L} , then all elements of 𝒫 ∘ ⁣ ∘ \mathcal{P}^{\circ\circ} are also absolutely continuous with respect to 𝕃 \mathbb{L} , so that we can identify any ℙ ∈ 𝒫 ∘ ⁣ ∘ \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}^{\circ\circ} with its density p = d ​ ℙ / d ​ 𝕃 p=\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}/\mathrm{d}\mathbb{L} . Further, each of the above conditions is equivalent to Assume that ℚ ≪ 𝒫 \mathbb{Q}\ll\mathcal{P} . Let E ∗ E^{*} be a ℚ \mathbb{Q} -almost surely strictly positive e-variable and let ℙ ∗ \mathbb{P}^{*} be the measure given by d ​ ℙ ∗ / d ​ ℚ = 1 / E ∗ \mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{*}/\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}=1/E^{*} . Then E ∗ E^{*} is a numeraire if and only if ℙ ∗ \mathbb{P}^{*} belongs to the effective null 𝒫 ∘ ⁣ ∘ \mathcal{P}^{\circ\circ} . Thus the numeraire is the only e-variable that is also a likelihood ratio between ℚ \mathbb{Q} and some equivalent element of 𝒫 ∘ ⁣ ∘ \mathcal{P}^{\circ\circ} . Finally, 𝔼 ℙ ∗ ​ [ E ∗ ] = 1 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}^{*}}[E^{*}]=1 and ℙ ∗ \mathbb{P}^{*} is maximal in the sense that no other ℙ ∈ 𝒫 ∘ ⁣ ∘ \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}^{\circ\circ} equivalent to ℚ \mathbb{Q} can satisfy ℙ ⁡ ( A ) ≥ ℙ ∗ ​ ( A ) \mathbb{P}(A)\geq\mathbb{P}^{*}(A) for all A A with strict inequality for some A A . Assume ℚ ≪ 𝒫 \mathbb{Q}\ll\mathcal{P} . Let 𝔈 0 \mathfrak{E}_{0} be a family of [ 0 , ∞ ] [0,\infty] -valued random variables that generates the null hypothesis in the sense that Let E ∗ E^{*} be a ℚ \mathbb{Q} -almost surely strictly positive e-variable such that Then E ∗ E^{*} is the numeraire and the RIPr belongs to 𝒫 \mathcal{P} . Assume that ℚ ≪ 𝒫 \mathbb{Q}\ll\mathcal{P} , and let ℙ ∗ \mathbb{P}^{*} denote the RIPr of ℚ \mathbb{Q} onto 𝒫 \mathcal{P} and let X ∗ = d ​ ℚ / d ​ ℙ ∗ X^{*}=\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}/\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{*} denote the numeraire for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} against ℚ \mathbb{Q} . For any 𝒫 ¯ ⊆ 𝒫 \overline{\mathcal{P}}\subseteq\mathcal{P} , if ℙ ∗ ∈ 𝒫 ¯ ∘ ⁣ ∘ \mathbb{P}^{*}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}^{\circ\circ} , then ℙ ∗ \mathbb{P}^{*} is the RIPr of ℚ \mathbb{Q} onto 𝒫 ¯ \overline{\mathcal{P}} , so X ∗ X^{*} is still the numeraire for 𝒫 ¯ \overline{\mathcal{P}} against ℚ \mathbb{Q} . Let f f be a (potentially random) function such that f ≥ p f\geq p , 𝕃 \mathbb{L} -almost surely, for all p ∈ 𝒫 p\in\mathcal{P} . Then f ≥ p f\geq p , 𝕃 \mathbb{L} -almost surely, for all p ∈ 𝒫 ∘ ⁣ ∘ p\in\mathcal{P}^{\circ\circ} . We first give the definition of the numeraire e-variable, which is closely connected to the log-optimal e-variable in Section 3.5 . Numeraires are unique up to ℚ \mathbb{Q} -nullsets. Indeed, if E 1 ∗ E^{*}_{1} and E 2 ∗ E^{*}_{2} are numeraires, then the ratio Y = E 2 ∗ / E 1 ∗ Y=E^{*}_{2}/E^{*}_{1} satisfies 1 ≤ 1 / 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ Y ] ≤ 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ 1 / Y ] ≤ 1 1\leq 1/\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[Y]\leq\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[1/Y]\leq 1 thanks to the numeraire property of E 1 ∗ E^{*}_{1} , Jensen’s inequality, and the numeraire property of E 2 ∗ E^{*}_{2} . Thus Jensen’s inequality holds with equality, so Y Y is ℚ \mathbb{Q} -almost surely equal to a constant which must be one. It follows that E 1 ∗ E^{*}_{1} and E 2 ∗ E^{*}_{2} are ℚ \mathbb{Q} -almost surely equal. In view of this uniqueness, we often speak of ‘the’ numeraire. In the case of a simple null 𝒫 = { ℙ } \mathcal{P}=\{\mathbb{P}\} with ℚ ≪ ℙ \mathbb{Q}\ll\mathbb{P} , the numeraire is just the likelihood ratio E ∗ = d ​ ℚ / d ​ ℙ E^{*}=\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}/\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P} . Indeed, E ∗ E^{*} is ℚ \mathbb{Q} -almost surely strictly positive, it is an e-variable, and for any other e-variable E ∈ 𝔈 E\in\mathfrak{E} we have by a simple change of measure that 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ E / E ∗ ] = 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ E ] ≤ 1 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[E/E^{*}]=\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[E]\leq 1 . Even for composite nulls, the numeraire is a likelihood ratio of ℚ \mathbb{Q} to the ‘reverse information projection’. Before getting there, we present a few other simple results. The proof is simple. If E E is an e-variable for 𝒫 ′ \mathcal{P}^{\prime} , it is also an e-variable for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} and one has 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ E / E ∗ ] ≤ 1 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[E/E^{*}]\leq 1 by assumption, proving the lemma. A numeraire E ∗ E^{*} is also log-optimal (Section 3.5 ) in the sense that where the left-hand side may be − ∞ -\infty . This follows directly from Jensen’s inequality and the numeraire property. The converse is also true, and we record the equivalence in the following proposition. The proof shows that the numeraire property is really the first-order condition for log-optimality. Note also that a numeraire E ∗ E^{*} is the ℚ \mathbb{Q} -almost surely unique log-optimal e-variable in the sense of ( 6.1 ) even if 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ E ∗ ] \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log E^{*}] happens to be infinite. The following result says that a numeraire always exists without any assumptions whatsoever on 𝒫 \mathcal{P} or ℚ \mathbb{Q} . Although no assumptions on 𝒫 \mathcal{P} or ℚ \mathbb{Q} are needed in general, we do not present this more general theory for reasons of brevity. It turns out that the theory simplifies under the following condition, which, despite being nontrivial, is still very weak. We say that ℚ \mathbb{Q} is absolutely continuous with respect to 𝒫 \mathcal{P} , written ℚ ≪ 𝒫 , \mathbb{Q}\ll\mathcal{P}, if ℚ \mathbb{Q} does not assign positive probability to any event that is null under every ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} . Equivalently, This natural generalization of absolute continuity for pairs of measures is satisfied in most statistically relevant settings, while being significantly weaker than requiring a common dominating reference measure. It is also weaker than requiring that ℚ ≪ ℙ \mathbb{Q}\ll\mathbb{P} for every ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} , which would require that ℚ ⁡ ( A ) = 0 \mathbb{Q}(A)=0 as long as any (as opposed to every) ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} assigns ℙ ⁡ ( A ) = 0 \mathbb{P}(A)=0 (or contrapositively, that ℚ ⁡ ( A ) > 0 \mathbb{Q}(A)>0 implies ℙ ⁡ ( A ) > 0 \mathbb{P}(A)>0 for all ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} ). The numeraire is ℚ \mathbb{Q} -almost surely finite. Every e-variable is ℚ \mathbb{Q} -almost surely finite. ℚ \mathbb{Q} is absolutely continuous with respect to 𝒫 \mathcal{P} , that is ℚ ≪ 𝒫 \mathbb{Q}\ll\mathcal{P} . We end with a few additional properties of the numeraire. Since the constant one is always an e-variable, the numeraire E ∗ E^{*} satisfies the aesthetically pleasing property that When 𝒫 = { ℙ } \mathcal{P}=\{\mathbb{P}\} is simple and ℚ ≪ ℙ \mathbb{Q}\ll\mathbb{P} , both inequalities above hold with equality, attained by the numeraire d ​ ℚ / d ​ ℙ \mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}/\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P} . Another pleasing property is obtained by Jensen’s inequality: The numeraire E ∗ E^{*} satisfies Finally, note that the definition of KL divergence in ( 3.4 ) can be rewritten and generalized to nonnegative measures ℙ \mathbb{P} in the following manner. Let ℳ + \mathcal{M}_{+} denote the set of all nonnegative measures on Ω \Omega . Then, for any ℙ ∈ ℳ + \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{M}_{+} and ℚ ∈ ℳ 1 \mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{M}_{1} , define where ℙ a \mathbb{P}^{a} is the absolutely continuous part of ℙ \mathbb{P} with respect to ℚ \mathbb{Q} . When ℙ ∈ ℳ 1 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{M}_{1} , this definition coincides with ( 3.4 ). Note that both sides of ( 6.4 ) are nonnegative and well defined, since the constant 1 1 is always an e-variable. ### 11. Strong duality for finite 𝒫 \mathcal{P} / Reverse information projection / The effective null hypothesis is the bipolar of 𝒫 \mathcal{P} / Numeraire is the only e-variable that is a likelihood ratio

We start here with the case that 𝒫 \mathcal{P} is finite. Then, it is possible to show the following result. Recall that Δ K \Delta_{K} denotes the probability simplex. To elaborate, we know that the numeraire achieves the left hand side ( ℚ \mathbb{Q} -almost surely uniquely), so the first equality holds by its definition. The minimization problem can be rewritten as This is a (strictly) convex minimization problem over the compact convex set Δ K \Delta_{K} , so it achieves its minimum uniquely at some λ ∗ ∈ Δ K \lambda^{*}\in\Delta_{K} , and we define the reverse information projection (RIPr) to be so the last equality also holds by definition. Since KL ⁡ ( ℚ , ℙ ∗ ) < ∞ \mathrm{KL}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{P}^{*})<\infty by assumption, we must have ℚ ≪ ℙ ∗ \mathbb{Q}\ll\mathbb{P}^{*} . We now prove the strong duality result in the middle equality. Remarkably, all assumptions in Proposition 6.6 can be dropped. But doing this is very challenging, as explained below. First, it is clear that in the previous section, ℙ ∗ \mathbb{P}^{*} is the closest distribution in Conv ⁡ ( 𝒫 ) \mathrm{Conv}(\mathcal{P}) to ℚ \mathbb{Q} as measured by KL. However, when the infimum KL divergence equals infinity, it is unclear how do define the “closest” distribution to ℚ \mathbb{Q} . Second, in the previous section, we used the compactness of Conv ⁡ ( 𝒫 ) \mathrm{Conv}(\mathcal{P}) when 𝒫 \mathcal{P} is finite to argue that the infimum is attained by a distribution in Conv ⁡ ( 𝒫 ) \mathrm{Conv}(\mathcal{P}) . However, when 𝒫 \mathcal{P} is uncountable (as would typically be the case in statistical applications), the set is no longer compact (with respect to the weak topology) and it is not clear that the infimum is achieved inside the set. Indeed, it is typically the case that the minimizer lies outside of Conv ⁡ ( 𝒫 ) \mathrm{Conv}(\mathcal{P}) , and in fact may be a sub distribution in general (a nonnegative measure that integrates to less than one). In that case, where does it lie, and how do we characterize it? Finally, when all absolute continuity requirements are dropped, all arguments involving likelihood ratios (Radon–Nikodym derivatives) and KL divergences become much more subtle, and one cannot proceed as easily as in the previous section. Since formally handling the above subtleties requires some technical measure-theoretic care, we only state the results here but do not prove them. The first step is to define the reverse information projection (RIPr) appropriately. In words, ℙ ∗ \mathbb{P}^{*} is a nonnegative measure whose likelihood ratio with respect to ℚ \mathbb{Q} equals 1 / E ∗ 1/E^{*} . Since E ∗ E^{*} exists, and is ℚ \mathbb{Q} -almost surely unique and positive, we see that ℙ ∗ \mathbb{P}^{*} is uniquely defined up to ℚ \mathbb{Q} -nullsets. Also, ℙ ∗ ≪ ℚ \mathbb{P}^{*}\ll\mathbb{Q} by definition. Moreover, ℙ ∗ ​ ( Ω ) ≤ 1 \mathbb{P}^{*}(\Omega)\leq 1 because 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ 1 / E ∗ ] ≤ 1 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[1/E^{*}]\leq 1 , and hence ℙ ∗ \mathbb{P}^{*} is in general a sub-probability measure, though it is a probability measure in some special cases. 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ d ​ ℙ a d ​ ℙ ∗ ] ≤ 1 ​ for all ​ ℙ ∈ 𝒫 ∘ ⁣ ∘ \displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{a}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{*}}\right]\leq 1\text{ for all }\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}^{\circ\circ} 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ d ​ ℙ a d ​ ℙ ∗ ] ≤ 0 ​ for all ​ ℙ ∈ 𝒫 ∘ ⁣ ∘ \displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}\left[\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{a}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{*}}\right]\leq 0\text{ for all }\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}^{\circ\circ} . 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ p / p ∗ ] ≤ 1 ​ for all densities p of ℙ ∈ 𝒫 . \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}\left[p/p^{*}\right]\leq 1\text{ for all densities $p$ of $\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}$.} Let 𝒳 + \mathcal{X}_{+} denote the set of all [ 0 , ∞ ] [0,\infty] -valued measurable functions on Ω \Omega and recall that ℳ + \mathcal{M}_{+} denotes the set of all nonnegative measures on Ω \Omega . The polar of 𝒫 \mathcal{P} is defined by This is precisely the set of e-variables, i.e., 𝒫 ∘ = 𝔈 \mathcal{P}^{\circ}=\mathfrak{E} . The bipolar of 𝒫 \mathcal{P} is Equality holds when 𝒫 \mathcal{P} is finite, but the inclusion can be strict. Because the constant E = 1 E=1 is always an e-variable, elements of 𝒫 ∘ ⁣ ∘ \mathcal{P}^{\circ\circ} have total mass at most one, and sometimes strictly less than one. For example, if ℙ \mathbb{P} belongs to 𝒫 ∘ ⁣ ∘ \mathcal{P}^{\circ\circ} , then so does any ℙ ′ ∈ ℳ + \mathbb{P}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{+} that is set-wise dominated by ℙ \mathbb{P} , meaning ℙ ′ ​ ( A ) ≤ ℙ ⁡ ( A ) \mathbb{P}^{\prime}(A)\leq\mathbb{P}(A) for all A A . Below we give two examples where 𝒫 ∘ ⁣ ∘ ∩ ℳ 1 \mathcal{P}^{\circ\circ}\cap\mathcal{M}_{1} is a strict superset of Conv ⁡ ( 𝒫 ) . \mathrm{Conv}(\mathcal{P}). The reader may note that if we had considered the total variation closure of the convex hull of 𝒫 \mathcal{P} (motivated by Theorem 3.17 ) in Example 6.8 , it would have coincided with 𝒫 ∘ ⁣ ∘ ∩ ℳ 1 \mathcal{P}^{\circ\circ}\cap\mathcal{M}_{1} . However, more generally, this is not the case. In the above example, if, instead of total variation closure, we had taken a weak closure of the convex hull of 𝒫 \mathcal{P} , then again we would have obtained 𝒫 ∘ ⁣ ∘ ∩ ℳ 1 \mathcal{P}^{\circ\circ}\cap\mathcal{M}_{1} . However, one can construct yet other examples to show that this is not true in general, but this gets rather deep into topological measure theory, and is out of the scope of the current book. The bipolar of 𝒫 \mathcal{P} can be interpreted as the effective null hypothesis : it is the largest family of distributions whose set of e-variables is exactly 𝔈 \mathfrak{E} . Thus it consists of those distributions against which e-variables in 𝔈 \mathfrak{E} do not provide any evidence. In particular, we note the following simple yet important consequence, recalling the definition of a powered e-variable from Definition 3.1 . It turns out that considering only bounded e-variables suffices. Let 𝔈 b ⊊ 𝒫 ∘ = 𝔈 \mathfrak{E}^{b}\subsetneq\mathcal{P}^{\circ}=\mathfrak{E} be defined as 𝔈 b = { E ∈ 𝒳 + : 𝔼 ℙ [ E ] ≤ 1 for all ℙ ∈ 𝒫 , sup ω ∈ Ω E ( ω ) < ∞ } \mathfrak{E}^{b}=\{E\in\mathcal{X}_{+}\colon\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[E]\leq 1\text{ for all }\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P},~\sup_{\omega\in\Omega}E(\omega)<\infty\} . Then an application of the monotone convergence theorem yields that We now claim that even though it is possible that ℙ ∗ ∉ Conv ⁡ ( 𝒫 ) \mathbb{P}^{*}\notin\mathrm{Conv}(\mathcal{P}) in general, it does lie in 𝒫 ∘ ⁣ ∘ \mathcal{P}^{\circ\circ} . The proof follows because for any e-variable E E , because E ∗ E^{*} is the numeraire. The above result can be visualized as Figure 6.12 . We first present a verification theorem that simplifies the task of checking that candidates E ∗ E^{*} and ℙ ∗ \mathbb{P}^{*} (for the numeraire and RIPr respectively) are in fact optimal. We stress that the above result is quite remarkable: the numeraire is clearly a likelihood ratio between ℚ \mathbb{Q} and some equivalent element of 𝒫 ∘ ⁣ ∘ \mathcal{P}^{\circ\circ} , but it is also the unique e-variable with such a representation! This property helps identify the numeraire in several simple examples (eg: Sections 6.7.1 and 6.7.2 ), where we guess the numeraire as being a particular simple likelihood ratio of the alternative to some “extreme” point of the null, verify that it is indeed an e-variable, and conclude that it is in fact the log-optimal e-variable. Thanks to Lemma 6.2 , Corollary 6.16 also holds if one has ‘ ⊇ \supseteq ’ instead of ‘ = = ’ in ( 6.7 ). This is intuitive: if one enlarges the generating set 𝔈 0 \mathfrak{E}_{0} beyond what is necessary to specify 𝒫 \mathcal{P} , then the right-hand side of ( 6.7 ) can become smaller than 𝒫 \mathcal{P} . In that case, ( 6.8 ) only gets harder to satisfy, and if we find an e-variable E ∗ E^{*} satisfying it, it surely must still be the numeraire. Another useful corollary is the following shrinking lemma , a dual result to the lifting lemma (Lemma 6.2 ). As an example of how one might apply the shrinking and lifting lemmata referenced above, consider 𝒫 \mathcal{P} being unit-variance Gaussians with nonpositive mean, and ℚ \mathbb{Q} being N ⁡ ( 1 , 1 ) N(1,1) . It can be easily checked that the RIPr is the standard Gaussian, and the numeraire is the likelihood ratio of N ⁡ ( 1 , 1 ) N(1,1) to N ⁡ ( 0 , 1 ) N(0,1) . The shrinking lemma implies that if we consider any subset of 𝒫 \mathcal{P} that still contains N ⁡ ( 0 , 1 ) N(0,1) , the RIPr and numeraire remain identical. We could have also gone in the other direction (from the singleton null of N ⁡ ( 0 , 1 ) N(0,1) to the one-sided null 𝒫 \mathcal{P} ), and used the lifting lemma instead to reach the same conclusions.

<sub>§6.2 Strong duality for finite 𝒫 \mathcal{P} · §6.3 Reverse information projection · §6.3.1 The effective null hypothesis is the bipolar of 𝒫 \mathcal{P} · §6.4 Numeraire is the only e-variable that is a likelihood ratio</sub>

### 12. Numeraire dominates universal inference / Connections between e-values and Bayes factors

Assume now that there exists a common reference measure 𝕃 \mathbb{L} , and identify distributions with their respective densities, written in lowercase. For a point alternative q q over the data Z Z , the method of universal inference boils down to constructing the e-variable E UI = q ⁡ ( Z ) / p max ​ ( Z ) E^{\mathrm{UI}}=q(Z)/p_{\text{max}}(Z) where p max ​ ( Z ) = sup p ∈ 𝒫 p ⁡ ( Z ) p_{\text{max}}(Z)=\sup_{p\in\mathcal{P}}p(Z) is the maximum likelihood. (Strictly speaking, the supremum here should be understood as an essential supremum under the reference measure.) To compare the universal inference e-variable with the numeraire E ∗ = q ⁡ ( Z ) / p ∗ ​ ( Z ) E^{*}=q(Z)/p^{*}(Z) , we first claim that the RIPr satisfies p ∗ ​ ( Z ) ≤ p max ​ ( Z ) p^{*}(Z)\leq p_{\text{max}}(Z) up to 𝕃 \mathbb{L} -nullsets. This is obvious if the RIPr belongs to 𝒫 \mathcal{P} , but in general it only belongs to 𝒫 ∘ ⁣ ∘ \mathcal{P}^{\circ\circ} . In this case the claim follows by applying the following lemma with f = p max f=p_{\text{max}} . We omit the technical proof. As an immediate consequence of the lemma, we see that p ∗ ​ ( Z ) ≤ p max ​ ( Z ) p^{*}(Z)\leq p_{\text{max}}(Z) , and hence E ∗ ≥ E UI E^{*}\geq E^{\mathrm{UI}} , up to 𝕃 \mathbb{L} -nullsets. In nondegenerate situations involving a composite null hypothesis, the inequality will be strict with positive 𝕃 \mathbb{L} -probability. Thus, in such cases, the relatively general method of universal inference is in fact inadmissible. We end by noting that the numeraire imposes weaker assumptions than universal inference (and thus is, in some sense, even more universal), since the latter needs a reference measure to define likelihoods. We end with a note on logical coherence. Saying that a composite null 𝒫 \mathcal{P} is true is equivalent to saying that a simple null ℙ \mathbb{P} is true for some ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} . Thus, one may expect to only reject 𝒫 \mathcal{P} if one can also reject every ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} separately. A method that satisfies this property is called logically coherent. We can extend this definition to e-variables as well. Let E 𝒫 E^{\mathcal{P}} denote an e-variable for testing 𝒫 \mathcal{P} . The collection ( E 𝒫 ) 𝒫 ⊆ ℳ 1 (E^{\mathcal{P}})_{\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{1}} can be called coherent if E 𝒫 ≤ E 𝒫 ′ E^{\mathcal{P}}\leq E^{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}} whenever 𝒫 ′ ⊆ 𝒫 \mathcal{P}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{P} . Fixing an alternative ℚ \mathbb{Q} for simplicity, we now make two simple observations, stated here without proof: If E 𝒫 E^{\mathcal{P}} equals the universal inference e-variable for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} against ℚ \mathbb{Q} , the collection ( E 𝒫 ) 𝒫 ⊆ ℳ 1 (E^{\mathcal{P}})_{\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{1}} is always logically coherent. (This point is easy to verify by definition.) If E 𝒫 E^{\mathcal{P}} equals the numeraire e-variable for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} against ℚ \mathbb{Q} , the collection ( E 𝒫 ) 𝒫 ⊆ ℳ 1 (E^{\mathcal{P}})_{\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{1}} is generally not logically coherent, except for some special cases. Incoherent families of e-variables may be harder to interpret, because enlarging the null may counterintuitively increase the evidence against the null. Thus, there appears to be a tradeoff between e-power and coherence/interpretability when comparing universal inference and the numeraire. For those Bayesians who are willing to consider testing hypotheses, one of the standard quantities to examine is the Bayes factor . Suppose we observe n n iid data points X 1 , … , X n X_{1},\dots,X_{n} , and we are interested in testing whether their marginal distribution lies in a composite null 𝒫 \mathcal{P} or in a composite alternative 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} . Also suppose that there is a common reference measure, allowing us to identify distributions with their densities. Then the Bayes factor is defined as where μ \mu and ν \nu are (often subjective) priors over 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} and 𝒫 \mathcal{P} . Bayesians often refer to tables like in Section 2.7 in order to interpret the realized value, and typical Bayesians do not threshold B n B_{n} to make clear decisions of accepting or rejecting the null, and consequently no effort is made to control the type-I error. The law of iterated expectation yields that B n B_{n} is an e-variable if nature draws a random null from the scientist’s prior — 𝔼 p ∼ ν ⁡ ( d ​ p ) ​ [ B n ] ≤ 1 \mathbb{E}^{p\sim\nu(\mathrm{d}p)}[B_{n}]\leq 1 —but it remains unclear to many why this is a reasonable assumption. Indeed, this is often the problem with Bayesian methods: the scientist using them is perfectly convinced of its correctness (internal consistency) but their analysis fails to convince all others (lack of external consistency). Thus the main drawbacks of this approach are: (a) it is unclear how to generalize these ideas to nonparametric settings without densities and likelihoods, (b) it is unclear how to formally make decisions with B n B_{n} with (frequentist) error guarantees. In contrast, the typical frequentist approach in the above setting would be to consider the generalized likelihood ratio: Under some regularity conditions, Wilks’ theorem implies that log ⁡ G n \log G_{n} has a chi-squared limiting distribution as n → ∞ n\to\infty , with degrees of freedom determined by some notion of parametric dimensionality difference between 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} and 𝒫 \mathcal{P} (for the discussion here we allow 𝒫 \mathcal{P} and 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} to be nested, which is different from the main setting of the book). This is then used to construct an asymptotic p-value. However, the main drawback here is once more (a) it is unclear how to generalize these ideas to nonparametric settings without densities and likelihoods, and (b) what to do when the regularity conditions for Wilks’ theorem fails, and the asymptotics are unwieldy. In contrast, we note that universal inference takes a middle ground between the above two approaches, using a mixture (i.e. a prior without Bayesian connotations) over the alternative, but a maximum likelihood over the null: which is always an e-variable, under no regularity conditions. However, there are still two drawbacks (a) the previous section shows that this is not optimal, and (b) we still require reference measures and densities to define likelihoods, so nonparametric extensions are not immediately obvious. The numeraire resolves these issues. This sections shows that one should calculate the RIPr ℙ ∗ \mathbb{P}^{*} of ℚ eff := ∫ 𝒬 ℚ n ​ μ ​ ( 𝑑 ℚ ) \mathbb{Q}^{\text{eff}}:=\int_{\mathcal{Q}}\mathbb{Q}^{n}\mu(\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}) onto (the bipolar of) { ℙ n : ℙ ∈ 𝒫 } \{\mathbb{P}^{n}:\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}\} . Since ℙ ∗ ≪ ℚ eff \mathbb{P}^{*}\ll\mathbb{Q}^{\text{eff}} by definition, we can define This is always well defined, even in nonparametric settings with no reference measures for 𝒫 , 𝒬 \mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q} , it is always an e-variable (thus guaranteeing the frequentist error control), and it dominates U n U_{n} (Section 6.5 ). In some situations, the RIPr will actually lie in the convex hull (set of mixtures) of { ℙ n : ℙ ∈ 𝒫 } \{\mathbb{P}^{n}:\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}\} , and in this case, the numeraire can be written in the form of B n B_{n} . However, for every choice of alternative mixture, there is a unique mixture over the nulls that would yield such a representation (Section 6.4 ), meaning that one cannot decouple the pair of mixtures as the Bayesian did for B n B_{n} . In these cases, the numeraire can be seen as the unique Bayes factor which is also an e-variable. Of course, when the null and alternative are simple, all the above methods coincide. When only the null is simple, the Bayes factor, the universal inference e-variable, and the numeraire coincide. When neither is simple, the Bayes factor is sometimes (but usually not) an e-variable, the numeraire is sometimes (but usually not) a Bayes factor, and the generalized likelihood ratio is neither an e-variable nor a Bayes factor in general.

### 13. Examples / Exponential family with one-dimensional sufficient statistic / Monotone likelihood ratio / Testing symmetry

We now turn to examples of numeraire e-variables, revisiting several examples in Section 2.6 . Consider a single random observation (statistic) Z Z in a measurable space 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} , and we take ( Ω , ℱ ) (\Omega,\mathcal{F}) to be 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} with its σ \sigma -algebra σ ⁡ ( Z ) \sigma(Z) . The example here will be subsumed by the (much shorter) Section 6.7.2 . Take 𝒵 = ℝ \mathcal{Z}=\mathbb{R} . As in Section 2.6.2 , we consider the null hypothesis that Z Z is symmetric, which is a non-dominated family.
