If ( 𝔢 t ) (\mathfrak{e}_{t}) is a 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -e-process, then ( 1 ∧ inf s ≤ t 1 / 𝔢 s ) (1\wedge\inf_{s\leq t}1/\mathfrak{e}_{s}) is a 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -p-process. If { ( ψ t ​ ( α ) ) } α ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] \{(\psi_{t}(\alpha))\}_{\alpha\in[0,1]} is a nested family of 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -STs, then ( inf { α : ψ t ​ ( α ) = 1 } ) (\inf\{\alpha:\psi_{t}(\alpha)=1\}) is a 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -p-process. If { ( ℭ t ​ ( α ) ) } α ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] \{(\mathfrak{C}_{t}(\alpha))\}_{\alpha\in[0,1]} is a nested family of ( ϕ , 𝒫 ) (\phi,\mathcal{P}) -CSs, then ( inf { α : ϕ ⁡ ( 𝒬 ) ∩ ℭ t ​ ( α ) = ∅ } ) (\inf\{\alpha:\phi(\mathcal{Q})\cap\mathfrak{C}_{t}(\alpha)=\emptyset\}) is a 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -p-process. An e-process for 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} is also automatically an e-process for conv ⁡ ( 𝒬 ) \rm{conv}(\mathcal{Q}) . Such closure under the convex hull also holds for p-processes and sequential tests. An admissible e-process for 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} is also automatically an admissible e-process for conv ⁡ ( 𝒬 ) \rm{conv}(\mathcal{Q}) . Such closure under the convex hull also holds for p-processes and sequential tests.

<sub>§4 Tools for anytime sequential inference and their admissibility</sub>

### 6. 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -p-processes, 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -e-processes, and ( 𝒬 , α ) (\mathcal{Q},\alpha) -sequential tests

Let the (unknown) distribution of the observed data sequence be denoted 𝙿 \mathtt{P} . Suppose we wish to test the null H 0 : 𝙿 ∈ 𝒬 H_{0}:\mathtt{P}\in\mathcal{Q} for some 𝒬 ⊂ 𝒫 \mathcal{Q}\subset\mathcal{P} , against H 1 : 𝙿 ∈ 𝒫 H_{1}:\mathtt{P}\in\mathcal{P} (or against H 1 : 𝙿 ∈ 𝒫 \ 𝒬 H_{1}:\mathtt{P}\in\mathcal{P}\backslash\mathcal{Q} ). A sequence ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) is called a p-process for 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} if it satisfies Above, we have implicitly defined and used For succinctness, we say ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) is a 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -p-process , or simply a p-process if 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} is clear from context. By Lemma 2 , the above condition is equivalent to requiring that Note that ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) is a p-process if and only if the running infimum ( inf s ≤ t 𝔭 s ) (\inf_{s\leq t}\mathfrak{p}_{s}) is a p-process, so it helps to think of ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) as a nonincreasing sequence; in this case, we have 𝔭 ∞ = lim t → ∞ 𝔭 t \mathfrak{p}_{\infty}=\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathfrak{p}_{t} (the limit exists and equals the lim inf \liminf used earlier). When we refer only to the validity of the single random variable 𝔭 t \mathfrak{p}_{t} and not the sequence ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) , we say that 𝔭 t \mathfrak{p}_{t} is a p-value for 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} , meaning that its distribution is stochastically larger than uniform under any 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} . The connection to Ville’s inequality ( 7a ), and thus to martingales, should be apparent. A sequence ( 𝔢 t ) (\mathfrak{e}_{t}) is called an e-process for 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} , or a 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -e-process , if Analogous to the p-process case above, we have implicitly defined and used In fact, e-processes themselves can be defined in many equivalent ways, and we record this below. Property (i) is used as the defining property in Grünwald et al. [9] , while (vi) is the defining property used in Howard et al. [10] , and while these seem to be visually different, they are in fact identical. The proof below establishes that property (iv) implies (v) , by constructing the Snell envelope (under each 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} ) for ( 𝔢 t ) (\mathfrak{e}_{t}) , and taking its Doob decomposition. The optional stopping theorem for nonnegative supermartingales establishes that property (vi) implies (i) . All other implications are straightforward. The implications (i) ⇒ \Rightarrow (ii) ⇒ \Rightarrow (iii) ⇒ \Rightarrow (iv) and (v) ⇒ \Rightarrow (vi) ⇒ \Rightarrow (i) are immediate. We thus focus on the non-trivial implication (iv) ⇒ \Rightarrow (v) . To do so we fix any 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} and define the Snell envelope of ( 𝔢 t ) (\mathfrak{e}_{t}) as the process ( L t ) (L_{t}) given by where τ \tau ranges over all bounded stopping times. It is clear that L t ≥ 𝔢 t L_{t}\geq\mathfrak{e}_{t} since τ = t \tau=t is one of the stopping time occurring in the essential supremum, for all t ∈ ℕ 0 t\in\mathbb{N}_{0} . We claim that ( L t ) (L_{t}) is a 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -supermartingale with 𝙴 𝚀 ​ [ L 0 ] ≤ 1 \mathtt{E}_{\mathtt{Q}}[L_{0}]\leq 1 . The supermartingale property of the Snell envelope is well-known, but for the convenience of the reader we include the short proof. This uses properties of the essential supremum reviewed in Appendix A.2 , in particular Proposition 45 . For each fixed t ∈ ℕ 0 t\in\mathbb{N}_{0} , L t L_{t} is the essential supremum of the family consisting of all 𝙴 𝚀 ​ [ M τ | 𝒢 t ] \mathtt{E}_{\mathtt{Q}}[M_{\tau}|\mathcal{G}_{t}] where τ ≥ t \tau\geq t is a bounded stopping time. This family is closed under maxima. To see this, let τ \tau and τ ′ \tau^{\prime} be two bounded stopping times, define A = { 𝙴 𝚀 [ 𝔢 τ | 𝒢 t ] > 𝙴 𝚀 [ 𝔢 τ ′ ∣ 𝒢 t ] } A=\{\mathtt{E}_{\mathtt{Q}}[\mathfrak{e}_{\tau}|\mathcal{G}_{t}]>\mathtt{E}_{\mathtt{Q}}[\mathfrak{e}_{\tau^{\prime}}\mid\mathcal{G}_{t}]\} , and set τ ′′ = τ ​ 𝟏 A + τ ′ ​ 𝟏 A c \tau^{\prime\prime}=\tau\mathbf{1}_{A}+\tau^{\prime}\mathbf{1}_{A^{c}} . Since A A lies in 𝒢 t \mathcal{G}_{t} and τ , τ ′ ≥ t \tau,\tau^{\prime}\geq t , we have that τ ′′ \tau^{\prime\prime} is a (bounded) stopping time and we obtain This demonstrates closure under maxima. Consequently we can apply Proposition 45 to obtain a sequence of bounded stopping times { τ n } n ∈ ℕ \{\tau_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}} with τ n ≥ t \tau_{n}\geq t such that 𝙴 𝚀 ​ [ 𝔢 τ n | 𝒢 t ] ↑ L t \mathtt{E}_{\mathtt{Q}}[\mathfrak{e}_{\tau_{n}}|\mathcal{G}_{t}]\uparrow L_{t} almost surely. The conditional version of the monotone convergence theorem, the tower rule, and the definition of L t − 1 L_{t-1} , then yield Note that this argument applies even with t = 0 t=0 , where we use the convention that 𝒢 − 1 \mathcal{G}_{-1} is the trivial sigma-field and L − 1 = 1 L_{-1}=1 . This is where the hypothesis (iv) is used: in the last inequality we have 𝙴 𝚀 ​ [ 𝔢 τ n ] ≤ 1 \mathtt{E}_{\mathtt{Q}}[\mathfrak{e}_{\tau_{n}}]\leq 1 . We conclude that 𝙴 𝚀 ​ [ L 0 ] ≤ 1 \mathtt{E}_{\mathtt{Q}}[L_{0}]\leq 1 , that each L t L_{t} is integrable, and that ( L t ) (L_{t}) is a supermartingale. Since we have established that the Snell envelope ( L t ) (L_{t}) is a supermartingale we can write down its Doob decomposition as L t = M t − A t L_{t}=M_{t}-A_{t} for a unique 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -martingale ( M t ) (M_{t}) with M 0 = L 0 M_{0}=L_{0} , and a unique nondecreasing predictable process ( A t ) (A_{t}) with A 0 = 0 A_{0}=0 . Thus M t ≥ L t ≥ 𝔢 t M_{t}\geq L_{t}\geq\mathfrak{e}_{t} 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -almost surely for all t ∈ ℕ 0 t\in\mathbb{N}_{0} , showing that ( M t ) (M_{t}) is the required 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -NM that upper bounds ( 𝔢 t ) (\mathfrak{e}_{t}) . ∎ It is worth noting that already in the case of a singleton 𝒬 := { 𝚀 } \mathcal{Q}:=\{\mathtt{Q}\} , if one additionally desired a conditional safety property to hold for ( 𝔢 t ) (\mathfrak{e}_{t}) , namely that 𝙴 𝚀 ​ [ 𝔢 τ | ℱ s ] ≤ 𝔢 s ∧ τ \mathtt{E}_{\mathtt{Q}}[\mathfrak{e}_{\tau}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]\leq\mathfrak{e}_{s\wedge\tau} for arbitrary times s s and stopping times τ \tau , then ( 𝔢 t ) (\mathfrak{e}_{t}) must necessarily be a 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -NSM; indeed by taking τ = t \tau=t for t ≥ s t\geq s , we recover the definition of a 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -NSM. In fact, if one would like such a property to hold for every 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} in a composite 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} , such a requirement can only be satisfied by a 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -NSM. Despite the fact that we will not require this conditional property, we will see that (composite) 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -NMs or (pointwise) 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -NMs play a central, universal role in constructing e-processes that may not themselves be martingales. A p-process or e-process does not directly yield a decision rule for when to reject the null hypothesis; they are real-valued measures of evidence, and need to be coupled with a decision rule in order to yield a test. A binary sequence ( ψ t ) (\psi_{t}) is called a ( 𝒬 , α ) (\mathcal{Q},\alpha) -sequential test for H 0 H_{0} —or ( ψ t ) (\psi_{t}) is a ( 𝒬 , α ) (\mathcal{Q},\alpha) -ST —if As with p-processes, we think of ( ψ t ) (\psi_{t}) as nondecreasing, and have implicitly defined ψ ∞ := lim t → ∞ ψ t \psi_{\infty}:=\lim_{t\to\infty}\psi_{t} .

<sub>§4.1 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -p-processes, 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -e-processes, and ( 𝒬 , α ) (\mathcal{Q},\alpha) -sequential tests</sub>

### 7. Defining inadmissibility and admissibility

We follow the standard convention of using the term ‘admissible’ as a shorthand for ‘not inadmissible’, so we only define inadmissibility below. We say that ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) is inadmissible if there exists ( 𝔭 t ′ ) (\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}_{t}) that is a 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -p-process, and is always at least as good but sometimes strictly better; more formally, 𝚀 ⁡ ( 𝔭 t ′ ≤ 𝔭 t ) = 1 \mathtt{Q}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}_{t}\leq\mathfrak{p}_{t})=1 for all t ∈ ℕ 0 t\in\mathbb{N}_{0} and all 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} , and that 𝚀 ⁡ ( 𝔭 t ′ < 𝔭 t ) > 0 \mathtt{Q}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}_{t}<\mathfrak{p}_{t})>0 for some t ∈ ℕ 0 t\in\mathbb{N}_{0} , and some 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} . We say that ( 𝔢 t ) (\mathfrak{e}_{t}) is inadmissible if there exists a 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -e-process ( 𝔢 t ′ ) (\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{t}) , such that 𝚀 ⁡ ( 𝔢 t ′ ≥ 𝔢 t ) = 1 \mathtt{Q}(\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{t}\geq\mathfrak{e}_{t})=1 for all t ∈ ℕ 0 t\in\mathbb{N}_{0} and all 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} , and also 𝚀 ⁡ ( 𝔢 t ′ > 𝔢 t ) > 0 \mathtt{Q}(\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{t}>\mathfrak{e}_{t})>0 for some t ∈ ℕ 0 t\in\mathbb{N}_{0} , and some 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} . Finally, ( ψ t ) (\psi_{t}) is inadmissible if there exists a ( 𝒬 , α ) (\mathcal{Q},\alpha) -sequential test ( ψ t ′ ) (\psi^{\prime}_{t}) , such that 𝚀 ⁡ ( ψ t ′ ≥ ψ t ) = 1 \mathtt{Q}(\psi^{\prime}_{t}\geq\psi_{t})=1 for all t ∈ ℕ 0 t\in\mathbb{N}_{0} and all 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} , and 𝚀 ⁡ ( ψ t ′ > ψ t ) > 0 \mathtt{Q}(\psi^{\prime}_{t}>\psi_{t})>0 for some t ∈ ℕ 0 t\in\mathbb{N}_{0} , and some 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} . The above definition of admissibility does not specify any alternative. What allows us to do so is that the admissibility conditions are stated ‘almost surely’. To elaborate, assume there was an alternative, say 𝙿 ∗ \mathtt{P}^{*} such that there existed some time t t and an event A ∈ ℱ t A\in\mathcal{F}_{t} with 𝙿 ∗ ​ ( A ) > 0 \mathtt{P}^{*}(A)>0 but 𝚀 ⁡ ( A ) = 0 \mathtt{Q}(A)=0 for all 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} . In this case, on the event A A we would always set 𝔭 t = 0 \mathfrak{p}_{t}=0 , 𝔢 t = ∞ \mathfrak{e}_{t}=\infty , and ψ t = 1 \psi_{t}=1 . We could always do so because no 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} ‘is aware of’ the event A A , hence modifying a p-process on A A would not change any of its 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -distributional properties (like validity). For this reason, and to avoid any notational complication arising, from now on we always assume the following: If there exists some t ∈ ℕ t\in\mathbb{N} , some A ∈ ℱ t A\in\mathcal{F}_{t} , and some 𝙿 ∗ ∈ 𝒫 \mathtt{P}^{*}\in\mathcal{P} with 𝙿 ∗ ​ ( A ) > 0 \mathtt{P}^{*}(A)>0 then there exists also some 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} with 𝚀 ⁡ ( A ) > 0 \mathtt{Q}(A)>0 . This is a very mild assumption! It is, for example, satisfied if there exists some 𝚀 ∗ ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}^{*}\in\mathcal{Q} such that every 𝙿 ∈ 𝒫 \mathtt{P}\in\mathcal{P} is locally absolutely continuous with respect to 𝚀 ∗ \mathtt{Q}^{*} (see Appendix A.1 for a review). This previous condition is satisfied, for example, if all measures in 𝒫 \mathcal{P} are locally equivalent. In interesting situations a locally dominating measure 𝚀 ∗ \mathtt{Q}^{*} may not actually exist—for example when testing for symmetry, as we encounter later—but our standing assumption above is still true. Specifically, the aforementioned assumption also holds if each 𝙿 ∈ 𝒫 \mathtt{P}\in\mathcal{P} is locally absolutely continuous with respect to some specific 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} (potentially different for each 𝙿 \mathtt{P} )—this is often easier to check, and indeed holds in the symmetric example. Under the above assumption, the inadmissibility condition ( 10 ) holds with 𝚀 , 𝒬 \mathtt{Q},\mathcal{Q} swapped to 𝙿 , 𝒫 \mathtt{P},\mathcal{P} . Note that validity is subset-proof, meaning that validity for 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} implies validity for any subset of 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} . In the presence of validity, admissibility is — under a condition involving polar sets — superset-proof. The following proposition will play a key role in Section 9 . Let ( 𝔢 t ) (\mathfrak{e}_{t}) be as described in the proposition, and assume it is 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -admisible. We must show it is 𝒬 ′ \mathcal{Q}^{\prime} -admissible. Consider another 𝒬 ′ \mathcal{Q}^{\prime} -e-process ( 𝔢 t ′ ) (\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{t}) such that 𝚀 ′ ​ ( 𝔢 t ′ ≥ 𝔢 t ) = 1 \mathtt{Q}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{t}\geq\mathfrak{e}_{t})=1 for all t ∈ ℕ 0 t\in\mathbb{N}_{0} and all 𝚀 ′ ∈ 𝒬 ′ \mathtt{Q}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}^{\prime} , hence all 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} . By 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -admissibility, 𝚀 ⁡ ( 𝔢 t ′ = 𝔢 t ) = 1 \mathtt{Q}(\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{t}=\mathfrak{e}_{t})=1 for all 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} and then also for all 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 ′ \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}^{\prime} thanks to the assumption regarding the polar sets. Thus ( 𝔢 t ) (\mathfrak{e}_{t}) is 𝒬 ′ \mathcal{Q}^{\prime} -admissible. ∎ We sometimes call 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -admissibility as just admissibility if 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} is clear from context. A family of sequential tests { ( ψ t ​ ( α ) ) } α ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] \{(\psi_{t}(\alpha))\}_{\alpha\in[0,1]} is said to be ‘nested in α \alpha ’, if ψ t ​ ( α ′ ) ≥ ψ t ​ ( α ) \psi_{t}(\alpha^{\prime})\geq\psi_{t}(\alpha) for any t t and α ′ ≥ α \alpha^{\prime}\geq\alpha . This ensures that it is easier to reject the null with a larger error budget. We conclude this subsection with the following observation. The proof (Appendix B ) uses transfinite induction. We now move from testing to estimation.

<sub>§4.2 Defining inadmissibility and admissibility</sub>

### 8. ( ϕ , 𝒫 , α ) (\phi,\mathcal{P},\alpha) -confidence sequences

Let ϕ \phi be a measurable map from 𝒫 \mathcal{P} to an arbitrary set 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} . For every γ ∈ 𝒵 \gamma\in\mathcal{Z} , define and note that { 𝒫 γ } γ ∈ 𝒵 \{\mathcal{P}^{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\mathcal{Z}} is a partition of 𝒫 \mathcal{P} . Of special interest is the i.i.d. case where 𝙿 = μ ∞ \mathtt{P}=\mu^{\infty} . As examples, consider ϕ ​ ( 𝙿 ) = ϕ med ​ ( μ ) \phi(\mathtt{P})=\phi^{\rm{med}}(\mu) denoting the median of μ \mu , or ϕ ​ ( 𝙿 ) = ϕ mean ​ ( μ ) \phi(\mathtt{P})=\phi^{\rm{mean}}(\mu) denoting the mean of μ \mu . Another case of interest is where each 𝙿 ∈ 𝒫 \mathtt{P}\in\mathcal{P} can be represented as 𝙿 θ \mathtt{P}_{\theta} for some unique θ ∈ Θ \theta\in\Theta (the ‘parametric’ setting) and then ϕ ⁡ ( 𝙿 θ ) = θ \phi(\mathtt{P}_{\theta})=\theta . One may also care about fully nonparametric functionals, for example ϕ cdf \phi^{\rm{cdf}} , which maps μ \mu to its cumulative distribution function (or ϕ pdf \phi^{\rm{pdf}} if μ \mu has a Lebesgue density). For a set of distributions 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} , define ϕ ⁡ ( 𝒬 ) = ⋃ 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 ϕ ⁡ ( 𝚀 ) \phi(\mathcal{Q})=\bigcup_{\mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}}\phi(\mathtt{Q}) for short. We then define a ( 1 − α ) (1-\alpha) -confidence sequence for a functional ϕ \phi as an adapted sequence of confidence sets ( ℭ t ) (\mathfrak{C}_{t}) such that This error condition can be phrased equivalently as a coverage criterion: for every 𝙿 ∈ 𝒫 \mathtt{P}\in\mathcal{P} , we have 𝙿 ( ∀ t ∈ ℕ 0 : ϕ ( 𝙿 ) ∈ ℭ t ) ≥ 1 − α \mathtt{P}\left(\forall t\in\mathbb{N}_{0}:\phi(\mathtt{P})\in\mathfrak{C}_{t}\right)\geq 1-\alpha . Above, we have suppressed the dependence of ℭ t \mathfrak{C}_{t} on α \alpha for notational succinctness. Moreover, we emphasize that the probability 𝙿 \mathtt{P} on the outside matches with the inner functional ϕ ⁡ ( 𝙿 ) \phi(\mathtt{P}) , as it should. Indeed, the coverage probability for ϕ ⁡ ( 𝙿 ) \phi(\mathtt{P}) should hold given that the data used to construct ℭ t \mathfrak{C}_{t} was drawn according to this 𝙿 \mathtt{P} . We then say that ( ℭ t ) (\mathfrak{C}_{t}) is a ( ϕ , 𝒫 , α ) (\phi,\mathcal{P},\alpha) -CS . If required, we define ℭ ∞ := lim inf t → ∞ ℭ t \mathfrak{C}_{\infty}:=\liminf_{t\to\infty}\mathfrak{C}_{t} . Note that even though we defined ( 𝒬 , α ) (\mathcal{Q},\alpha) -sequential tests without explicit reference to any functional ϕ \phi , one could associate 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} to the subset of 𝒫 \mathcal{P} in which ϕ \phi takes on certain values. In other words, we can absorb the role of any such ϕ \phi into the definition of 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} to reduce notation. We say ( ℭ t ) (\mathfrak{C}_{t}) is inadmissible if there exists ( ℭ t ′ ) (\mathfrak{C}_{t}^{\prime}) that is a ( ϕ , 𝒫 , α ) (\phi,\mathcal{P},\alpha) -CS such that 𝙿 ⁡ ( ℭ t ′ ⊆ ℭ t ) = 1 \smash{\mathtt{P}(\mathfrak{C}_{t}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathfrak{C}_{t})=1} for all t t and all 𝙿 ∈ 𝒫 \mathtt{P}\in\mathcal{P} , and 𝙿 ⁡ ( ℭ t ′ ⊊ ℭ t ) > 0 \mathtt{P}(\mathfrak{C}_{t}^{\prime}\subsetneq\mathfrak{C}_{t})>0 for some t t and some 𝙿 ∈ 𝒫 \mathtt{P}\in\mathcal{P} . Finally, let us mention a minor technical point. A CS ( ℭ t ) (\mathfrak{C}_{t}) is by definition an adapted sequence of random sets . In this paper, we take ‘adapted’ to simply mean that all events of the form { z ∈ ℭ t } \{z\in\mathfrak{C}_{t}\} belong to ℱ t \mathcal{F}_{t} . This is sufficient for our needs, and lets us avoid dealing with σ \sigma -algebras on spaces of sets. Last, as for sequential tests, a family of CSs { ( ℭ t ​ ( α ) ) } α ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] \{(\mathfrak{C}_{t}(\alpha))\}_{\alpha\in[0,1]} is said to be ‘nested in α \alpha ’ if ℭ t ​ ( α ′ ) ⊆ ℭ t ​ ( α ) \mathfrak{C}_{t}(\alpha^{\prime})\subseteq\mathfrak{C}_{t}(\alpha) for any t t and α ′ ≥ α \alpha^{\prime}\geq\alpha .

<sub>§4.3 ( ϕ , 𝒫 , α ) (\phi,\mathcal{P},\alpha) -confidence sequences</sub>

### 9. Reductions between the four instruments

We now demonstrate how to transform one tool for sequential inference into another; some of these are well-known or ‘obvious’ but some are new, especially in the composite setting. These transformations are usually not lossless or bidirectional, meaning that something may be lost in going from one to another and back again. All proofs are relegated to Appendix B . We start with the simplest direction: forming ( 𝒬 , α ) (\mathcal{Q},\alpha) -sequential tests from the other three. Next, we show how to form a composite p-process from the other three. A confidence sequence can be formed by inverting families of tests, as follows. Finally, we can form e-processes by calibrating p-processes, following Shafer et al. [22] . We end this section with some basic closure properties. The proof can be found in Appendix B . Example 48 in Appendix C shows that the statement of Proposition 15 does not extend to confidence sequences.

<sub>§4.4 Reductions between the four instruments</sub>

### 10. Reducing admissible composite inference to the pointwise case

To build intuition towards composite admissibility, we begin with a question on validity: is there a systematic way to construct tools for valid (potentially inadmissible) inference in composite settings? ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) is a 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -p-process if and only if 𝔭 t ≥ 𝔭 t 𝚀 \mathfrak{p}_{t}\geq\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}} , 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -a.s., for all t t and 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} , where ( 𝔭 t 𝚀 ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}}) is some p-process for 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} . ( 𝔢 t ) (\mathfrak{e}_{t}) is a 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -e-process if and only if 𝔢 t ≤ 𝔢 t 𝚀 \mathfrak{e}_{t}\leq\mathfrak{e}_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}} , 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -a.s., for all t t and 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} , where ( 𝔢 t 𝚀 ) (\mathfrak{e}_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}}) is some e-process for 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} . ( ψ t ) (\psi_{t}) is a ( 𝒬 , α ) (\mathcal{Q},\alpha) -ST if and only if ψ t ≤ ψ t 𝚀 \psi_{t}\leq\psi_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}} , 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -a.s., for all t t and 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} , where ( ψ t 𝚀 ) (\psi_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}}) is some ( 𝚀 , α ) (\mathtt{Q},\alpha) -ST. If 𝔭 t = ess ​ sup 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 ⁡ 1 ∧ inf s ≤ t 1 / N s 𝚀 \mathfrak{p}_{t}=\esssup_{\mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}}1\wedge\inf_{s\leq t}1/{N^{\mathtt{Q}}_{s}} , where ( N t 𝚀 ) (N^{\mathtt{Q}}_{t}) is upper bounded by a 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -NSM, then ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) is a 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -p-process. If 𝔢 t = ess ​ inf 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 N t 𝚀 \mathfrak{e}_{t}=\mathrm{ess}\inf_{\mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}}N_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}} , where ( N t 𝚀 ) (N_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}}) is upper bounded by a 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -NSM, then ( 𝔢 t ) (\mathfrak{e}_{t}) is 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -e-process. If ψ t = ess ​ inf 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 sup s ≤ t 𝟏 N s 𝚀 ≥ 1 / α \smash{\psi_{t}=\essinf_{\mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}}\sup_{s\leq t}\mathbf{1}_{N^{\mathtt{Q}}_{s}\geq 1/\alpha}} , where ( N t 𝚀 ) (N_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}}) is upper bounded by a 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -NSM, then ( ψ t ) (\psi_{t}) is a ( 𝒬 , α ) (\mathcal{Q},\alpha) -ST. If ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) is 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -admissible, then 𝔭 t = ess ​ sup 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 ⁡ 𝔭 t 𝚀 \mathfrak{p}_{t}=\esssup_{\mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}} for all t t , where ( 𝔭 t 𝚀 ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}}) is 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -admissible. If ( 𝔢 t ) (\mathfrak{e}_{t}) is 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -admissible, then 𝔢 t = ess ​ inf 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 ⁡ 𝔢 t 𝚀 \mathfrak{e}_{t}=\essinf_{\mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathfrak{e}_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}} for all t t , where ( 𝔢 t 𝚀 ) (\mathfrak{e}_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}}) is 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -admissible. If ( ψ t ) (\psi_{t}) is 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -admissible, then ψ t = ess ​ inf 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 ⁡ ψ t 𝚀 \smash{\psi_{t}={\essinf}_{\mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}}~\psi^{\mathtt{Q}}_{t}} for all t t , where ( ψ t 𝚀 ) (\psi_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}}) is 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -admissible. If ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) is 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -admissible, then ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) is nonincreasing and 𝔭 t = ess ​ sup 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 ⁡ 𝔭 t 𝚀 \mathfrak{p}_{t}=\esssup_{\mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}} for all t t , where ( 𝔭 t 𝚀 ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}}) is a closed 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -MM. If ( 𝔢 t ) (\mathfrak{e}_{t}) is 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -admissible, then 𝔢 t = ess ​ inf 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 ⁡ M t 𝚀 \mathfrak{e}_{t}=\essinf_{\mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}}M_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}} for all t t , where ( M t 𝚀 ) (M_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}}) is a 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -NM with 𝙴 𝚀 ​ [ M 0 𝚀 ] = 1 \mathtt{E}_{\mathtt{Q}}[M_{0}^{\mathtt{Q}}]=1 . If ( ψ t ) (\psi_{t}) is 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -admissible, then ψ t = ess ​ inf 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 sup s ≤ t 𝟏 M s 𝚀 ≥ 1 / α \smash{\psi_{t}=\essinf_{\mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}}\sup_{s\leq t}\mathbf{1}_{M^{\mathtt{Q}}_{s}\geq 1/\alpha}} , where ( M t 𝚀 ) (M_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}}) is a 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -NM with 𝙴 𝚀 ​ [ M 0 𝚀 ] = 1 \mathtt{E}_{\mathtt{Q}}[M_{0}^{\mathtt{Q}}]=1 , M ∞ 𝚀 ∈ { 0 , 1 / α } M^{\mathtt{Q}}_{\infty}\in\{0,1/\alpha\} and ( M t 𝚀 ) (M^{\mathtt{Q}}_{t}) has no overshoot at level 1 / α 1/\alpha . If ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) is a 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -p-process and ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) is 𝚀 i \mathtt{Q}_{i} -admissible for each i ∈ I i\in I , then ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) is 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -admissible. If ( 𝔢 t ) (\mathfrak{e}_{t}) is a 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -e-process and ( 𝔢 t ) (\mathfrak{e}_{t}) is 𝚀 i \mathtt{Q}_{i} -admissible for each i ∈ I i\in I , then ( 𝔢 t ) (\mathfrak{e}_{t}) is 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -admissible. If ( ψ t ) (\psi_{t}) is a ( 𝒬 , α ) (\mathcal{Q},\alpha) -ST and ( ψ t ) (\psi_{t}) is 𝚀 i \mathtt{Q}_{i} -admissible for each i ∈ I i\in I , then ( ψ t ) (\psi_{t}) is 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -admissible. ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) is 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -admissible if it is a closed 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -MM and inf t ∈ ℕ 𝔭 t \inf_{t\in\mathbb{N}}\mathfrak{p}_{t} is 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -uniformly distributed for every 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} . ( 𝔢 t ) (\mathfrak{e}_{t}) is 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -admissible if it is a 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -NM with 𝙴 𝚀 ​ [ 𝔢 0 ] = 1 \mathtt{E}_{\mathtt{Q}}[\mathfrak{e}_{0}]=1 for all 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} . ( ψ t ) (\psi_{t}) is 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -admissible if ψ t = sup s ≤ t 𝟏 M s ≥ 1 / α \smash{\psi_{t}=\sup_{s\leq t}\mathbf{1}_{M_{s}\geq 1/{\alpha}}} , where ( M t ) (M_{t}) is a 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -NM with M 0 = 1 M_{0}=1 , M ∞ ∈ { 0 , 1 / α } M_{\infty}\in\{0,1/\alpha\} , 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -almost-surely, for every 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} , and no overshoot at 1 / α 1/{\alpha} . (Validity) If the process ( N t 𝙿 ) (N_{t}^{\mathtt{P}}) is upper bounded by a 𝙿 \mathtt{P} -NSM with initial expected value one, then ℭ t := ⋃ 𝙿 ∈ 𝒫 { ϕ ⁡ ( 𝙿 ) : sup s ≤ t N s 𝙿 < 1 / α } \smash{\mathfrak{C}_{t}:=\bigcup_{\mathtt{P}\in\mathcal{P}}\{\phi(\mathtt{P}):\sup_{s\leq t}N_{s}^{\mathtt{P}}<1/{\alpha}\}} , is a ( ϕ , 𝒫 , α ) (\phi,\mathcal{P},\alpha) -CS. (Admissibility) If ( M t γ ) (M^{\gamma}_{t}) is a 𝒫 γ \mathcal{P}^{\gamma} -NM with M 0 γ = 1 M^{\gamma}_{0}=1 , M ∞ γ ∈ { 0 , 1 / α } M^{\gamma}_{\infty}\in\{0,1/\alpha\} , 𝙿 \mathtt{P} -almost surely, for every 𝙿 ∈ 𝒫 γ \mathtt{P}\in\mathcal{P}^{\gamma} , and has no overshoot at 1 / α 1/\alpha , then ℭ t := { γ ∈ 𝒵 : sup s ≤ t M s γ < 1 / α = 0 } \mathfrak{C}_{t}:=\{\gamma\in\mathcal{Z}:\sup_{s\leq t}M^{\gamma}_{s}<1/\alpha=0\} is 𝒫 \mathcal{P} -admissible.

<sub>§7 Reducing admissible composite inference to the pointwise case</sub>

### 11. Necessary and sufficient conditions for valid (composite) inference

The following observations are straightforward and arguably well-known in some form or another, but are nevertheless useful to spell out formally in order to lay the path for the admissibility results. We only prove (1) , the other two assertions are argued analogously. Suppose we are given that ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) is a 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -p-process. Then, by definition, 𝔭 t \mathfrak{p}_{t} is a 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -p-process for every 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} ; so choosing ( 𝔭 t 𝚀 ) := ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}^{\mathtt{Q}}_{t}):=(\mathfrak{p}_{t}) itself, we have proved the ‘only if’ direction. For the other direction, suppose for every 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} we are given a p-process ( 𝔭 t 𝚀 ) (\mathfrak{p}^{\mathtt{Q}}_{t}) for 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} , and that ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) satisfies 𝔭 t ≥ 𝔭 t 𝚀 \mathfrak{p}_{t}\geq\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}} , 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -almost surely, for all t t . Let 𝚀 ∗ ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}^{*}\in\mathcal{Q} be some true (arbitrary) data-generating distribution. We must argue that ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) is a 𝚀 ∗ \mathtt{Q}^{*} -p-process. Indeed, for any α ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] \alpha\in[0,1] , we have where the first inequality follows because 𝔭 t ≥ 𝔭 t 𝚀 ∗ \mathfrak{p}_{t}\geq\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}^{*}} , and the second inequality follows because ( 𝔭 t 𝚀 ∗ ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}^{*}}) is a 𝚀 ∗ \mathtt{Q}^{*} -p-process by assumption. This concludes the proof. ∎ The proposition provides a generic reduction from the composite setting to the pointwise setting for performing valid inference, but we can deduce a similar result for admissible inference, presented later. While Proposition 23 forms a useful building block, it makes no mention of martingales. The notions of essential supremum and essential infimum used below are reviewed in Appendix A.2 . Note that we will need the additional restriction that 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} is locally dominated in order for these essential extrema to be well defined. This corollary follows from Proposition 23 and so its proof is omitted; see also Remark 16 .

<sub>§7.1 Necessary and sufficient conditions for valid (composite) inference</sub>

### 12. Necessary conditions for admissible composite inference

Not all constructions using martingales are admissible: we provide an analog to Proposition 23 for admissibility. Let ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) denote a 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -admissible p-process. For each 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} , let ( 𝔭 t 𝚀 ) (\mathfrak{p}^{\mathtt{Q}}_{t}) be a 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -admissible p-process that dominates ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) . Such ( 𝔭 t 𝚀 ) (\mathfrak{p}^{\mathtt{Q}}_{t}) exists thanks to Proposition 9 . Let us now define 𝔭 t ′ := ess ​ sup 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 ⁡ 𝔭 ~ t 𝚀 \mathfrak{p}_{t}^{\prime}:=\esssup_{\mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}}\widetilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{\mathtt{Q}}_{t} , which is a 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -p-process thanks to Proposition 23 (1) . Clearly, we have 𝔭 t ′ ≥ 𝔭 t \mathfrak{p}_{t}^{\prime}\geq\mathfrak{p}_{t} for all t ∈ ℕ 0 t\in\mathbb{N}_{0} . Since ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) is 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -admissible, we indeed have 𝔭 t ′ = 𝔭 t \mathfrak{p}_{t}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{p}_{t} for all t ∈ ℕ 0 t\in\mathbb{N}_{0} , yielding the assertion for p-processes. The assertions for e-processes and sequential tests are shown in the same manner. ∎ The following corollary describes the restrictions that every admissible construction necessarily satisfies. The above statements are a direct consequence of Proposition 25 and Theorem 18 .

<sub>§7.2 Necessary conditions for admissible composite inference</sub>

### 13. Sufficient conditions for admissible (composite) inference

The next proposition argues that it suffices to consider only a subset of 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} when constructing 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -admissible p-processes, e-processes, or sequential tests. Note that we do not require 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} to be locally dominated below. Let us only argue here the case of e-processes. The other cases follow in exactly the same manner. Assume that there exists an e-process ( 𝔢 t ′ ) (\mathfrak{e}_{t}^{\prime}) for 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} such that 𝚀 ⁡ ( 𝔢 t ′ ≥ 𝔢 t ) = 1 \mathtt{Q}(\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{t}\geq\mathfrak{e}_{t})=1 for all t ∈ ℕ 0 t\in\mathbb{N}_{0} and all 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} , and that 𝚀 ∗ ​ ( 𝔢 t ′ > 𝔢 t ) > 0 \mathtt{Q}^{*}(\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{t}>\mathfrak{e}_{t})>0 for some t ∈ ℕ 0 t\in\mathbb{N}_{0} , and some 𝚀 ∗ ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}^{*}\in\mathcal{Q} . By assumption, there exists some i ∈ I i\in I such that 𝚀 i ​ ( 𝔢 t ′ > 𝔢 t ) > 0 \mathtt{Q}_{i}(\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{t}>\mathfrak{e}_{t})>0 . Since ( 𝔢 t ) (\mathfrak{e}_{t}) is assumed to be 𝚀 i \mathtt{Q}_{i} -admissible, we get a contradiction. ∎ Of course, two special cases are found at the extremes: when the reference family is a singleton, it means there is a common reference measure 𝚁 \mathtt{R} , and when the reference family is 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} itself, the proposition is vacuous. The proposition is particularly useful in the first case; then to get an admissible e-process, for example, it suffices to construct a 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -NSM ( M t ) (M_{t}) that is also a 𝚀 ∗ \mathtt{Q}^{*} -NM, thanks to Proposition 27 (2) and Theorem 18 (2) . The following example demonstrates one such setting. This corollary is again a direct consequence of Proposition 27 and Theorem 18 . Recall that as in Corollary 21 , a sufficient condition for ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) to be a closed 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -MM and inf t ∈ ℕ 0 𝔭 t \inf_{t\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\mathfrak{p}_{t} be 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -uniformly distributed, for some fixed 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} , is that the p-process has the representation 𝔭 t = F 𝚀 ​ ( inf s ≤ t 1 / M s 𝚀 ) \mathfrak{p}_{t}=F^{\mathtt{Q}}(\inf_{s\leq t}1/M_{s}^{\mathtt{Q}}) , where ( M t 𝚀 ) (M_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}}) is a 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -NM and inf s ∈ ℕ 0 1 / M s \inf_{s\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}1/M_{s} has an atomless distribution function F 𝚀 F^{\mathtt{Q}} under 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} . As an immediate consequence of Corollary 29 (2) , recall the Gaussian example in Section 5.1 , and consider testing if the underlying mean is zero. Since ( G t 0 ) (G_{t}^{0}) is a 𝒢 0 \mathcal{G}^{0} -NM, it is also a 𝒢 0 \mathcal{G}^{0} -e-process, and hence a 𝒢 0 \mathcal{G}^{0} -admissible e-process when testing against, for example, 𝒫 = ⋃ m ∈ ℝ 𝒢 m \mathcal{P}=\bigcup_{m\in\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{G}^{m} .

<sub>§7.3 Sufficient conditions for admissible (composite) inference</sub>

### 14. Necessary and sufficient conditions for confidence sequences

Proposition 13 shows that we can construct a confidence sequence by inverting a family of sequential tests. We now show that their admissibility is also tightly linked to those of the underlying tests. For the first statement of the theorem, note that ℭ t = { γ ∈ 𝒵 : ψ t γ = 0 } \mathfrak{C}_{t}=\{\gamma\in\mathcal{Z}:\psi_{t}^{\gamma}=0\} yields a ( ϕ , 𝒫 , α ) (\phi,\mathcal{P},\alpha) -confidence sequence by Proposition 13 . Suppose for contradiction that ( ℭ t ′ ) (\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}_{t}) is another ( ϕ , 𝒫 , α ) (\phi,\mathcal{P},\alpha) -confidence sequence that witnesses the inadmissibility of ( ℭ t ) (\mathfrak{C}_{t}) . Proposition 11 (3) then yields a corresponding family { ( η t γ ) } γ ∈ 𝒵 \{(\eta_{t}^{\gamma})\}_{\gamma\in\mathcal{Z}} of sequential tests. The inadmissibility of ( ℭ t ) (\mathfrak{C}_{t}) then yields some γ ∈ 𝒵 \gamma\in\mathcal{Z} such that ( η t γ ) (\eta_{t}^{\gamma}) strictly dominates ( ψ t γ ) (\psi_{t}^{\gamma}) , a contradiction to the assumption that ( ψ t γ ) (\psi^{\gamma}_{t}) is admissible. The second statement follows in exactly the same way, again by an application of Propositions 13 and 11 (3) . Statements (1) and (2) are direct corollaries of combining the first part of the theorem with Corollary 24 (3) and Corollary 29 (3) . Assume now that 𝙿 γ \mathtt{P}^{\gamma} is locally dominated, for each γ \gamma , and that ( ℭ t ) (\mathfrak{C}_{t}) is 𝒫 \mathcal{P} -admissible. The statement then follows from Proposition 25 (3) and Corollary 26 (3) . ∎ This and the previous section argued in detail that restricting our attention to constructions based on NMs (not NSMs!) does not hurt us: these are universal constructions. Indeed, if one is presented with a p-process, e-process, sequential test, or confidence sequence constructed in some arbitrary fashion, we show that one can always uncover a ‘hidden’ underlying NM, such that applying Ville’s inequality or the optional stopping theorem yields an instrument that is at least as good as the original one.

<sub>§7.4 Necessary and sufficient conditions for confidence sequences</sub>

## 总结与展望
这篇证明：满足某种有效性的随时可停推断，其检验量必须来自一个非负鞅。它对我们的意义是限制性的——如果我们的配对序列不满足这个结构，那么算出来的 E 值就没有它声称的保证。

**It connects to**

- `2210.01948` — 框架的数学基石。
- `2410.23614` — 构造方法必须遵守这里的约束。

## 不足之处
**What a reader should still not conclude**

- 论文的结果是必要条件的刻画，不是充分条件；满足结构不等于有功效。
